Sublinear Algorithms and Lower Bounds for Estimating MST and TSP Cost in General Metrics
Yu Chen, Sanjeev Khanna, Zihan Tan

Abstract
We consider the design of sublinear space and query complexity algorithms for estimating the cost of a minimum spanning tree (MST) and the cost of a minimum traveling salesman (TSP) tour in a metric on n n points. We start by exploring this estimation task in the regime of o ⁡ ( n ) o(n) space, when the input is presented as a stream of all ( n 2 ) \binom{n}{2} entries of the metric in an arbitrary order (a metric stream). For any α ≥ 2 \alpha\geq 2 , we show that both MST and TSP cost can be α \alpha -approximated using O ~ ​ ( n / α ) \tilde{O}(n/\alpha) space, and moreover, Ω ⁡ ( n / α 2 ) \Omega(n/\alpha^{2}) space is necessary for this task. We further show that even if the streaming algorithm is allowed p p passes over a metric stream, it still requires Ω ~ ​ ( n / α ​ p 2 ) \tilde{\Omega}(\sqrt{n/\alpha p^{2}}) space. We next consider the well-studied semi-streaming regime. In this regime, it is straightforward to compute MST cost exactly even in the case where the input stream only contains the edges of a weighted graph that induce the underlying metric (a graph stream), and the main challenging problem is to estimate TSP cost to within a factor that is strictly better than 2 2 . We show that in graph streams, for any ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , any one-pass ( 2 − ε ) (2-\varepsilon) -approximation of TSP cost requires Ω ⁡ ( ε 2 ​ n 2 ) \Omega(\varepsilon^{2}n^{2}) space. On the other hand, we show that there is an O ~ ​ ( n ) \tilde{O}(n) space two-pass algorithm that approximates the TSP cost to within a factor of 1.96. Finally, we consider the query complexity of estimating metric TSP cost to within a factor that is strictly better than 2 2 when the algorithm is given access to an n × n n\times n matrix that specifies pairwise distances between n n points. The problem of MST cost estimation in this model is well-understood and a ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximation is achievable by O ~ ​ ( n / ε O ⁡ ( 1 ) ) \tilde{O}(n/\varepsilon^{O(1)}) queries. However, for estimating TSP cost, it is known that obtaining a ( 1 + ε ) (1+\varepsilon) -approximation requires Ω ⁡ ( n 2 ) \Omega(n^{2}) queries even for ( 1 , 2 ) (1,2) -TSP, and for general metrics, no algorithm that achieves a better than 2 2 -approximation with o ⁡ ( n 2 ) o(n^{2}) queries is known. We make progress on this task by designing an algorithm that performs O ~ ​ ( n 1.5 ) \tilde{O}(n^{1.5}) distance queries and achieves a strictly better than 2 2 -approximation when either the metric is known to contain a spanning tree supported on weight- 1 1 edges or the algorithm is given access to a minimum spanning tree of the graph. Prior to our work, such results were only known for the special cases of graphic TSP and ( 1 , 2 ) (1,2) -TSP. In terms of techniques, our algorithms for metric TSP cost estimation in both streaming and query settings rely on estimating the cover advantage which intuitively measures the cost needed to turn an MST into an Eulerian graph. One of our main algorithmic contributions is to show that this quantity can be meaningfully estimated by a sublinear number of queries in the query model. On one hand, the fact that a metric stream reveals pairwise distances for all pairs of vertices provably helps algorithmically. On the other hand, it also seems to render useless techniques for proving space lower bounds via reductions from well-known hard communication problems. Our main technical contribution in lower bounds is to identify and characterize the communication complexity of new problems that can serve as canonical starting point for proving metric stream lower bounds.



1
Introduction

The minimum spanning tree (MST) problem and the metric traveling salesman (TSP) problem are among the most well-studied combinatorial optimization problems with a long and rich history (see, e.g.,
[
Chr76
,
KLS15
,
CQ17
,
CQ18
,
Gao18
,
MM18
,
KKG21
]
​).
The two problems are intimately connected to one another, as many approximation algorithms for metric TSP use a minimum spanning tree as a starting point for efficiently constructing an approximate solution. In particular, any algorithm for estimating the MST cost to within a factor of
\alpha
immediately implies an algorithm for estimating the metric TSP cost to within a factor of
2\alpha
.
In this work, we consider the design of sublinear space and query complexity algorithms for estimating the cost of a minimum spanning tree (MST) and the cost of a minimum metric traveling salesman (TSP) tour in an
n
-vertex weighted undirected graph
G
. An equivalent view of both problems is that we are given an
n\times n
matrix
w
specifying pairwise distances between them, where the entry
w[u,v]
corresponds to the weight of the shortest path from
u
to
v
in
G
. It is clear that any algorithm that works with a weighted graph as input also works when the input is presented as the complete metric. However, the converse is not true. For instance, no single-pass streaming algorithm can obtain a finite approximation to the diameter (or even determine the connectivity) of a graph in
o(n)
space when the graph is presented as a sequence of edges (a
graph stream
). But if instead we are presented a stream of
n^{2}
entries of the metric matrix
w
(a
metric stream
), there is a trivial
\tilde{O}(1)
space algorithm for this problem – simply track the largest entry seen.

1.1
Our Results

In the first part of this work, we explore the power and limitations of graph and metric streams for MST and TSP cost estimation. We start by exploring this estimation task in the regime of
o(n)
space in the streaming model. It is easy to show that no finite approximation to MST/TSP cost is achievable in this regime when the input stream simply contains the edges of a weighted graph that induce the underlying metric (a graph stream). However, we show that this state of affairs changes completely if the input is instead presented as all entries of the shortest-path-distance metric induced by the input graph (a metric stream).

Theorem 1
.

For any
\alpha>1
, there is a randomized one-pass
\alpha
-approximation streaming algorithm for MST cost estimation in metric streams using
\tilde{O}(n/\alpha)
space.

Note that this also immediately gives a one-pass
\tilde{O}(n/\alpha)
-space algorithm for TSP cost estimation for any
\alpha\geq 2
by simply doubling the MST cost estimate. The result above is in sharp contrast to what is achievable in graph streams. Using a simple reduction from the
\mathsf{Disjointness}
problem, we can show the following lower bound for graph streams.

Theorem 2
.

For any
\alpha>1
, any randomized
p
-pass
\alpha
-approximation streaming algorithm for MST cost estimation in graph streams requires
\tilde{\Omega}(n/p)
space.

We next show that there are limits to the power of metric streams, and in particular, any non-trivial approximation of MST cost still requires polynomial space even if we allow multiple passes over the stream.

Theorem 3
.

For any
\alpha>1
, any randomized one-pass
\alpha
-approximation streaming algorithm for MST cost estimation in metric streams requires
\Omega(n/\alpha^{2})
space.

Theorem 4
.

For any
\alpha>1
, any randomized
p
-pass
\alpha
-approximation streaming algorithm for MST cost estimation requires
\tilde{\Omega}(\sqrt{n/\alpha p^{2}})
space.

Summary of results for MST-cost estimation streaming algorithms.

We next consider the well-studied semi-streaming regime when the streaming algorithm is allowed to use
\tilde{O}(n)
space. In this regime, it is straightforward to design a deterministic one-pass streaming algorithm to compute MST cost exactly even in graph streams, and this in turn, immediately gives an
\tilde{O}(n)
space algorithm to estimate TSP cost to within a factor of
2
. Thus in the semi-streaming regime, the key challenging problem is to estimate TSP cost to within a factor that is strictly better than
2
.
A special case of this problem,
graphic TSP cost
estimation, where the input metric corresponds to the shortest-path distances induced by an unweighted undirected graph, was recently studied in
[
CKK20
]
, and the authors gave an
\tilde{O}(n)
space randomized one-pass streaming algorithm that achieves an
(11/6)
-approximation even in the setting of graph streams.
This ratio was recently improved
1
1

1

In their paper, they give an
\tilde{O}(n)
-time
1.83
-approximation algorithm, which can be easily turned into a one-pass streaming algorithm with space
\tilde{O}(n)
with the same approximation ratio.
by Behnezhad, Roghani, Rubinstein, and Saberi to
1.83

[
BRRS23
]
.
However, no analogous result is known for general TSP. We show that there is in fact a good reason for this state of affairs:

Theorem 5
.

For any
0<\epsilon<1
, any randomized one-pass
(2\!-\!\varepsilon)
-approximation streaming algorithm for TSP cost estimation in graph streams requires
\Omega(\epsilon^{2}n^{2})
space.

However, we show that the situation changes considerably once we allow two passes and indeed there is now a deterministic
\tilde{O}(n)
space algorithm that achieves better than a
2
-approximation to TSP cost.

Theorem 6
.

There is a deterministic two-pass
1.96
-approximation algorithm for TSP cost estimation in graph streams using
\tilde{O}(n)
space.

We note that an interesting remaining question here is if a similar result is achievable using one pass when the input is a metric stream. As a step towards understanding the power of metric streams in semi-streaming regime, we show that any one-pass algorithm that computes TSP cost exactly requires
\Omega(n^{2})
space.

The second part of our paper focuses on the design of sublinear query complexity algorithms for TSP cost estimation. The related problem of estimating the MST cost using sublinear queries was first studied in the graph adjacency-list model by Chazelle, Rubinfeld, and Trevisan
[
CRT05
]
. The authors gave an
\tilde{O}(dW/\varepsilon^{2})
-time algorithm to estimate the MST cost to within a factor of
(1+\varepsilon)
in a graph where the average degree is
d
, and all edge costs are integers in
\left\{1,\ldots,W\right\}
. For certain parameter regimes this gives a sublinear time algorithm for estimating the MST cost, but in general, this run-time need not be sublinear. In fact, it is not difficult to show that in general, even checking if a graph is connected requires
\Omega(n^{2})
queries in the graph adjacency-list model, and hence no finite approximation to MST cost can be achieved in
o(n^{2})
queries. However, the situation changes if one restricts attention to the
metric
MST problem where the edge weights satisfy the triangle inequality, and the algorithm is given access to an
n\times n
matrix
w
specifying pairwise distances between vertices. Czumaj and Sohler
[
CS09
]
showed that for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an
\tilde{O}(n/\varepsilon^{O(1)})
query algorithm that returns a
(1+\varepsilon)
-approximate estimate of the metric MST cost. This result immediately implies an
\tilde{O}(n/\varepsilon^{O(1)})
time algorithm to estimate the TSP cost to within a factor of
(2+\varepsilon)
for any
\varepsilon>0
. In sharp contrast to this result, so far no
o(n^{2})
query algorithms are known to approximate metric TSP cost to a factor that is strictly better than
2
. In this work, we consider sublinear query algorithms for TSP cost when the algorithm is given query access to the
n\times n
distance matrix
w
.
We will assume throughout the paper that all entries of
w
are positive integers.

For the special case of graphic TSP, where the metric corresponds to shortest path distances of some underlying connected unweighted graph, the algorithm of Chen, Kannan, and Khanna
[
CKK20
]
combined with the recent result of Behnezhad
[
Beh21
]
(which builds on the work of Yoshida et al.
[
YYI12
]
and Onak et al.
[
ORRR12
]
),
gives an
\tilde{O}(n)
-query
(27/14)
-approximation algorithm for estimating graphic TSP cost. The authors in
[
CKK20
]
also show that there exists an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, such that any algorithm that estimates the cost of graphic TSP (or even
(1,2)
-TSP) to within a
(1+\varepsilon_{0})
-factor, necessarily requires
\Omega(n^{2})
queries.
Later on, Behnezhad, Roghani, Rubinstein, and Saberi
[
BRRS23
]
improved the graphic TSP result by giving an
\tilde{O}(n)
-query
1.83
-approximation, and they also gave an
\tilde{O}(n)
-query
(1.5+\varepsilon)
-approximation algorithm for
(1,2)
-TSP.
This leaves open the following question: Is there an
o(n^{2})
query algorithm to estimate TSP cost to a factor strictly better than
2
when the metric is
arbitrary
?

We make progress on this question by designing an
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
-query algorithm that achieves a strictly better than
2
-approximation when either the metric is known to contain a spanning tree supported on weight-
1
edges or the algorithm is given access to a minimum spanning tree of the graph.
Prior to our work, such results were only known for the special cases of graphic TSP and
(1,2)
-TSP.

Theorem 7
.

There is a randomized algorithm, that, given access to an
n
-point metric
w
with the promise that
w
contains a minimum spanning tree supported only on weight-
1
edges, estimates with high probability the metric TSP cost to within a factor of
(2-\varepsilon_{0})
for some universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, by performing
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries to
w
.

We note that the setting of Theorem
7
captures as a special case graphic TSP but is considerably more general, and hence difficult.

Theorem 8
.

There is a randomized algorithm, that, given access to an
n
-point metric
w
and an arbitrary minimum spanning tree of the complete graph with edge weights given by
w
, estimates with high probability the metric TSP cost to within a factor of
(2-\varepsilon_{0})
for some universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, by performing
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries to
w
.

In what follows, we give an overview of the techniques underlying our results.

1.2
Technical Overview

1.2.1
Overview of Algorithmic Techniques

Our streaming algorithm for MST estimation (
Theorem

1
) utilizes a rather natural idea. We sample
O(n/\alpha)
vertices and maintain a MST
T^{\prime}
over them. For the remaining vertices, we maintain an estimate of the cost of connecting them to the nearest vertex in
T^{\prime}
. We show that these estimates can be suitably combined to obtain an
\alpha
-approximation of MST cost.
In this subsection we focus on providing a high-level overview of the algorithms for TSP estimation.

It is well-known that
{\mathsf{MST}}\leq\mathsf{TSP}\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
holds for any graph/metric, since we can construct a TSP-tour by doubling all edges of a MST (and then shortcut the obtained walk into a tour).
Since the MST cost of a graph/metric can be exactly computed by a one-pass
\tilde{O}(n)
space algorithm (the greedy algorithm) in the streaming model, and can be approximated to within a factor of
(1+\varepsilon)
by performing
\tilde{O}(n)
queries in the query model
[
CS09
]
, to obtain a factor
(2-\varepsilon)
approximation for
\mathsf{TSP}
, it suffices to establish either
\mathsf{TSP}\geq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
or
\mathsf{TSP}\leq(2-\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
holds.
From the approach due to
[
Chr76
]
, the minimum weight of a perfect matching on the set of all odd-degree vertices in an MST can immediately gives us the answer. However, obtaining a good approximation to the minimum weight of such a perfect matching appears hard to do, both for semi-streaming algorithms and for a query algorithm that performs
o(n^{2})
queries, even if we are given an MST at the start. To get around this issue, we consider an alternative measure, called the
cover advantage
, that turns out to be more tractable in both models.

Cover Advantage.

Let
T
be a MST of the input graph/metric. For an edge
f\in E(T)
and an edge
e\notin E(T)
, we say that
f
is
covered
by
e
, iff
f
belongs to the unique tree-path in
T
connecting the endpoints of
e
.
For a set
E^{\prime}
of edges, we denote by
{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime},T)
the set of all edges in
E(T)
that are covered by at least one edge in
E^{\prime}
. The
cover advantage
of
E^{\prime}
, denoted by
\mathsf{adv}(E^{\prime})
, is defined to be the total weight of all edges in
{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime},T)
minus the total weight of all edges in
E^{\prime}
.
Intuitively, if a single-edge set
\left\{e\right\}
where
e=(u,v)
has cover advantage
c
, then we can construct a tour by starting from some Euler-tour of
T
and replacing the segment corresponding to the tree path of
T
connecting
u
to
v
by the single edge
e
, and thereby “saving a cost of
c
” from
2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, the cost of the Euler-tour obtained by doubling MST edges.
Generalizing this idea, we show that if there exists a set
E^{\prime}
with cover advantage bounded away from
0
(at least
\varepsilon\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
), then
\mathsf{TSP}\leq(2-\varepsilon/2)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. Conversely, if there does not exist any set
E^{\prime}
with cover advantage close to
{\mathsf{MST}}/2
(say at least
(1/2-\varepsilon/2)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
), then
\mathsf{TSP}\geq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.
In fact, we show that the same hold for a more restricted notion called
special cover advantage
, which is defined to be the maximum cover advantage of any subset
E^{\prime}
of edges that have at least one endpoint being a special vertex in
T
(a vertex
v
is called a
special vertex
of
T
iff
\deg_{T}(v)\neq 2
).
Therefore, to obtain a better-than-factor-2 approximation for
\mathsf{TSP}
, it suffices to obtain a constant-factor approximation for the maximum cover advantage or the maximum special cover advantage.

Estimating maximum cover advantage in the streaming setting.

We construct a one-pass streaming algorithm
O(1)
-estimating the maximum cover advantage, which leads to a two-pass algorithm in
Theorem

6
where in the first pass we only compute an MST of the input graph. We store edges with substantial cover advantage with respect to the MST in a greedy manner. Since all edges appear in the stream, it can be shown that, if we end up not discovering a large cover advantage, then the real maximum cover advantage is indeed small (bounded away from
{\mathsf{MST}}/2
).

Estimating maximum cover advantage in the query model.

The task of obtaining a constant-factor approximation to maximum cover advantage turns out to be distinctly more challenging in the query model, even if we are given explicit access to an MST of the metric. The design of sublinear query algorithms for estimating cover advantage is indeed our central algorithmic contribution.
We design an
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
-query algorithm for estimating the maximum cover advantage when either an MST is explicitly given or we can assume that the MST is supported on weight-
1
edges. Note that the latter case generalizes graphic TSP studied in
[
CKK20
]
.

The algorithms for these two cases share several similarities. To illustrate the ideas behind them, it might be instructive to consider the following two examples. In the first example, we are given an MST
T
on
V
that has at most
O(\sqrt{n})
leaves.
We can simply query the distances between all pairs
u,v\in V
where
u
is a special vertex of
T
, and then use the obtained information to compute the maximum special cover advantage, which takes
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries since there can be at most
O(\sqrt{n})
special vertices (or in fact we can even query the distances between all pairs of special vertices in
T
and compute the minimum weight perfect matching on them).
In the second example, we are given an MST
T
on
V
which is a star graph centered at a vertex
r\in V
, and all edges have weight
1
. Note that, since all edge weights are integers, in this case the distances between every pair of vertices in
V\setminus\left\{r\right\}
is either
1
or
2
, and it is not hard to see that the maximum cover advantage is exactly the size of a maximum weight-
1
matching on
V\setminus\left\{r\right\}
.
Therefore, we can adapt the algorithm from
[
Beh21
]
to obtain an
O(1)
-approximation of the maximum weight-
1
matching size, using
\tilde{O}(n)
queries.
Note that, in this case we obtain an estimate of the maximum cover advantage without computing a set of edges that achieves it.

Taking a step back, we observe that, in the first example where the number of special vertices is small, the cover advantage can be computed in a local and exhaustive manner, while in the second example where the number of special vertices is large, the cover advantage has to be estimated in a global and “superficial” manner. Intuitively, our query algorithms interpolate between these two approaches in an organic manner.

We now provide more details of our query algorithms in the two special cases. We first consider the special case where we are given the structure of an MST.

When MST is given:

We root the given MST
T
at an arbitrary vertex. For each vertex
v\in V
, we say that it is
light
iff the subtree of
T
rooted at
v
, denoted by
T_{v}
, contains at most
\sqrt{n}
vertices, and we call
T_{v}
a
light subtree
of
T
. On the one hand, the cover advantages that are local at some light subtree (achieved by edges with both endpoints in the same light subtree) can be efficiently estimated in an exhaustive manner. On the other hand, if we peel off all light subtrees from
T
, then the remaining subtree, that we denote by
T^{\prime}
, contains at most
\sqrt{n}
leaves, and therefore the special cover advantage achieved by any set of edges with at least one endpoint being a special vertex of
T^{\prime}
can also be computed in an exhaustive manner. The only type of cover advantages that is not yet computed are the one achieved by edges with endpoints in different light subtrees. We then observe that the light subtrees hanged at
T
are similar to the edges of a star graph hanged at its root, and eventually manage to adapt the algorithm from
[
Beh21
]
in a delicate way to estimate the cover advantage by edges of this type in a global manner.

When MST consists of only weight-
1
edges:

This special case appears trickier since we do not know the structure of an MST at the start, and there may not even be a unique MST.
To circumvent this, we need to utilize the following technical result of
[
CKK20
]
:
Let
G_{1}
be the graph on
V
induced by all weight-
1
edges in the given metric, then if
G_{1}
contains a size-
s
matching consisting of only edges in
2
-edge-connected components of
G_{1}
, then
\mathsf{TSP}\leq 2n-\Omega(s)
.
This result allows us to construct a local procedure that explores some neighborhood of the unknown graph
G_{1}
up to a certain size, such that in the end we either reconstruct a size-
\sqrt{n}
subgraph of the (locally) unique MST, or certify that a set of
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
vertices belong to some
2
-edge-connected components of
G_{1}
,
which will be later collected to estimate the maximum weight-
1
matching size.

We then use this local procedure on a set of vertices randomly sampled from
V
. Let
T
be an MST. Intuitively, if the total size of light subtrees of
T
is non-negligible, then with high probability some of the sampled vertices will lie in light subtrees of
T
, and we can obtain an estimate of the local cover advantage within subtrees. If the total size of light subtrees is negligible, then
T^{\prime}
, the subtree obtained from
T
by peeling off all light subtrees, has roughly the same size as
T
, which means that
T
is close to the first instructive example mentioned before – a tree with only
O(\sqrt{n})
special vertices. Then we can apply the local procedure to
\Omega(\sqrt{n})
sampled vertices, to almost reconstruct the whole tree
T
, and the rest of the algorithm is similar to the algorithm in the first special case.

1.2.2
Overview of Lower Bound Techniques

As our algorithmic results illustrate that metric streams are more powerful than graph streams, it is perhaps not surprising that proving space lower bounds for metrics streams turns out to be a more challenging task that requires new tools.
To illustrate this point, it might be instructive to consider the following simplified versions of metric and graph streams. Let
G
be a graph.

•

Unweighted Graph Stream: a sequence that contains all edges of
E(G)
, and the same edge may appear more than once in the stream;

•

Unweighted Metric Stream: a sequence that contains, for each pair
u,v
of
V(G)
, a symbol
f(u,v)
indicating whether or not the edge
(u,v)
belongs to
E(G)
.

Note that in unweighted metric streams, the non-edge information between pairs of vertices is also explicitly given (as the edge information), as opposed to being given implicitly in the unweighted graph stream. This seemingly unimportant distinction, unexpectedly, makes proving lower bounds for several problems much harder in unweighted metric streams than unweighted graph streams.

For example, consider the problem of deciding whether the input graph is a clique.
On the one hand, to prove a space lower bound for streaming algorithms in unweighted graph streams, we consider the following two-player one-way communication game: Alice is given a graph
G_{A}
and Bob is given a graph
G_{B}
on a common vertex set
V
, and Alice and Bob want to decide if
G_{A}\cup G_{B}
is the complete graph on
V
. It is easy to show that this communication game has back-and-forth communication complexity
\Omega(n^{2})
. In fact, Alice’s input graph
G_{A}
can be viewed as a vector
x^{A}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\binom{V}{2}}
and Bob’s input graph
G_{B}
can be viewed as a vector
x^{B}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\binom{V}{2}}
, where the coordinate
x^{A}_{(u,u^{\prime})}
indexed by the pair
u,u^{\prime}
of vertices in
V
indicates whether or not the edge
(u,u^{\prime})
appears in graph
G_{A}
, and similarly
x^{B}_{(u,u^{\prime})}
indicates whether or not the edge
(u,u^{\prime})
appears graph
G_{B}
. It is then easy to see that the two players need to detect whether or not the bitwise-OR of vectors
x^{A}
and
x^{B}
is the all-one vector, which requires
\Omega(n^{2})
-bits information exchange even in the back-and-forth communication model.
On the other hand, in the corresponding two-player one-way communication game for unweighted metric streams, Alice and Bob are each given a set of edge/non-edge information, with the promise that the edge/non-edge information between each pair of vertices appears in at least one of the player’s input. There is a one-bit protocol: Alice simply sends to Bob a signal indicating whether or not in her input there is non-edge information between any pair of vertices, and Bob outputs “Not a Clique” iff either he sees Alice’s “non-edge” signal or he sees a non-edge information in his input.

The distinction that all non-edge information is explicitly given in the unweighted stream seems to fail all reductions from standard problems (like Disjointness and Index) to prove lower bounds. Therefore, in the lower bound proofs of
Theorem

3
and
Theorem

4
, we identify new “primitive” graph-theoretic problems, prove communication lower bounds for them, and then reduce them to MST-estimation problems. Here we briefly provide some ideas for the proof of
Theorem

4
.

We consider the special type of metrics, in which the distance between every pair of vertices is either
1
or a large enough real number. Intuitively, the problem of estimating the MST cost is equivalent to the problem of estimating the number of connected components of the graph induced by all weight-
1
edges, which is essentially a graph-theoretic problem in unweighted metric streams.

As a first step, we consider the following problem: given an unweighted metric stream, decide whether the underlying graph is a perfect matching or a perfect matching minus one edge.
Unlike the previous clique-identification problem, we show that the corresponding two-player communication game for this problem has communication complexity
\Omega(n)
in the back and forth communication model, even if the complete edge/non-edge information is split between Alice and Bob. The proof is by analyzing the information complexity of any protocol for the problem,
We construct several similar input combinations for Alice and Bob, among which some cross-combination lead to different answers, and then lower bound the mutual information between the protocol transcript and the players’ inputs.

However, this perfect matching vs perfect matching minus one edge problem is not sufficient for our purpose, since a perfect matching graph on
n
vertices has
n/2
connected components, while a perfect matching minus one edge graph on
n
vertices has
n/2-1
connected components, and the ratio between
n/2
and
n/2-1
are too small to provide a space lower bound for
\alpha
-approximation of the number of connected components.
To fix this issue, we next consider a generalization of this problem, called the
Clique or Independent Set
problem (
{\mathsf{COI}}_{a,b}
) parametrized by two integers
a,b
. In this problem, we are required to decide whether the input graph is the disjoint union of
b
cliques of size
a
each (Yes case) or it is a disjoint union of
(b-1)
cliques of size
a
each and an independent set of size
a
(No case). Note that if
a=2
and
b=n/2
then this problem is exactly the perfect matching vs perfect matching minus one edge problem. Now if we let
a\gg b
, then the ratio between numbers of connected components in Yes case and in No case is
(a+b-1)/b=\Omega(a/b)
, which is enough for giving a space lower bound for
o(a/b)
-approximation streaming algorithms for MST estimation.
For the proof of the communication lower bound of problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{a,b}
, we first consider the special case
{\mathsf{COI}}_{a,2}
and show that the communication complexity is
\tilde{\Omega}(1)
via a Hellinger distance analysis on transcript distributions on certain input combinations, and then use a direct sum type argument to show that the communication complexity of
{\mathsf{COI}}_{a,b}
is
\tilde{\Omega}(b)
.
Both steps use techniques similar to the ones used in the proof of communication lower bound for the perfect matching vs perfect matching minus one edge problem.
Now for a given approximation ratio
\alpha>1
, setting
a=\Theta(\sqrt{\alpha n})
and
b=\Theta(\sqrt{n/\alpha})
yields the desired communication lower bound, which then implies the space lower bounds for streaming algorithms.

1.3
Organization

We first introduce the notion of cover advantage in
Section

2
. The remainder of the paper is divided into three parts. In the first part, we present our results on streaming algorithms and lower bounds for estimating MST and TSP cost (the proofs of
Theorem

1
to
6
).
In the second part, we present the algorithm in the query model where we assume that MST is supported on weight-
1
edges (the proof of
Theorem

7
).
In the third part, we present the algorithm in the query model where we are additionally given access to an MST (the proof of
Theorem

8
).

2
Cover Advantage

In this section, we introduce the notion of cover advantage, which is a key notion that
captures the gap between the MST cost and the TSP cost. Our TSP cost estimation algorithms in both streaming and query settings will crucially utilize this notion.

At a high-level, the TSP estimation algorithms in this paper are based on converting an MST
T
of the input graph/metric into a spanning Eulerian subgraph. A trivial approach is to simply double all edges in
T
obtaining a
2
-approximation. A more clever approach due to Christofides
[
Chr76
]
instead makes
T
Eulerian by adding a minimum weight perfect matching on odd-degree vertices in
T
, obtaining a
3/2
-appproximation. However, computing a good approximation to the minimum weight perfect matching on a set of vertices appears hard to do either in the semi-streaming setting or with sublinear number of queries. We instead identify the more tractable notion, called the cover advantage, that can be efficiently implemented in the semi-streaming and query model.

We say that an edge
f
of tree
T
is
covered
by an edge
e
that may or may not belong to
T
, iff
f\in E(P^{T}_{e})
; and we say that
f
is covered by a set
E^{\prime}
of edges, iff it is covered by some edge of
E^{\prime}
. We denote by
{\mathsf{cov}}(e)
the set of all edges of
T
that are covered by
e
, and define
{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime})=\bigcup_{e\in E^{\prime}}{\mathsf{cov}}(e)
.

Let
T^{\prime}
be a subtree of
T
. For each edge
e\notin E(T)
, we define
{\mathsf{cov}}(e,T^{\prime})={\mathsf{cov}}(e)\cap E(T^{\prime})
. Similarly, for a set
E^{\prime}
of edges, we define
{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime},T^{\prime})=\bigcup_{e\in E^{\prime}}{\mathsf{cov}}(e,T^{\prime})
.
Clearly,
{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime},T^{\prime})={\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime})\cap E(T^{\prime})
.

We define the
cover advantage
of a set
E^{\prime}
of edges on a subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
, denoted by
\mathsf{adv}(E^{\prime},T^{\prime})
, to be
\mathsf{adv}(E^{\prime},T^{\prime})=w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime},T^{\prime}))-w(E^{\prime})
.
The
optimal cover advantage
of a subtree
T^{\prime}
, denoted by
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})
, is defined to be the maximum cover advantage of any set
E^{\prime}
of edges that have at least one endpoint lying in
V(T^{\prime})
on
T^{\prime}
.
The
optimal special cover advantage
of a subtree
T^{\prime}
, denoted by
\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})
, is defined to be the maximum cover advantage of any set
E^{\prime}
of edges that have at least one endpoint being a special vertex of
T^{\prime}
(a vertex
v
is a special vertex of
T^{\prime}
iff
\deg_{T^{\prime}}(v)\neq 2
).
Clearly, by definition,
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})\geq\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})\geq 0
.

The next two lemmas show that the optimal cover advantage and the optimal special cover advantage of any subtree can be computed using a small number of queries.

Lemma 2.1
.

There is an algorithm, that given a subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
, computes the optimal cover advantage of
T^{\prime}
as well as a set
E^{\prime}
of edges achieving the optimal cover advantage of
T^{\prime}
, by performing at most
O(n\cdot|V(T^{\prime})|)
queries.

Proof.

We first query the distances between all pairs
(u,u^{\prime})
of vertices with
u\in V(T^{\prime})
and
u^{\prime}\in V
. We then enumerate all eligible edge sets (sets of edges with at least one endpoint in
V(T^{\prime})
) and compute the cover advantage of each of them on
T^{\prime}
, from the distance information we acquired. Finally, we return the eligible set
E^{\prime}
with the maximum cover advantage on
T^{\prime}
, and the cover advantage on
T^{\prime}
it achieves. Clearly, we have performed
O(n\cdot|V(T^{\prime})|)
queries.
∎

Similarly, we can prove the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2
.

There is an algorithm, that given a subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
, computes the optimal special cover advantage of
T^{\prime}
as well as a set
E^{\prime}
of edges achieving the optimal special cover advantage of
T^{\prime}
, by performing
O(n\cdot k_{T^{\prime}})
queries, where
k_{T^{\prime}}
is the number of special vertices in
T^{\prime}
.

We now show that the cover advantage of edge-disjoint subtrees of an MST translates into a TSP tour whose cost is much better than
2
times the MST cost.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
T
be an MST on a set
V
of vertices, and
let
{\mathcal{T}}
be a set of edge-disjoint subtrees of
T
. Then
\mathsf{TSP}\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We introduce some definitions before providing the proof.

Let
E^{\prime}
be a set of edges that do not belong to
E(T)
. We define the multi-graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
as follows. Its vertex set is
V(H_{T,E^{\prime}})=V
. Its edge set is the union of (i) the set
E^{\prime}
; and (ii) the set
E_{[T,E^{\prime}]}
that contains, for each edge
f\in E(T)
,
2
copies of
f
iff
f
is covered by an even number of edges in
E^{\prime}
,
1
copy of
f
iff
f
is covered by an odd number of edges in
E^{\prime}
.
Equivalently, graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
can be obtained from the following iterative algorithm. Throughout, we maintain a graph
\hat{H}
on the vertex set
V
, that initially contains two copies of each edge of
E(T)
. We will maintain the invariant that, over the course of the algorithm, for each edge
f
of
E(T)
, graph
\hat{H}
contains either one copy or two copies of
f
.
We then process edges of
E^{\prime}
one-by-one (in arbitrary order) as follows. Consider now an edge
e\in E^{\prime}
and the tree-path
P^{T}_{e}
. We add one copy of edge
e
to
\hat{H}
. Then for each edge
f\in E(P^{T}_{e})
, if currently the graph
\hat{H}
contains
2
copies of
f
, then we remove one copy of it from
\hat{H}
; if currently the graph
\hat{H}
contains
1
copy of
f
, then we add one copy of it into
\hat{H}
. Clearly after each iteration of processing some edge of
E^{\prime}
, the invariant still holds. It is also easy to see that the resulting graph we obtain after processing all edges of
E^{\prime}
is exactly the graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
defined above.

We prove the following observation.

Observation 2.1
.

For any set
E^{\prime}
, graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
is Eulerian.

Proof.

Consider the algorithm that produces the graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
. Initially, graph
\hat{H}
contains
2
copies of each edge of
T
, and is therefore Eulerian. It is easy to see that, in the iteration of processing the edge
e\in E^{\prime}
, we only modify the degrees of vertices in the cycle
e\cup P^{T}_{e}
. Specifically, for each vertex in the cycle
e\cup P^{T}_{e}
, either its degree is increased by
2
(if a copy is added to both of its incident edges in the cycle), or its degree is decreased by
2
(if a copy is removed from both of its incident edges in the cycle), or its degree remains unchanged (if a copy is removed from one of its incident edges, and a copy is added to the other incident edge). Therefore, the graph
\hat{H}
remains Eulerian after this iteration, and it follows that the resulting graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
is Eulerian.
∎

We now provide the proof of
Lemma

2.3
.
Denote
{\mathcal{T}}=\left\{T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}\right\}
.
For each index
1\leq i\leq k
, let
E^{*}_{i}
be the set of edges that achieves the maximum cover advantage on
T_{i}
. Denote
E^{*}=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}E^{*}_{i}
, and then we let
E^{\prime}
be the random subset of
E^{*}
that includes each edge of
E^{*}
independently with probability
1/2
.
We will show that the expected total weight of all edges in
E(H_{T,E^{\prime}})
is at most
2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(G)-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathsf{adv}(T_{i})
. Note that this implies that there exists a subset
E^{**}
of
E^{*}
, such that the weight of graph
H_{T,E^{**}}
is at most
2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(G)-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathsf{adv}(T_{i})
. Combined with
2.1
and
Lemma

1.1
, this implies
\mathsf{TSP}\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(G)-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathsf{adv}(T_{i})
, completing the proof of
Lemma

2.3
.

We now show that
\mathbb{E}[w(H_{T,E^{\prime}})]\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(G)-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathsf{adv}(T_{i})
. From the definition of graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
,
E(H_{T,E^{\prime}})=E^{\prime}\cup E_{[T,E^{\prime}]}
. On one hand, from the construction of set
E^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{E}[w(E^{\prime})]=w(E^{*})/2
.
On the other hand, for each edge
f\in{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*})
, with probability
1/2
graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
contains
1
copy of it, and with probability
1/2
graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
contains
2
copies of it. Therefore,
\mathbb{E}[w(E_{[T,E^{\prime}]})]=2\cdot w(E(T))-w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))/2
.
Note that subtrees
\left\{T_{i}\right\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
are edge-disjoint, so the edge sets
\left\{{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*},T_{i})\right\}_{1\leq i\leq k}
are mutually disjoint.
Altogether,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[w(H_{T,E^{\prime}})]&=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\frac{w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))-w(E^{*})}{2}\\
&\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\bigg(w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*},T_{i}))-w(E^{*}_{i})\bigg)\\
&\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\bigg(w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}_{i},T_{i}))-w(E^{*}_{i})\bigg)\\
&=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathsf{adv}(T_{i}).\end{split}

∎

We show in the next lemma that, although the special cover advantage of any tree is upper bounded by its cover advantage by definition, but it is greater than the cover advantage of any single edge.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
T^{\prime}
be a subtree of
T
. Let
e=(u,v)
be an edge with
u\in V(T^{\prime})
and
v\notin V(T^{\prime})
, then
\mathsf{adv}(e,T^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})
.

Proof.

If
u
is a special vertex of
T^{\prime}
, then the lemma follows immediately from the definition of
\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})
. Assume now that
u
is not a special vertex of
T^{\prime}
, so
\deg_{T^{\prime}}(u)=2
.
Since
u\in V(T^{\prime})
and
v\notin V(T^{\prime})
,
{\mathsf{cov}}(e,T^{\prime})
is a path
P
of
T^{\prime}
with
u
being one of its endpoint. Clearly. we can find another leaf
u^{\prime}
of
T^{\prime}
such that the tree-path
P^{T^{\prime}}_{u,u^{\prime}}
is edge-disjoint from
P
. Consider now the edge
e^{\prime}=(u^{\prime},v)
. On the one hand, since
u^{\prime}
is a special vertex of
T^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{adv}(e^{\prime},T^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})
. On the other hand, clearly
{\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime},T^{\prime})=P^{T^{\prime}}_{u,u^{\prime}}\cup P
, and from triangle inequality,
w(u^{\prime},v)\leq w(u^{\prime},u)+w(u,v)\leq w(P^{T^{\prime}}_{u,u^{\prime}})+w(u,v)
. Therefore,
\mathsf{adv}(e^{\prime},T^{\prime})\geq\mathsf{adv}(e,T^{\prime})
. Altogether, we get that
\mathsf{adv}(e,T^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})
.
∎

We show in the next technical lemma that the edge set of any TSP tour can be partitioned into two subsets that both cover the whole tree.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
T^{\prime}
be a subtree of
T
and let
\pi
be a tour that traverses all vertices of
T^{\prime}
. Then
E(\pi)
can be partitioned into two subsets
E(\pi)=E_{0}\cup E_{1}
, such that
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{0})
and
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{1})
.

Proof.

Let
V^{\prime}
be the set of all odd-degree vertices in
T^{\prime}
, so
|V^{\prime}|
is even. Denote
V^{\prime}=\left\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{2k}\right\}
, where the vertices are index according to the order in which they appear in
\pi
.
For each
1\leq i\leq 2k
, we define edge
e_{i}=(v_{i},v_{i+1})
and define
E^{i}_{\pi}
to be the set of all edges traversed by
\pi
between vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
.
Clearly,
{\mathsf{cov}}(e_{i})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{i}_{\pi})
.
We define
E_{0}=\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k-1}E^{2i}_{\pi}
and
E_{1}=\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k-1}E^{2i+1}_{\pi}
.

Consider now the tour
\pi^{\prime}
induced by edges of
e_{1},\ldots,e_{2k}
. Clearly,
\pi^{\prime}
is a tour that visits all vertices of
V^{\prime}
. We define sets

F_{0}=\left\{e_{2i}\mid 0\leq i\leq k-1\right\}
and
F_{1}=\left\{e_{2i+1}\mid 0\leq i\leq k-1\right\}
, so
E(\pi^{\prime})=F_{0}\cup F_{1}
.
We now show that
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(F_{0})
and
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(F_{1})
.
Note that this implies that
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{0})
and
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{1})
, since

{\mathsf{cov}}(F_{0})=\bigg(\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k-1}{\mathsf{cov}}(e_{2i})\bigg)\subseteq\bigg(\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k-1}{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{2i}_{\pi})\bigg)\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}\bigg(\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq k-1}E^{2i}_{\pi}\bigg)={\mathsf{cov}}(E_{0}),

and similarly
{\mathsf{cov}}(F_{1})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{1})
.

In fact, note that
F_{0}
is a perfect matching on
V^{\prime}
. Since
V^{\prime}
is the set of odd-degree vertices of
T^{\prime}
, the graph on
V(T^{\prime})
induced by edges of
E(T^{\prime})\cup F_{0}
is Eulerian. Therefore, every edge of
T^{\prime}
appears in at least two sets of
\left\{{\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})\mid e^{\prime}\in E(T^{\prime})\cup M_{o}\right\}
. Note that for each
e^{\prime}\in E(T^{\prime})
,
{\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})=\left\{e^{\prime}\right\}
. Therefore, every edge
e\in E(T^{\prime})
appears in at least one set of
\left\{{\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})\mid e^{\prime}\in F_{0}\right\}
, i.e.,
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(F_{0})
. Similarly, we get that
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(F_{1})
.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

2.5
.
∎

Using
Lemma

2.5
, we show in the next lemma that, if the special cover advantage is small, then the TSP cost is close to
2
times the MST cost.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
T^{\prime}
be any subtree of an MST
T
. Then
\mathsf{TSP}\geq 2\cdot w(T^{\prime})-2\cdot\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
\pi^{*}
be the optimal TSP-tour that visits all vertices of
V
, so
\mathsf{TSP}=w(\pi^{*})
.
Let
\pi
be the tour obtained from
\pi^{*}
by deleting all vertices of
T\setminus T^{\prime}
, so
\pi
is a tour that visits all vertices of
V(T^{\prime})
, and, from triangle inequality,
w(\pi^{*})\geq w(\pi)
. We define set
V^{\prime}
and edge sets
F_{0},F_{1},E_{0},E_{1}
in the same way as in the proof of
Lemma

2.5
. From triangle inequality,
w(F_{0})+w(F_{1})\leq w(E_{0})+w(E_{1})=w(\pi)
. Note that edges of
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
have both endpoint in
V^{\prime}
, and moreover, from the definition of
V^{\prime}
, all vertices of
V^{\prime}
are special vertices of
T^{\prime}
.
Since
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(F_{0})
, from the definition of
\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})
, we get that

\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})\geq w({\mathsf{cov}}(F_{0},T^{\prime}))-w(F_{0})=w(T^{\prime})-w(F_{0})
, and similarly
\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})\geq w(T^{\prime})-w(F_{1})
. Therefore,

\mathsf{TSP}=w(\pi^{*})\geq w(\pi)=w(E_{0})+w(E_{1})\geq w(F_{0})+w(F_{1})\geq 2\cdot(w(T^{\prime})-\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})).

∎

The last two lemmas in this section show that the total cover advantage and the total special cover advantage of a set of small edge-disjoint paths of an MST
T
can be efficiently and accurately estimated.

Lemma 2.7
.

There is an algorithm, that, given a constant
0<\varepsilon<1
and a set
{\mathcal{T}}
of edge-disjoint subtrees of
T
, with high probability, either correctly reports that
\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})\geq\varepsilon\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, or correctly reports that
\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})\leq 2\varepsilon\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, by performing
\tilde{O}((n/\varepsilon^{2})\cdot\max\left\{|V(T^{\prime})|\right\}_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}})
queries.

Proof.

Denote
s=\max\left\{|V(T^{\prime})|\right\}_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}
.
We define the random variable
\gamma
as follows. Denote
w({\mathcal{T}})=\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}w(T^{\prime})
. For each subtree
T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}
, we let
\gamma
takes value
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})/w(T^{\prime})
with probability
w(T^{\prime})/w({\mathcal{T}})
. Therefore,
\gamma
is supported on
[0,1]
, and

\mathbb{E}[\gamma]=\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\frac{\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})}{w(T^{\prime})}\cdot\frac{w(T^{\prime})}{w({\mathcal{T}})}=\frac{\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})}{w({\mathcal{T}})}=\frac{\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})}{{\mathsf{MST}}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{{\mathsf{MST}}}{w({\mathcal{T}})}\bigg).

From Chernoff Bound, for every
0<\varepsilon<1
, the value of
\mathbb{E}[\gamma]
can be estimated to within an additive error of
\varepsilon/2
, by
\tilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2})
independent samples of
\gamma
.
Since
w({\mathcal{T}})\leq{\mathsf{MST}}
, it follows that the value of
\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})/w({\mathcal{T}})
can be also estimated to within an additive error of
\varepsilon/2
by
\tilde{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2})
independent samples of
\gamma
.
It suffices to show that we can obtain one independent sample of
\gamma
using
O(ns)
queries. In fact, we can sample a subtree from the distribution on
{\mathcal{T}}
, in which each
T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}
has probability
w(T^{\prime})/w({\mathcal{T}})
, and then we use the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
to compute the value
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})
using at most
O(ns)
queries, and finally return the value
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})/w(T^{\prime})
. It is easy to verify that this value is a sample of
\gamma
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

2.7
.
∎

Similarly, using the algorithm from
Lemma

2.2
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 2.8
.

There is an algorithm, that, given a constant
0<\varepsilon<1
and a set
{\mathcal{T}}
of edge-disjoint subtrees of
T
, with high probability, either correctly reports that
\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})\geq\varepsilon\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, or correctly reports that
\sum_{T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})\leq 2\varepsilon\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, by performing
\tilde{O}((n/\varepsilon^{2})\cdot\max\left\{k_{T^{\prime}}\mid T^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}\right\})
queries, where
k_{T^{\prime}}
is the number of special vertices in
T^{\prime}
.

Part I
Streaming Algorithms and Lower Bounds

In this part we present our results on streaming algorithms and lower bounds for MST and TSP cost estimation. We start with some preliminaries in this part, and then provide the proofs of
Theorems

1
,
3
,
4
,
2
,
6
and
5
in the subsequent sections.

1
Preliminaries

By default, all logarithms in this paper are to the base of
2
. All graphs are undirected and simple.

1.1
Graph-Theoretic Notation

We follow standard graph-theoretic notation.
Let
G=(V,E)
be an undirected unweighted graph.
For a vertex
v\in V
, we denote by
\deg_{G}(v)
the degree of vertex
v
in
G
.
For a pair
A,B
of disjoint subsets of vertices of
G
, we denote by
E_{G}(A,B)
the set of edges in
G
with one endpoint in
A
and the other endpoint in
B
.
For a subset
A
of vertices of
G
, we denote by
\delta_{G}(A)
the set of edges with exactly one endpoint in
A
.
We may sometimes omit the subscript
G
in the notation above if the graph
G
is clear from the context.

Let
V
be a set of vertices and let
w:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
be a metric on
V
. For an edge
e=(u,v)
where
u,v\in V
, we sometimes write
w(e)=w(u,v)
. We denote by
{\mathsf{MST}}(w)
the MST cost of the weighted complete graph on
V
with edge weights given by the metric
w
, and we denote by
\mathsf{TSP}(w)
minimum metric TSP cost of same graph.

Let
T
be a tree rooted at some vertex of
V(T)
. For a vertex
v
of
T
, we denote by
T_{v}
the subtree of
T
rooted at vertex
v
.
We say that a vertex
v
in
T
is a
special vertex
, iff
\deg_{T}(v)\neq 2
.
For a pair
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices in
T
, we call the unique path in
T
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
the
tree path
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
, which we denote by
P^{T}_{v,v^{\prime}}
.
If vertices
u,v
are connected by an edge
e
that does not belong to
T
, then we also write
P^{T}_{e}=P^{T}_{u,v}
.
Let
T
be a tree rooted at some vertex
r\in V(T)
. Let
\hat{T}
be a subtree of
T
. We call the vertex of
\hat{T}
that is closest to
r
in
T
the root of subtree
\hat{T}
.

Euler Tour of a tree.

Let
T
be a rooted tree. The
euler tour
of
T
, or equivalently the
depth-first traversal
of
T
, is a vertex traversal of
T
that begins and ends at the root of
T
, traversing each edge of
T
exactly twice – once to enter the subtree and once to exit it.

Graph Streams and Metric Streams.

In the first part of the paper, we consider two types of streams:
graph streams
and
metric streams
. Let
w
be a metric on a set
V
of
n
vertices. A metric stream of
w
contains the distances
w(v,v^{\prime})
between all pairs
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices in
V
. A graph stream of
w
contains all edges of an
n
-vertex weighted graph that induces the metric
w
. Specifically, let
G
be a minimal (inclusion-wise) weighted graph on
V
such that the shortest-path distance metric in
G
on
V
is identical to
w
, then a graph stream of
w
contains all edges (and their weights) of
G
.

1.2
Tools from Information Theory

Let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
be random variables. We denote by
H(\mathbf{X})
the
Shannon Entropy
of
\mathbf{X}
, and denote by
I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y})
the
mutual information
of
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
. By definition,

I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y})=H(\mathbf{X})-H(\mathbf{X}|\mathbf{Y})=H(\mathbf{Y})-H(\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X})
.
We will sometimes use
I_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y})
instead of
I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y})
if the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
of the random variables
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
is unclear from the context.
We use the following basic properties of entropy and mutual information.

Claim 1.1
.

Let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{W}
be random variables. Then

1.

I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})=I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Z}|\mathbf{Y})+I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y})
(the chain rule for mutual information);

2.

If
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{W}
are independent conditioned on
\mathbf{Z}
, then

I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{W})\geq I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{Z})
.

Total variation distance, KL-divergence, and Hellinger distance.

Let
P,Q
be distributions on a discrete set
\Omega
. The
total variation distance
between
P
and
Q
, is defined as

\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}|P(\omega)-Q(\omega)|.

The
KL-divergence
between
P
and
Q
is defined as

D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}(P\|Q)=\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}P(\omega)\cdot\log\left(\frac{P(\omega)}{Q(\omega)}\right).

The
Hellinger distance
between
P
and
Q
is defined as

h(P,Q)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}\bigg(\sqrt{P(\omega)}-\sqrt{Q(\omega)}\bigg)^{2}}=\sqrt{1-\sum_{\omega\in\Omega}\sqrt{P(\omega)Q(\omega)}}.

We use properties of the above measures between distributions.

Claim 1.2
.

Let
P,Q,R
be distributions on
\Omega
. Then
h(P,Q)\leq h(P,R)+h(Q,R)
, and
\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(P,Q)\leq\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(P,R)+\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(Q,R)
.

Claim 1.3

(Pinsker’s Inequality)
.

Let
P,Q
be distributions on
\Omega
. Then
\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(P,Q)\leq\sqrt{2D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}(P\|Q)}
.

Claim 1.4

(​​​
[
Har11
]
​)
.

Let
P,Q
be distributions on
\Omega
. Then
h^{2}(P,Q)\leq\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(P,Q)\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot h(P,Q)
.

Claim 1.5

(​​​
[
Lin91
]
​)
.

Let
P,Q
be distributions on
\Omega
. Then

h^{2}(P,Q)\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}\bigg(P\text{ }\bigg|\bigg|\text{ }\frac{P+Q}{2}\bigg)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}\bigg(Q\text{ }\bigg|\bigg|\text{ }\frac{P+Q}{2}\bigg).

Let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
be any random variables.
We denote by
P_{\mathbf{X}}
the distribution of
\mathbf{X}
, by
P_{\mathbf{Y}}
the distribution of
\mathbf{X}
, and by
P_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y}}
the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{X}
given
\mathbf{Y}
. We use the following claim.

Claim 1.6
.

I(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{Y})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{Y}}\bigg[D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}\big(P_{\mathbf{X}\mid\mathbf{Y}}\mid\mid P_{\mathbf{X}}\big)\bigg]
.

1.3
Communication Complexity and Information Complexity

In this paper we will work with two standard communication models: the
2-player one-way communication
model and the

multi-party blackboard communication
model.
In this subsection we provide the definitions of communication complexity and information complexity in these two models.

Two-player one-way communication model.

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}
be discrete sets and let
P\subseteq\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
be a relation (we will also call
P
a
problem
).
Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be a distribution over the product set
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, and let
(X,Y)
(where
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
and
Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}
) be a pair sampled from
{\mathcal{D}}
.
Alice receives
X
as her input and Bob receives
Y
as his input.
In the 2-player one-way communication model, Alice is allowed to send to Bob a single message based on her input
X
, and Bob, upon receiving this message, needs to output an answer
Z
. We say that the answer is correct iff
(X,Y,Z)\in P
.

We always allow a one-way protocol of Alice and Bob to use both public and private randomness, even against a prior distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
on the input pairs
(X,Y)\in{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Let
\pi
be an one-way protocol. We say that
\pi
is an
\delta
-error protocol (for some
0<\delta<1
) for
P
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
, iff the probability that, upon receiving a random pair
(X,Y)\in{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}
from distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
as the input, the protocol computes an answer
Z
with
(X,Y,Z)\notin P
, is at most
\delta
.

Definition 1.1
.

The cost of a one-way protocol
\pi
for a problem
P
with respect to an input distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
, denoted by
\textnormal{{CC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}(\pi)
, is defined to be the expected bit-length of the transcript communicated from Alice to Bob in
\pi
, when the input pair is sampled from distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
.
The communication complexity of an one-way protocol
\pi
, denoted by
\textnormal{{CC}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}(\pi)
, is defined to be the maximum cost of
\pi
with respect to any distribution, namely
\textnormal{{CC}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}(\pi)=\max_{{\mathcal{D}}}\left\{\textnormal{{CC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}(\pi)\right\}
.

If the input pair is
(X,Y)
, we denote by
\Pi_{X,Y}
the transcript of protocol
\pi
when executed on the input pair
(X,Y)
. When the procotol
\pi
is randomized, we denote by
\mathbf{\Pi}_{X,Y}
the random variable representing the transcript of
\pi
when the input pair is
(X,Y)
. When the inputs are random variables
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})
, we denote
\mathbf{\Pi}=\mathbf{\Pi}_{\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}}
.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
\pi
be a protocol for a problem
P
with respect to input distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
. The
information cost
of
\pi
is defined to be
\textnormal{{IC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\pi)=I_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\mathbf{\Pi};\mathbf{X}|\mathbf{Y})+I_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\mathbf{\Pi};\mathbf{Y}|\mathbf{X})
.

We use the following well-known propositions.

Proposition 1.1

(​​​
[
BR14
]
​)
.

Let
P
be a problem and let
{\mathcal{D}}
be an input distribution of
P
. Then for any one-way protocol
\pi
for
P
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
,
\textnormal{{IC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\pi)\leq\textnormal{{CC}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\pi)
.

Proposition 1.2

(​​​
[
BJKS04
]
​)
.

Let
\pi
be any randomized protocol. Let
X,X^{\prime}
be any pair of elements in
\mathcal{X}
and let
Y,Y^{\prime}
be any pair of elements in
\mathcal{Y}
. Then,
h(\mathbf{\Pi}_{X,Y},\mathbf{\Pi}_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}})=h(\mathbf{\Pi}_{X,Y^{\prime}},\mathbf{\Pi}_{X^{\prime},Y})
.

Multi-party blackboard communication model.

Let
P\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{X}_{k}\times\mathcal{Y}
be a relation.
In the multi-party communication model, there are
k
players, that are denoted by
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq k
, the player
P_{i}
receives an element
X_{i}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{i}
as her own input, where the tuple
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})
is sampled from some input distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
on the product set
\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{X}_{k}
.
The players
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
may exchange several messages publicly by writing them on a shared blackboard for all other players to see.
Eventually, some players will announce an output
Y
of the function. We say that the answer is correct iff
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k},Y)\in P
.
For convenience, we will also call it the blackboard model.

Similar to the two-player one-way communication model, we allow a protocol to use both public and private randomness, and we say that
\pi
is an
\delta
-error protocol (for some
0<\delta<1
) for
P
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
, iff the probability that, upon receiving a random tuple
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{X}_{k}
from distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
as the input, the protocol computes an answer
Y
with
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k},Y)\notin P
, is at most
\delta
.
The cost of a protocol
\pi
for a problem
P
with respect to an input distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
, denoted by
\textnormal{{CC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\pi)
, is defined to be the expected bit-length of the transcript communicated between the players
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
in
\pi
, when their input is sampled from
{\mathcal{D}}
.
The communication complexity of a protocol
\pi
, denoted by
\textnormal{{CC}}(\pi)
, is defined to be the maximum cost of
\pi
with respect to any distribution.

We denote
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})
, and for each
1\leq i\leq k
, we denote
X_{-i}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{i-1},X_{i+1},\ldots,X_{k})
. Let
\pi
be a protocol for
P
over
{\mathcal{D}}
. We denote by
\Pi
the transcript of
\pi
and by
\mathbf{\Pi}
the random variable representing the transcript of
\pi
, when
\pi
is randomized.
We use the following propositions.

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
P
be a problem and let
{\mathcal{D}}
be an input distribution.
Let
\pi
be any
\delta
-error protocol for
P
over
{\mathcal{D}}
in the blackboard model. Then for each
1\leq i\leq k
,
I_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\mathbf{\Pi};\mathbf{X}_{-i}|\mathbf{X}_{i})\leq\textnormal{{CC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\pi)
.

Proposition 1.4

(​​​
[
BJKS04
]
​)
.

Let
P
be a problem and let
\pi
be a randomized protocol for
P
. Let
X,X^{\prime}
be a pair of inputs to
P
. Let
A\subseteq\left\{1,\ldots,k\right\}
be an index set.
If we define
X^{a}
as the vector such that for each
i\in A
,
X^{a}_{i}=X_{i}
, and for each
i\notin A
,
X^{a}_{i}=X^{\prime}_{i}
; and we define
X^{b}
as the vector such that for each
i\in A
,
X^{b}_{i}=X^{\prime}_{i}
, and for each
i\notin A
,
X^{b}_{i}=X_{i}
, then
h(\mathbf{\Pi}_{X},\mathbf{\Pi}_{X^{\prime}})=h(\mathbf{\Pi}_{X^{a}},\mathbf{\Pi}_{X^{b}})
.

Lastly, we mention the connection between
the communication complexity defined in this section and the space complexity of streaming algorithms. It is well-known that any streaming algorithm can be immediately converted into a one-way communication protocol, and therefore any communication complexity lower bound in the one-way communication model implies the same lower bound on the space complexity of one-pass streaming algorithms.
Similarly, any communication complexity lower bound in the blackboard communication model also implies the same lower bound on the space complexity of multi-pass streaming algorithms.

2
One-Pass MST Estimation in Metric Streams

In this section, we provide results on one-pass streaming algorithms for MST cost estimation, when the algorithm is given as input a metric stream. We will show an upper bound on the space complexity in
Section

2.1
and a lower bound in
Section

2.2
.
Our proof for the lower bound in
Section

2.2
is via analyzing a two-player communication game, and we show in
Section

2.3
that our analysis in
Section

2.2
is asymptotically optimal by giving a near-optimal protocol for the game.

2.1
A One-Pass Streaming Algorithm for MST Estimation

In this subsection, we will construct a one-pass streaming algorithm that outputs an
\alpha
-approximation of the minimum spanning tree weight, using
\tilde{O}(n/\alpha)
space, thus establishing
Theorem

1
.
At a high level, our algorithm computes the estimate by sampling a subset
V^{\prime}
of vertices, computing the weight of an MST on vertices of
V^{\prime}
, and then adding to it an estimate of the distances of all remaining vertices to
V^{\prime}
.
Clearly, the result computed by the algorithm is the total weight of some spanning tree, and is, therefore, lower bounded by
{\mathsf{MST}}
.

Algorithm.

We perform three tasks in parallel over the stream.
In the first task, we simply record the largest weight over all edges in the stream, namely we record
\textsf{diam}(w)=\max\left\{w(u,v)\mid u,v\in V\right\}
, the diameter of metric
w
, using
\tilde{O}(1)
space.
In the second task, we sample a subset
V^{\prime}
of
k=\left\lceil n/\alpha\right\rceil
distinct vertices in
V
uniformly at random at the start, and then compute a minimum spanning tree
T^{\prime}
on vertices of
V^{\prime}
and its cost
{\mathsf{MST}}^{\prime}
, using
\tilde{O}(n/\alpha)
space over the stream.
In the third task, we sample a subset
V^{\prime\prime}
of
k=\left\lceil n/\alpha\right\rceil
vertices in set
V\setminus V^{\prime}
uniformly at random at the start, and for each vertex
v\in V^{\prime\prime}
, we record
\textnormal{{dist}}_{w}(v,V^{\prime})=\min\left\{w(v,v^{\prime})\mid v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}\right\}
, using a total of
\tilde{O}(n/\alpha)
space.
Finally, we output
{\mathsf{MST}}^{\prime}+100\cdot\alpha\log n\cdot(\textsf{diam}(w)+\sum_{v\in V^{\prime\prime}}\textnormal{{dist}}_{w}(v,V^{\prime}))
as the estimate of the cost of a minimum spanning tree on
V
.

Approximation Ratio Analysis.

It is clear that the algorithm described above uses
\tilde{O}(n/\alpha)
space. We show that with high probability,

{\mathsf{MST}}\leq{\mathsf{MST}}^{\prime}+100\cdot\alpha\log n\cdot\bigg(\textsf{diam}(w)+\sum_{v\in V^{\prime\prime}}\textnormal{{dist}}_{w}(v,V^{\prime})\bigg)\leq\tilde{O}(\alpha)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}.

(1)

Let
T
be a minimum spanning tree on
V
. Let
\pi=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{2n-2})
be an Euler-tour of
T
, and for each
1\leq t\leq 2n-2
, we denote by
R_{\pi,t}=\left\{v_{i}\mid t\leq i\leq t+100\alpha\log n\right\}
.
We now define two bad events such that if neither of them happens, then
1
holds. We then show that with high probability, neither event happens.

Bad event
\xi_{1}
.

Let
\xi_{1}
be the event that there exists some
t
, such that
R_{\pi,t}\cap V^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
We now show that
\Pr[\xi_{1}]=O(n^{-49})
.
Since each edge of
T
appears at most twice in the set
\left\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\mid v_{i}\in R_{\pi,t}\right\}
,
R_{\pi,t}
contains at least
50\alpha\log n
distinct vertices. Therefore, the probability that a random subset of
\left\lceil n/\alpha\right\rceil
vertices in
V
does not intersect with
R_{\pi,t}
is at most
(1-(50\alpha\log n/n))^{n/\alpha}\leq n^{-50}
. Taking the union bound over all
1\leq t\leq 2n-2
, we get that
\Pr[\xi_{1}]=O(n^{-49})
.
Note that, if the event
\xi_{1}
does not happen, then every consecutive window of
\pi
of length
100\alpha\log n
contains at least one element of
V^{\prime}
.

Let
v_{i_{1}},v_{i_{2}},\ldots,v_{i_{k^{\prime}}}
be the vertices of
\pi
that belongs to
V^{\prime}
(
k^{\prime}
may be larger than
k
since we keep all copies of the same vertex), then for each
1\leq j\leq k^{\prime}
,
|i_{j}-i_{j+1}|\leq 100\alpha\log n
.

Claim 2.1
.

If the event
\xi_{1}
does not happen, then
{\mathsf{MST}}\leq{\mathsf{MST}}^{\prime}+\sum_{v\in V\setminus V^{\prime}}\emph{\textnormal{{dist}}}_{w}(v,V^{\prime})\leq\tilde{O}(\alpha)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

Proof.

On the one hand, if we denote, for each
v\in V\setminus V^{\prime}
, by
v^{*}
the vertex of
V^{\prime}
that is closest to
v
(under the metric
w
), then it is clear that the edges of
E(T^{\prime})\cup\left\{(v,v^{*})\mid v\in V\setminus V^{\prime}\right\}
form a spanning tree on
V
, and therefore

{\mathsf{MST}}^{\prime}+\sum_{v\in V\setminus V^{\prime}}\textnormal{{dist}}_{w}(v,V^{\prime})\geq{\mathsf{MST}}
.

On the other hand, consider now a vertex
v\in V\setminus V^{\prime}
. Assume that the first appearance of vertex
v
in the tour
\pi
appears between
v_{i_{j}}
and
v_{i_{j+1}}
. We then define
\textnormal{{dist}}_{\pi}(v,V^{\prime})=\min\left\{w(v,v_{i_{j}}),w(v,v_{i_{j+1}})\right\}
, and it is easy to see that, by triangle inequality
\textnormal{{dist}}_{\pi}(v,V^{\prime})\leq\sum_{i_{j}\leq t\leq i_{j+1}-1}w(v_{t},v_{t+1})
.
Since the event
\xi_{1}
does not happen, we get that
\sum_{v\in V\setminus V^{\prime}}\textnormal{{dist}}_{\pi}(v,V^{\prime})\leq 100\alpha\log n\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.
Finally, since
{\mathsf{MST}}^{\prime}\leq{\mathsf{MST}}
, we conclude that
\sum_{v\in V\setminus V^{\prime}}\textnormal{{dist}}_{w}(v,V^{\prime})\leq\tilde{O}(\alpha)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.
∎

Define
W=\sum_{v\in V\setminus V^{\prime}}\textnormal{{dist}}_{w}(v,V^{\prime})
and
W^{\prime\prime}=\sum_{v\in V^{\prime\prime}}\textnormal{{dist}}_{w}(v,V^{\prime})
. We prove the following claim.

Claim 2.2
.

If
\emph{\textsf{diam}}(w)\leq W/(100\alpha\log n)
, then with probability
1-O(n^{-50})
,
W/2\leq\alpha\cdot W^{\prime\prime}\leq 2W
.

Proof.

Let
U_{V\setminus V^{\prime}}
be the uniform distribution on all vertices of
V\setminus V^{\prime}
.
We define the random variable
X:V\setminus V^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
as
X(v)=\textnormal{{dist}}_{w}(v,V^{\prime})
for each vertex
v\in V\setminus V^{\prime}
. Therefore,
X
is supported on the interval
[0,\textsf{diam}(w)]\subseteq[0,W/(100\alpha\log n)]
, and
\mathbb{E}[X]=W/(n-k)
. Observe that
W^{\prime\prime}
is in fact the sum of
k
i.i.d samples of
X
. From multiplicative Chernoff bound,

\Pr\bigg[\frac{Wk}{(3/2)(n-k)}\leq W^{\prime\prime}\leq\frac{(3/2)Wk}{n-k}\bigg]\geq 1-\exp\bigg(-k\cdot\frac{W/(n-k)}{W/(100\alpha\log n)}\bigg)\geq 1-n^{-50}.

Since
\alpha\cdot\frac{(3/2)Wk}{n-k}\leq 2W
and
\alpha\cdot\frac{Wk}{(3/2)(n-k)}\geq W/2
, with probability
1-O(n^{-50})
,
W/2\leq\alpha\cdot W^{\prime\prime}\leq W
.
∎

Bad event
\xi_{2}
.

Let
\xi_{2}
be the event that
\textsf{diam}(w)\leq W/(100\alpha\log n)
holds, and either
\alpha W^{\prime\prime}>2W
or
\alpha W^{\prime\prime}<W/2
holds.
From
2.2
,
\Pr[\xi_{2}]=O(n^{-50})
.

We are now ready to show that, if both events
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
do not happen, then (
1
) holds.
On the one hand, if
\textsf{diam}(w)\geq W/(100\alpha\log n)
, then
100\alpha\log n\cdot\textsf{diam}(w)\geq{\mathsf{MST}}
; if
\textsf{diam}(w)\leq W/(100\alpha\log n)
, then since
\xi_{2}
does not happen,
\alpha\cdot W^{\prime\prime}\geq W/2
, and then since
\xi_{1}
does not happen and from
2.1
,
{\mathsf{MST}}^{\prime}+100\alpha\log n\cdot W^{\prime\prime}\geq{\mathsf{MST}}^{\prime}+W\geq{\mathsf{MST}}
.
On the other hand, note that
{\mathsf{MST}}^{\prime}\leq{\mathsf{MST}}
and
\textsf{diam}(w)\leq{\mathsf{MST}}
. Moreover, since
\xi_{2}
does not happen,
\alpha\cdot W^{\prime\prime}\leq 2W
. Therefore,

{\mathsf{MST}}+100\alpha\log n\cdot\textsf{diam}(w)+100\alpha\log n\cdot W^{\prime\prime}\leq{\mathsf{MST}}+\tilde{O}(\alpha)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+\tilde{O}(\alpha)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}=\tilde{O}(\alpha)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}.

2.2
Space Lower Bound for One-Pass Streaming Algorithms for MST Estimation

In this subsection, we will show that
any one-pass streaming algorithm that outputs an
\alpha
-approximation of the cost of a minimum spanning tree needs to use space
\Omega(n/\alpha^{2})
, thus establishing
Theorem

3
.
At a high level, our proof consider a 2-player communication game, in which Alice and Bob are each given a partial metric, and are asked to estimate the MST cost of the complete metric obtained by combining their partial metrics to within a factor of
\alpha
. It is well-known that the space complexity of any one-pass
\alpha
-approximation streaming algorithm is lower bounded by the one-way communication complexity of the game. We then show that the one-way communication complexity of the game is
\Omega(n/\alpha^{2})
by constructing a hard distribution and analyzing the information complexity of any randomized protocol for this game.

In order to describe the communication game, we first introduce the following notion on metrics and partial metrics.

Metrics and partial metrics.

We say that a function
\bar{w}:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}\cup\left\{*\right\}
is a
partial metric
on
V
iff for all sequences
x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}
of vertices of
V
such that
\bar{w}(x_{i},x_{i+1})\neq*
for all
1\leq i\leq t
(where we use the convention that
t+1=1
), the inequality
\sum_{1\leq i\leq t-1}\bar{w}(x_{i},x_{i+1})\geq\bar{w}(x_{1},x_{t})
holds.
Equivalently,
\bar{w}
is a partial metric iff it can be obtained from some complete metric
w
by masking some of its entries by
*
.
Given a subset
S\subseteq V\times V
, we define the
restriction of
w
on set
S
, denoted by
\bar{w}_{\mid S}
, to be the partial metric where for each pair
(x,y)\in V\times V
, if
(x,y)\in S
, then
\bar{w}_{\mid S}(x,y)=w(x,y)
; otherwise
\bar{w}_{\mid S}(x,y)=*
.
Given a partial metric
\bar{w}
, we say that a complete metric
w
is a
completion
of
\bar{w}
, iff for all pairs
x,y
of vertices in
V
such that
\bar{w}(x,y)\neq*
,
w(x,y)=\bar{w}(x,y)
. In other words,
w
is a completion of
\bar{w}
iff
\bar{w}
is the restriction of
w
on some subset.

Let
\bar{w},\bar{w}^{\prime}
be two partial metrics on
V
, we say that partial metrics
\bar{w},\bar{w}^{\prime}
are
complementary
, iff there is a complete metric
w
on
V
and two subsets
S,S^{\prime}\subseteq V\times V
where
S\cup S^{\prime}=V\times V
, such that
\bar{w}
is the restriction of
w
on
S
, and
\bar{w}^{\prime}
is the restriction of
w
on
\bar{S}^{\prime}
.
In this case, we also write
w=\bar{w}\cup\bar{w}^{\prime}
.

Two-player metric-MST-cost approximation problem (
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
):

This is a one-way communication problem, in which Alice is given a partial metric
\bar{w}_{A}
and Bob is given a partial metric
\bar{w}_{B}
. The goal is to compute an estimate
Y
of
{\mathsf{MST}}(\bar{w}_{A}\cup\bar{w}_{B})
, when partial metrics
\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B}
are complementary. If
\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B}
are not complementary, then any answer returned by the protocol will be viewed as a correct answer.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be a distribution on the input pairs
(\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B})
of partial metrics.
We say that a protocol
\pi
of Alice and Bob
(\alpha,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
for some real numbers
\alpha>1
and
0<\delta<1
, iff, whenever
\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B}
are complementary, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the estimate
Y
returned by protocol
\pi
satisfies that
Y\leq{\mathsf{MST}}(w)\leq\alpha\cdot Y
.

The main result of this section is the following theorem, which immediately implies
Theorem

3
.

Theorem 9
.

For any real numbers
\alpha>1
and
0<\delta<1/10
, there exists a distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
on input pairs of the problem
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
, such that any one-way protocol
\pi
that
(\alpha,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
over
{\mathcal{D}}
has
\emph{\textnormal{{CC}}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\pi)=\Omega(n/\alpha^{2})
.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

9
. We first define a new problem called the
Overwrite
problem, and then prove
Theorem

9
by reducing this new problem to the
\textsf{MST}_{\textsf{apx}}
problem.

2.2.1
The Problem
\emph{\sf{Overwrite}}
and its Communication Complexity

Overwrite problem (
\emph{\sf{Overwrite}}
)
: This is a
2
-player one-way communication problem, in which Alice is given a vector
X^{A}\in\left\{0,1,*\right\}^{n}
, and Bob is given a vector
X^{B}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
and an index
i^{*}\in[n]
.
They are promised that, for each
i\neq i^{*}
, either
X^{A}_{i}=X^{B}_{i}
or
X^{A}_{i}=*
holds.
Alice is allowed to send a message to Bob, and upon receiving the message, the goal of Bob is to figure out the value of
X^{A}_{i^{*}}
, the
i^{*}
-th coordinate of Alice’s input vector. If
X^{A}_{i^{*}}\neq*
, then the correct output of Bob is
X^{A}_{i^{*}}
; otherwise both
0
and
1
are accepted as correct outputs.

We consider the following distribution of input pairs to the problem
\sf{Overwrite}
.

Distribution

\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{Overwrite}}
: A distribution on input pairs
(X^{A},X^{B})
to
\sf{Overwrite}
:

1.

Choose an index
i^{*}
uniformly at random from
[n]
.

2.

Choose
X^{B}
uniformly at random from the set
\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
.

3.

Set
X^{A}_{i^{*}}
to be
*
with probability
1/2
,
0
with probability
1/4
, and
1
with probability
1/4
. For each
i\in[n]
such that
i\neq i^{*}
, set
X^{A}_{i}
to be
*
with probability
1/2
and
X^{B}_{i}
with probability
1/2
.

The main result of this subsection is the following lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\delta
be any constant such that
0<\delta<1/10
. Then any
\delta
-error one-way communication protocol
\pi
for
\emph{\sf{Overwrite}}
problem over the input distribution
{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf Overwrite}
has
\emph{\textnormal{{CC}}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\textnormal{\sf Overwrite}}}(\pi)=\Omega(n)
.

Proof.

Consider the following one-bit version of the
\sf{Overwrite}
problem. Alice is given a bit
\hat{X}^{A}
, which is
*
with probability
1/2
,
0
with probability
1/4
, and
1
with probability
1/4
. Bob is also given a bit
\hat{X}^{B}
, which is chosen uniformly at random from set
\left\{0,1\right\}
.
We denote this input distribution by
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}
.
Alice is allowed to send a message to Bob, such that upon receiving this message, Bob needs to figure out the value of
\hat{X}^{A}
. Note that if
\hat{X}^{A}=*
, then Bob can return either
0
or
1
as the correct answer. We first show in the following claim that any protocol that allows Alice and Bob to return a correct answer with probability at least
9/10
has information complexity
\Omega(1)
.

Claim 2.3
.

For any
\delta
-error one-way protocol
\pi
for the one-bit
\sf{Overwrite}
problem,
\textnormal{{IC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{*}}(\pi)=\Omega(1)
.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be any
\delta
-error one-way protocol for the one-bit
\sf{Overwrite}
problem. Recall that
0<\delta<1/10
. We will show that
\textnormal{{IC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{*}}(\pi)>10^{-5}
. Assume for contradiction that this does not hold. We denote by
\Pi
the random variable representing the transcript of
\pi
. By definition,
I_{{\mathcal{D}}^{*}}(\Pi;\hat{X}^{A}|\hat{X}^{B})\leq\textnormal{{IC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{*}}(\pi)\leq 10^{-5}
. We denote by
\Pi_{*}
the random variable representing the transcript of
\pi
when
\hat{X}^{A}=*
, and we define
\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1}
similarly. Since random variables
\hat{X}^{A}
and
\hat{X}^{B}
are independent in
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}
, from
2
of
1.1
and
1.6
,

10^{-5}\geq I_{{\mathcal{D}}^{*}}(\Pi;\hat{X}^{A}|\hat{X}^{B})\geq I_{{\mathcal{D}}^{*}}(\Pi;\hat{X}^{A})=\frac{D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}(\Pi_{*}\|\Pi)}{2}+\frac{D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}(\Pi_{0}\|\Pi)}{4}+\frac{D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}(\Pi_{1}\|\Pi)}{4}.

From
1.3
,
\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(\Pi_{0},\Pi)\leq\sqrt{2D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}(\Pi_{0}\|\Pi)}\leq 10^{-2}
, and similarly
\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(\Pi_{1},\Pi)\leq\sqrt{2D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}(\Pi_{1}\|\Pi)}\leq 10^{-2}
. Then from
1.2
,
\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})\leq 1/50
. Recall that in distribution
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}
, with probability
1/2
,
\hat{X}_{A}=*
; with probability
1/4
,
\hat{X}_{A}=0
; and with probability
1/4
,
\hat{X}_{A}=1
.
Since
\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})\leq 1/50
, protocol
\pi
is correct with probability at most

\Pr[\hat{X}_{A}=*]+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\Pr[\hat{X}_{A}=0]+\Pr[\hat{X}_{A}=1]+2\cdot\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(\Pi_{0},\Pi_{1})\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{50}<\frac{9}{10},

a contradiction to the fact that
\pi
is an
\delta
-error protocol for
0<\delta<1/10
.
∎

Let
\pi_{\sf{Overwrite}}
be a
\delta
-error protocol for some
0<\delta<1/10
for the
\sf{Overwrite}
problem. We now construct a
\delta
-error protocol for the one-bit
\sf{Overwrite}
problem using
\pi_{\sf{Overwrite}}
as follows.

Protocol

\pi
: A protocol for one-bit
\sf{Overwrite}
using a protocol
\pi_{\sf{Overwrite}}
for
\sf{Overwrite}
.

Input:
An instance
(\hat{X}^{A},\hat{X}^{B})
of the one-bit
\sf{Overwrite}
problem.

Output:

\hat{Z}\in\left\{0,1\right\}
as the answer to the one-bit
\sf{Overwrite}
problem.

1.

Sampling the instance.
Alice and Bob create an instance
(X^{A},X^{B})
of
\sf{Overwrite}
as follows.

(a)

Choose an index
i^{*}
uniformly at random from
[n]
, using
public coins
.

(b)

For each
1\leq i<i^{*}
, using
public coins
, Alice and Bob jointly sample
X^{A}_{i}
from distribution
{\mathcal{D}}^{*}
. If
X^{A}_{i}=*
, then Bob chooses
X^{B}_{i}
uniformly at random from
\left\{0,1\right\}
, using
private coins
. If
X^{A}_{i}\neq*
, then Bob sets
X^{B}_{i}=X^{A}_{i}
.

(c)

Alice sets
X^{A}_{i^{*}}=\hat{X}^{A}
and Bob sets
X^{B}_{i^{*}}=\hat{X}^{B}
.

(d)

For each
i^{*}<i\leq n
, using
public coins
, Alice and Bob jointly sample
X^{B}_{i}
