uniformly at random from
\left\{0,1\right\}
. Then Alice, using
private coins
, sets
X^{A}_{i^{*}}=X^{B}_{i^{*}}
with probability
1/2
, and sets
X^{A}_{i^{*}}=*
with probability
1/2
.

2.

Computing the answer.
Alice and Bob run the protocol
\pi_{\sf{Overwrite}}
on the instance
(X^{A},X^{B})
of
\sf{Overwrite}
to compute an answer
Z
, and return
\hat{Z}=Z
.

It is easy to see that the distribution on instances
(X^{A},X^{B})
for
\sf{Overwrite}
created in the reduction by the choice of pairs
(\hat{X}^{A},\hat{X}^{B})
is exactly the same as the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{Overwrite}}
.
Moreover, the protocol
\pi
returns a correct answer to the one-bit
\sf{Overwrite}
problem iff the protocol
\pi_{\sf Overwrite}
returns a correct answer to the
\sf{Overwrite}
problem. Since
\pi_{\sf Overwrite}
is a
\delta
-error one-way protocol to the
\sf{Overwrite}
problem for some
0<\delta<1/10
,
\pi
is a
\delta
-error one-way protocol to the one-bit
\sf{Overwrite}
problem for the same
\delta
.
Therefore, from
2.3
, if we denote by
\Pi
the random variable representing the transcript of
\pi
, then
I(\Pi;\hat{X}^{A}|\hat{X}^{B})=\Omega(1)
.

We denote by
\Pi_{\sf{Overwrite}}
the random variable representing the transcript of
\pi_{\sf{Overwrite}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\Omega(1)=I(\Pi;\hat{X}^{A}|\hat{X}^{B})

\displaystyle\leq I(\Pi_{\sf{Overwrite}};\hat{X}^{A}\mid i^{*},X^{A}_{1},X^{A}_{2},\dots,X^{A}_{i^{*}-1},X^{B}_{1},X^{B}_{2},\dots,X^{B}_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}I(\Pi_{\sf{Overwrite}};X^{A}_{i}\mid X^{A}_{1},X^{A}_{2},\dots,X^{A}_{i-1},X^{B})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\cdot I(\Pi_{\sf{Overwrite}};X^{A}|X^{B}),

where the first equality is from
2
in
1.1
the mutual independence between the random variables in
\left\{X^{A}_{t}\mid 1\leq t<i^{*}\right\}
and
\left\{X^{B}_{t}\mid 1\leq t\leq n\right\}
(note that
\Pi=\Pi_{\sf{Overwrite}}
,
\hat{X}_{B}=X^{B}_{i^{*}}
and the variables
i^{*}
,
\left\{X^{A}_{t}\right\}_{1\leq t<i^{*}}
and
\left\{X^{B}_{t}\right\}_{1\leq t\leq n,t\neq i^{*}}
play the role of
W
in
2
), the second equality follows from the fact that
i^{*}
is chosen uniformly at random from set
[n]
, and the last equality is from the chain rule (
1
in
1.1
).

From
Proposition

1.1
, we get that
\emph{\textnormal{{CC}}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\textnormal{\sf Overwrite}}}(\pi_{\sf{Overwrite}})\geq\emph{\textnormal{{IC}}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\textnormal{\sf Overwrite}}}(\pi_{\sf{Overwrite}})\geq\Omega(n)
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

2.1
.
∎

2.2.2
Completing the Proof of
Theorem

9

We now prove
Theorem

9
by reducing the problem
\sf{Overwrite}
to the problem
\textsf{MST}_{\textsf{apx}}
. First we define two metrics for constructing the hard distribution for the
\textsf{MST}_{\textsf{apx}}
problem.

Metrics
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
.

In order to define the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{MST}}
on pairs
(\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B})
of partial metrics, which serves as input to the
\textsf{MST}_{\textsf{apx}}
problem, we need to first define metrics
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
and certain groups of vertices.
We use the parameters
k,L,r
to be fixed later.
Let
p=(n-kr)/(2rk)
.
The set of vertices in the metric spaces
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
is
V^{\prime}=U\cup S\cup T
, where

•

U=\left\{u_{i,j}\mid i\in[k],j\in[r]\right\}
, so
|U|=kr
;

•

S=\left\{s_{i,j,\ell}\mid i\in[k],j\in[r],\ell\in[p]\right\}
, so
|S|=prk=(n-kr)/2
; and

•

T=\left\{t_{i,j,\ell}\mid i\in[k],j\in[r],\ell\in[p]\right\}
, so
|T|=prk=(n-kr)/2
.

We now define the distances between pairs of vertices of
V^{\prime}
in metrics
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
. In fact, the metrics
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
differ in only the distance between vertices of
U
.
Consider now a pair
(v,v^{\prime})
of vertices of
V^{\prime}
, we distinguish between the following cases.

•

If
v,v^{\prime}\in S
, and
v=s_{i,j,\ell}
and
v=s_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}
, then

w_{\sf Y}(v,v^{\prime})=w_{\sf N}(v,v^{\prime})=\min\left\{L,|(irp+\ell r+j)-(i^{\prime}rp+\ell^{\prime}r+j^{\prime})|\right\}
.

•

If
v,v^{\prime}\in T
, and
v=t_{i,j,\ell}
and
v=t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}
, then

w_{\sf Y}(v,v^{\prime})=w_{\sf N}(v,v^{\prime})=\min\left\{L,|(irp+jp+\ell)-(i^{\prime}rp+j^{\prime}q+\ell^{\prime})|\right\}
.

•

If
v\in S
and
v^{\prime}\in T
, then
w_{\sf Y}(v,v^{\prime})=w_{\sf N}(v,v^{\prime})=L
.

•

If
v\in S\cup T
and
v^{\prime}\in U
, then
w_{\sf Y}(v,v^{\prime})=w_{\sf N}(v,v^{\prime})=L
.

•

Assume
v=u_{i,j}\in U
and
v^{\prime}=u_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\in U
.
First,
w_{\sf N}(v,v^{\prime})=L
.
Then if
i=i^{\prime}
,
w_{\sf Y}(v,v^{\prime})=|j-j^{\prime}|
; if
i\neq i^{\prime}
,
w_{\sf Y}(v,v^{\prime})=L
.

This completes the definitions of
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
. See
Figure

1
for an illustration.
It is easy to verify that
{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf Y})=(n-1)+(L-1)(k+1)
and
{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf N})=(n-1)+(L-1)((k-1)r+1)
. Additionally, for each index
1\leq i\leq p
, we define the set

S_{\ell}=\left\{s_{i,j,\ell}\mid i\in[k],j\in[r]\right\}
and the set

T_{\ell}=\left\{t_{i,j,\ell}\mid i\in[k],j\in[r]\right\}
.
Sets
\left\{S_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in[p]}
,
\left\{T_{\ell}\right\}_{\ell\in[p]}
and
U
are called
groups
of
V^{\prime}
.

(a)

An illustration of
w_{\sf Y}
. For
v,v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
,
w_{\sf Y}(v,v^{\prime})=\min\left\{L,\text{ the distance between }v,v^{\prime}\text{ on the path}\right\}
.

(b)

An illustration of
w_{\sf N}
. For
v,v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
,
w_{\sf N}(v,v^{\prime})=\min\left\{L,\text{ the distance between }v,v^{\prime}\text{ on the path}\right\}
.

Figure 1:
An illustration of metrics
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
in the case
k=r=2
,
p=1
and
n=12
.

Before we define the hard distribution on input partial metric pairs to the problem
\textsf{MST}_{\textsf{apx}}
, we first describe the set
V
of vertices on which the partial metrics will be defined. Denote
q=2p+1
.
Set
V
contains
n
vertices, and is partitioned into
q

groups

V=V_{1}\cup\ldots\cup V_{q}
, such that
|V_{1}|=\cdots=|V_{q}|=kr
.
We say that an one-to-one mapping
f:V\to V^{\prime}
is
group-preserving
, iff
f
maps an entire group of
V
to an entire group of
V^{\prime}
.

Distribution

\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{MST}}(k,L,r)
: An distribution on pairs
(\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B})
of partial metrics on
V
:

1.

Choose a random one-to-one group-preserving mapping
f:V\to V^{\prime}
.

2.

Choose
Z\in\left\{\sf Y,\sf N\right\}
uniformly at random, and then define metric
w
as: for every pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V
,
w(v,v^{\prime})=w_{Z}(f(v),f(v^{\prime}))
.

3.

Choose
i^{*}
from
[p]
uniformly at random. Then with probability
1/2
, set
R^{i^{*}}_{A}=\emptyset
and with probability
1/2
, set
R^{i^{*}}_{A}=f^{-1}(U)\times f^{-1}(U)
.

4.

For each
1\leq i\leq p
,
i\neq i^{*}
, choose
z_{i}
from
\left\{\mathbb{S},\mathbb{T}\right\}
uniformly at random. If
z_{i}=\mathbb{S}
, then define
R^{i}_{A}=f^{-1}(S_{i})\times f^{-1}(S_{i})
as a subset of
V\times V
; if
z_{i}=\mathbb{T}
, then define
R^{i}_{A}=f^{-1}(T_{i})\times f^{-1}(T_{i})
as a subset of
V\times V
.

5.

Define
\bar{w}_{A}
as the restriction of metric
w
on set
R_{A}=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq p}R^{i}_{A}
of pairs.

6.

Define
\bar{w}_{B}
as the restriction of
w
on the set
R_{B}=(V\times V)\setminus(f^{-1}(U)\times f^{-1}(U))
of pairs.

Let
\alpha>1
and
0<\delta<1/10
be any constants.
Let
\pi_{\textsf{MST}}
be a protocol that
(\alpha,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
over the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{MST}}(k,L,r)
.
We construct the following protocol
\pi_{\textsf{Overwrite}}
for the problem
\sf{Overwrite}
using the protocol
\pi_{\textsf{MST}}
.

Protocol

\pi_{\textsf{Overwrite}}
: A protocol for
\sf{Overwrite}
using a protocol
\pi_{\textsf{MST}}
for
\textsf{MST}_{\textsf{apx}}
.

Input:
An instance
(X^{A},X^{B})\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf Overwrite}
.

Output:

Z\in\left\{0,1\right\}
as the answer to
\sf{Overwrite}
.

1.

Sampling the instance.
Alice and Bob create an instance
(\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B})
of
\textsf{MST}_{\textsf{apx}}
as follows.

(a)

Set parameters
k=r=\left\lceil 2\alpha\right\rceil
and
L=n/2\alpha
.

(b)

Define the partial metric
\bar{w}_{A}
as follows: for each
t\in[p]
, if
X^{A}_{t}=1
, then Alice sets the restricted metric on group
V_{i}
to be the metric on
S_{1}
; if
X^{A}_{t}=0
, then Alice sets the restricted metric on group
V_{t}
to be the metric on
T_{1}
; if
X^{A}_{t}=*
then Alice does not modify the restricted metric on group
V_{t}
(which remains unknown to Alice).

(c)

Let
i^{*}
be the index given to Bob. Bob first finds an one-to-one group-preserving mapping
f:V\to V^{\prime}
, such that (i)
f(V_{i^{*}})=U
; and (ii) for each
t\in[q],t\neq i^{*}
, if
X^{B}_{t}=1
, then group
V_{t}
is mapped to some group of
\left\{S_{i}\right\}_{i\in[p]}
; and if
X^{B}_{t}=0
, then group
V_{t}
is mapped to some group of
\left\{T_{i}\right\}_{i\in[p]}
.

(d)

Define the partial metric
\bar{w}_{B}
as follows: for each pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V
such that at least one of
v,v^{\prime}
does not belong to group
V_{i^{*}}
,
\bar{w}_{B}(v,v^{\prime})=w_{\sf Y}(f(v),f(v^{\prime}))=w_{\sf N}(f(v),f(v^{\prime}))
.

2.

Computing the answer.
Alice and Bob run the protocol
\pi_{\textsf{MST}}
on
(\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B})
to compute an estimate
Y
. If
Y\leq\alpha\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf Y})
, then they return
Z=1
; otherwise they return
Z=0
.

Claim 2.4
.

If the protocol
\pi_{\sf MST}

(\alpha,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
over
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textnormal{MST}}(k,L,r)
, then the protocol
\pi_{\sf{Overwrite}}
is a
\delta
-error protocol for the problem
\sf{Overwrite}
over
{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf Overwrite}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that the distribution of instances
(\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B})
for
\textsf{MST}_{\textsf{apx}}
created in the reduction by the instance
(X^{A},X^{B})\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf Overwrite}
, is exactly the same as the distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{MST}}(\left\lceil 2\alpha\right\rceil,n/2\alpha,\left\lceil 2\alpha\right\rceil)
. Moreover, if
X_{i^{*}}=1
, then
w=w_{\sf Y}
, otherwise
w=w_{\sf N}
.
Recall that
{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf Y})=(n-1)+(L-1)(k+1)
and
{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf N})=(n-1)+(L-1)((k-1)r+1)
. Therefore, if we set
k=r=\alpha
and
L=n/\alpha
, then

\frac{{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf N})}{{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf Y})}=\frac{(n-1)+((n/2\alpha)-1)((\left\lceil 2\alpha\right\rceil-1)\cdot\left\lceil 2\alpha\right\rceil+1)}{(n-1)+((n/2\alpha)-1)(\left\lceil 2\alpha\right\rceil+1)}>\alpha,

and it follows that any
\alpha
-approximation of
{\mathsf{MST}}(w)
can determine the value of
{\mathsf{MST}}(w)
(to be either
{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf N})
or
{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf Y})
).
Therefore, if the protocol
\pi_{\sf MST}

(\alpha,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textnormal{MST}}(k,L,r)
, then the protocol
\pi_{\sf{Overwrite}}
is an
\delta
-error protocol for the problem
\sf{Overwrite}
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf Overwrite}
.
∎

Combining
2.4
and
Lemma

2.1
, we derive that, for any
\alpha>1
and any
0<\delta<1
, if a protocol
\pi

(\alpha,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
over
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textnormal{MST}}(k,L,r)
, then

\textnormal{{CC}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{\textnormal{MST}}(k,L,r)}(\pi)=\Omega(p)=\Omega\left(\frac{n-\left\lceil 2\alpha\right\rceil^{2}}{2\cdot\left\lceil 2\alpha\right\rceil^{2}}\right)=\Omega(n/\alpha^{2}).

This completes the proof of
Theorem

9
.

2.3
Discussion of the Lower Bound in
Theorem

9

In this subsection we show that
the exponent
2
in the lower bound
\Omega(n/\alpha^{2})
in
Theorem

9
is the best constant we can hope for.

Theorem 10
.

There is a deterministic one-way protocol that returns an
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
-approximation to the problem
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
with communication complexity
\tilde{O}(1)
.

In particular, if we set
\alpha=\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
, then the lower bound in
Theorem

9
shows that any protocol that returns an
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
-approximation must have communication complexity
\tilde{\Omega}(1)=\tilde{\Omega}(n/\alpha^{2})
.

Recall that in the communication game, Alice is given a partial metric
\bar{w}_{A}
and Bob is given another partial metric
\bar{w}_{B}
, such that
\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B}
are complementary.
The protocol is simple and is decribed below.

Protocol.

Alice computes
Z_{A}=\min\left\{{\mathsf{MST}}(\hat{w})\mid\hat{w}\text{ is a completion of }\bar{w}_{A}\right\}
, and Bob computes
Z_{B}=\min\left\{{\mathsf{MST}}(\hat{w})\mid\hat{w}\text{ is a completion of }\bar{w}_{B}\right\}
. Alice sends
Z_{A}
to Bob and then Bob outputs
\max\left\{Z_{A},Z_{B}\right\}
as the estimate of
{\mathsf{MST}}(w)
.

It is clear that the protocol is deterministic and has communication complexity
\tilde{O}(1)
. We now show that it indeed returns an
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
-approximation to the problem
\emph{\sf MST}_{\sf apx}
.

Proof of Correctness.

On the one hand, by definition,
w
is a completion of
\bar{w}_{A}
, so
{\mathsf{MST}}(w)\geq Z_{A}
, and similarly
{\mathsf{MST}}(w)\geq Z_{B}
, so
{\mathsf{MST}}(w)\geq\max\left\{Z_{A},Z_{B}\right\}
. It now remains to show that
{\mathsf{MST}}(w)\leq\tilde{O}(\sqrt{n})\cdot\max\left\{Z_{A},Z_{B}\right\}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

If
w(x,y)=1
for all pairs
x,y
of vertices of
V
, then
\max\left\{Z_{A},Z_{B}\right\}\geq\Omega(\sqrt{n})
.

Proof.

Let
w_{A}
be the complete metric that, over all completion
\hat{w}
of
\bar{w}_{A}
, minimizes
{\mathsf{MST}}(\hat{w})
.
If
Z_{A}={\mathsf{MST}}(w_{A})\geq\sqrt{n}
, then
\max\left\{Z_{A},Z_{B}\right\}\geq Z_{A}\geq\sqrt{n}/2
.
We will show that, if
Z_{A}={\mathsf{MST}}(w_{A})<\sqrt{n}
, then there is a set
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
of at least
\sqrt{n}/2
vertices, such that for each pair
x,y\in V^{\prime}
,
w_{A}(x,y)<1
. Note that this implies that, for each pair
x,y\in V^{\prime}
,
\bar{w}_{A}(x,y)\neq 1
, and therefore
\bar{w}_{B}(x,y)=1
since the partial metrics
\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B}
are complementary.
It follows that
Z_{B}\geq|V^{\prime}|/2=\Omega(\sqrt{n})
.

Let
T
be a minimum spanning tree of graph
G_{w_{A}}
, the complete graph with weights
w_{A}
on its edges, so
w(E(T))\leq\sqrt{n}
.
Let
T
be rooted at an arbitrary vertex
r
.
We now iteratively partition vertices of
V
into disjoint clusters as follows. Throughout, we maintain (i) a tree
\hat{T}
, that is initialized to be
T
; and (ii) a collection
{\mathcal{S}}
of clusters of
V
, that initially contains no clusters.
We will ensure that, over the course of the algorithm,
\hat{T}
is either an empty graph or a connected subtree of
T
that contains the root
r
.
We now describe an iteration.
Let
v
be the leaf of
\hat{T}
that, over all leaves of
\hat{T}
, maximizes its tree-distance to root
r
in
\hat{T}
. Let
e_{v}
be the unique incident edge of
v
.
If
w_{A}(e_{v})\geq 1/2
, then we simply (i) add a single-vertex cluster
\left\{v\right\}
to
{\mathcal{S}}
; (ii) delete vertex
v
and edge
e_{v}
from
\hat{T}
, and then continue to the next iteration.
If
w_{A}(e_{v})<1/2
, then we let
S_{v}=\left\{v^{\prime}\in V(\hat{T})\mid w_{A}(v,v^{\prime})<1/2\right\}
, and then (i) add the cluster
S_{v}
to
{\mathcal{S}}
; (ii) delete vertices of
S_{v}
and their incident edges from
\hat{T}
, and then continue to the next iteration.
It is easy to see that, after each iteration, the total weight (under
w_{A}
) of all edges of
\hat{T}
is decreased by at least
1/2
, and the diameter of each cluster added into
{\mathcal{S}}
is less than
1
. Therefore, the algorithm terminates in at most
2\sqrt{n}
iterations, so
{\mathcal{S}}
contains at most
2\sqrt{n}
clusters whose union is
V
, and the diameter of each cluster is less than
1
. Let
V^{\prime}
be the cluster of
{\mathcal{S}}
with maximum cardinality. It is easy to verify that
V^{\prime}
satisfies all desired conditions.
∎

Let
G_{w}
be the weighted complete graph on
V
, where for each pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V
, the weight on edge
(v,v^{\prime})
is
w(v,v^{\prime})
. For each
1\leq i\leq\log n
, we let
G^{i}_{w}
be the graph obtained from
G_{w}
by deleting all edges with weight greater than
2^{i}
, and we denote by
c_{i}
the number of connected components in graph
G^{i}_{w}
. We use the following claims.

Claim 2.5
.

{\mathsf{MST}}(w)\leq\sum_{i}c_{i}\cdot 2^{i}
.

Proof.

We define function
w^{+}
as follows. For each pair
x,y\in V
, let
i
be the unique integer such that
2^{i-1}<w(x,y)\leq 2^{i}
, and we define
w^{+}(x,y)=2^{i}
. Although
w^{+}
may no longer be a metric,
{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{+})
is well-defined and it is clear that
{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{+})\geq{\mathsf{MST}}(w)
.
We define graph
G_{w^{+}}
and graphs
\left\{G^{i}_{w^{+}}\right\}_{i}
similarly. Clearly, for each
i
, graph
G^{i}_{w^{+}}
contains the same set of edges as
G^{i}_{w}
. Therefore, the number of connected components in
G^{i}_{w^{+}}
is
c_{i}
, and it follows that any minimum spanning tree of
G_{w^{+}}
must contain, for each
i
, at least
c_{i}
edges of weight at least
2^{i+1}
. Therefore,

{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{+})={\mathsf{MST}}(G_{w^{+}})\geq\sum_{i}c_{i}\cdot(2^{i+1}-2^{i})=\sum_{i}c_{i}\cdot 2^{i}.

This completes the proof of
2.5
.
∎

Claim 2.6
.

For each
1\leq i\leq\log n
,
\max\left\{Z_{A},Z_{B}\right\}\geq\sqrt{c_{i}}\cdot 2^{i}
.

Proof.

For a partial metric
\bar{w}
, we define its
truncated metric at level-
i
to be the metric
\bar{w}^{(i)}
, where for every pair
x,y\in V
, if
\bar{w}(x,y)\neq*
, then
\bar{w}^{(i)}(x,y)=\min\left\{2^{i},\bar{w}(x,y)\right\}
; otherwise
\bar{w}^{(i)}(x,y)=*
. The truncated metric at level-
i
of a complete metric is defined similarly.
It is easy to see that a truncated metric of a complete metric is still a valid complete metric (that is, satisfying all triangle inequalities).

Fix now an integer
1\leq i\leq\log n

and consider the truncated metrics
w^{(i)}
,
\bar{w}_{A}^{(i)}
and
\bar{w}_{B}^{(i)}
.
We denote
Z^{(i)}_{A}=\min\left\{\hat{w}\mid\hat{w}\text{ is a completion of }\bar{w}^{(i)}_{A}\right\}
.
Let
\tilde{w}_{A}
be the completion of partial metric
\bar{w}_{A}
that minimizes
{\mathsf{MST}}(\tilde{w}_{A})
.
It is easy to see that the truncated metric
\tilde{w}^{(i)}_{A}
of
\tilde{w}_{A}
is a completion of
\bar{w}^{(i)}_{A}
, and
{\mathsf{MST}}(\tilde{w}^{(i)}_{A})\leq{\mathsf{MST}}(\tilde{w}_{A})
. Altogether, we get that
Z^{(i)}_{A}\leq Z_{A}
. Similarly, if we denote by
Z^{(i)}_{B}=\min\left\{\hat{w}\mid\hat{w}\text{ is a completion of }\bar{w}^{(i)}_{B}\right\}
, then
Z^{(i)}_{B}\leq Z_{B}
.
So
\max\left\{Z_{A},Z_{B}\right\}\geq\max\left\{Z^{(i)}_{A},Z^{(i)}_{B}\right\}
.

We now show that
\max\left\{Z^{(i)}_{A},Z^{(i)}_{B}\right\}\geq 2^{i}\cdot\sqrt{c_{i}}
, thus completing the proof of
2.6
. Recall that the graph
G^{i}_{w}
is obtained from graph
G_{w}
by deleting all edges of weight greater than
2^{i}
. Recall that
G^{i}_{w}
contains
c^{i}
connected component. Let
V^{\prime}=\left\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{c_{i}}\right\}
be a set of
c_{i}
vertices from different connected components of
G^{i}_{w}
. We define a partial metric
\bar{w}^{\prime}
as follows: for each pair
v_{p},v_{q}\in V^{\prime}
,
\bar{w}^{\prime}(v_{p},v_{q})=2^{i}
; for any other pair
(x,y)
,
\bar{w}^{\prime}(x,y)=*
.
We define two other partial metrics
\bar{w}^{\prime}_{A},\bar{w}^{\prime}_{B}
as follows. For a pair
v_{p},v_{q}\in V^{\prime}
, if
\bar{w}_{A}(v_{p},v_{q})\neq*
, then
\bar{w}^{\prime}_{A}(v_{p},v_{q})=2^{i}
, otherwise
\bar{w}^{\prime}_{A}(v_{p},v_{q})=*
. The metric
\bar{w}^{\prime}_{B}
is defined similarly. Clearly, partial metrics
\bar{w}^{\prime}_{A},\bar{w}^{\prime}_{B}
are restrictions of
\bar{w}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
\bar{w}^{\prime}_{A}
is a restriction of
\bar{w}^{(i)}_{A}
, and
\bar{w}^{\prime}_{B}
is a restriction of
\bar{w}^{(i)}_{B}
.
Therefore, if we denote
Z^{\prime}_{A}=\min\left\{\hat{w}\mid\hat{w}\text{ is a completion of }\bar{w}^{\prime}_{A}\right\}
(and define
Z^{\prime}_{B}
similarly), then

Z^{\prime}_{A}\leq Z^{(i)}_{A}
(and similarly
Z^{\prime}_{B}\leq Z^{(i)}_{B}
).
Note that, from
Lemma

2.2
,
\max\left\{Z^{\prime}_{A},Z^{\prime}_{B}\right\}\geq\Omega(\sqrt{c_{i}}\cdot 2^{i})
, so
\max\left\{Z^{(i)}_{A},Z^{(i)}_{B}\right\}\geq\Omega(\sqrt{c_{i}}\cdot 2^{i})
.
∎

From
2.5
and
2.6
, we get that

{\mathsf{MST}}(w)\leq\sum_{1\leq i\leq\log n}c_{i}\cdot 2^{i}\leq\sum_{1\leq i\leq\log n}\sqrt{n\cdot c_{i}}\cdot 2^{i}=\sqrt{n}\cdot\bigg(\sum_{1\leq i\leq\log n}\sqrt{c_{i}}\cdot 2^{i}\bigg)\leq O(\sqrt{n}\log n)\cdot\max\left\{Z_{A},Z_{B}\right\}.

3
Space Lower Bound for Multi-Pass MST Estimation in Metric Streams

In this section, we prove a space lower bound for multi-pass streaming algorithms for MST estimation in metric streams. We will show that, for any
\alpha>1
and
p\geq 1
, any randomized
p
-pass
\alpha
-approximation streaming algorithm for metric MST estimation requires
\tilde{\Omega}(\sqrt{n/\alpha p^{2}})
space, thus establishing
Theorem

4
.
At a high level, our proof first analyzes a multi-player communication game for MST estimation in the blackboard model, and then shows that the lower bound of the communication complexity of this problem implies the space lower bound on multi-pass streaming algorithms.

The
k
-player metric-MST-cost approximation problem (
\emph{\sf MST}^{k}_{\sf apx}
):

This is a communication problem between players
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
, in which each player
P_{i}
is given a partial metric
\bar{w}_{i}
on some common vertex set
V
known to all players. The goal of the players is to compute an estimate
Y
of
{\mathsf{MST}}(w)
, if (i) there exists some complete metric
w
on
V
, such that each
\bar{w}_{i}
is the restriction of
w
onto some subset
R_{i}\subseteq V\times V
of pairs; and (ii)
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}R_{i}=V\times V
.
If either (i) or (ii) does not hold, then any output by the algorithm will be viewed as a correct output.

In order to analyze the communication complexity of any randomized protocol that outputs an
\alpha
-approximation of the problem, we first define metrics
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
as follows. The vertex set
V
contains
n
vertices, and is partitioned into
m
groups of size
N=\left\lfloor\sqrt{3\alpha n}\right\rfloor
each, so
m=\Omega(\sqrt{n/3\alpha})
.
We denote one of the groups by
V^{*}
, and call it the
special group
. All other groups are called
regular groups
.
We let
M
be any parameter such that
M\gg n
.
In both metrics
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
, the distance between every pair of vertices that come from different groups is
M
. In
w_{\sf Y}
, the distance between every pair of vertices that come from the same group is
1
. In
w_{\sf N}
, the distance between every pair of vertices that come from the same regular group is
1
, while the distance between every pair of vertices from the special group is
M
. See
Figure

2
for an illustration. It is easy to verify that
{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf Y})\leq 2mM
and
{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf N})\geq MN
, so
{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf N})>\alpha\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w_{\sf Y})
. This means any protocol that returns an
\alpha
-approximation of the MST cost can indeed distinguish between the two metrics
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
.

(a)

Metric
w_{\sf Y}
(or graph
G_{\sf Y}
).

(b)

Metric
w_{\sf N}
(or graph
G_{\sf N}
).

Figure 2:
An illustration of
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
: pairs of vertices with distance
1
are connected by edges.

We define
G_{\sf Y}
to be the (unweighted) graph on the set
V
of vertices in which there is an edge between every pair of vertices that come from the same group, and define
G_{\sf N}
to be the graph on
V
in which there is an edge between every pair of vertices that come from the same regular group.
So the problem of determining whether a graph is
G_{\sf Y}
or
G_{\sf N}
is essentially determining whether the graph consists of several equal-sized cliques or several equal-sized cliques plus an independent set of the same size.
It is easy to see that, the problem of distinguishing between metrics
w_{\sf Y}
and
w_{\sf N}
in the
\emph{\sf MST}^{k}_{\sf apx}
problem is equivalent to the following graph-theoretic communication problem.

Clique or independent set problem (
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
):

This is a communication game between
k
players, in which each player
P_{i}
is given two subsets
R_{i},R^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq V\times V
with
R^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq R_{i}
. Intuitively, the subset
R_{i}
represents the set of all pairs of vertices whose edge/non-edge information is given to the player
P_{i}
, and the subset
R^{\prime}_{i}
represents the set of all pairs in
R_{i}
that has an edge connecting them. The goal of the players is to decide whether the graph
G=(V,E)
where
E=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}R^{\prime}_{i}
is
G_{\sf Y}
or
G_{\sf N}
, if (i)
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}R_{i}=V\times V
; and (ii) the information received by the players is consistent, i.e., there does not exists a pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V
and a pair
i,j\in[k]
, such that
(v,v^{\prime})\in R_{i}\cap R_{j}
and
(v,v^{\prime})\in R^{\prime}_{i}\setminus R^{\prime}_{j}
.
If either (i) or (ii) does not hold, then any output of the protocol will be viewed as a correct output.

The main result of this section is the following theorem, which immediately implies that the communication complexity of any randomized protocol that returns an
\alpha
-approximation of problem
\emph{\sf MST}^{k}_{\sf apx}
has communication complexity at least
\Omega(\sqrt{n/\alpha})
, which in turn implies
Theorem

4
.

Theorem 11
.

For any integers
m
and
N
, any randomized protocol
\pi
for the
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
problem in the blackboard model with
k=\left\lfloor 2\log N\right\rfloor
players has communication complexity
\Omega(m/\log^{3}N)
.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

11
.
As a warm up, we first consider the special case where
N=2
, namely the
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,m}
problem in the 2-player blackboard model (note that in this case
k=2=2\log N
). In this case, graph
G_{\sf Y}
is a perfect matching and graph
G_{\sf N}
is a matching of size
m-1
. We show in
Section

3.1
that any randomized protocol for this problem has communication complexity
\Omega(m)
. We then generalize the ideas used in
Section

3.1
to the general case, and complete the proof of
Theorem

11
in
Section

3.2
.

3.1
Communication Lower Bound for Bipartite Matching

In this section, we consider the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2m}
(we use
2m
instead of
m
for convenience) in the 2-player blackboard model, and
show that any randomized protocol for this problem has communication complexity
\Omega(m)
.
At a high level, we first show that the information cost of any randomized protocol for
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
problem is
\Omega(1)
, and then use a direct sum type argument to prove that the information complexity and hence the communication complexity of the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2m}
is
\Omega(m)
.

We first construct an input distribution
\nu
for the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
as follows.
We denote the vertex set by
U\cup V
where
U=\left\{u_{1},u_{2}\right\}
and
V=\left\{v_{1},v_{2}\right\}
.
Recall that
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
is a 2-player game.
Alice’s three possible inputs are:

•

A_{1}
:
R_{A}=(U\cup V)\times(U\cup V)
, and
R^{\prime}_{A}=\left\{(u_{1},v_{2}),(u_{2},v_{1})\right\}
;

•

A_{2}
:
R_{A}=(U\cup V)\times(U\cup V)
, and
R^{\prime}_{A}=\left\{(u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2})\right\}
;

•

A_{3}
:
R_{A}=(U\cup V)\times(U\cup V)\setminus\left\{(u_{2},v_{2})\right\}
, and
R^{\prime}_{A}=\left\{(u_{1},v_{2})\right\}
.

Bob’s three possible inputs are:

•

B_{1}
:
R_{B}=\left\{(u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2}),(u_{2},v_{2})\right\}
, and
R^{\prime}_{B}=\left\{(u_{2},v_{2})\right\}
;

•

B_{2}
:
R_{B}=\left\{(u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2})\right\}
, and
R^{\prime}_{B}=\emptyset
;

•

B_{3}
:
R_{B}=\left\{(u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2}),(u_{2},v_{2})\right\}
, and
R^{\prime}_{B}=\emptyset
.

Figure 3:
An illustration of the input pairs in distribution
\nu
for the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
. The red and blue lines represent edge/non-edge information in Alice’s and Bob’s inputs, respectively. Solid lines represent edges (pairs in
R^{\prime}_{A},R^{\prime}_{B}
), and dashed lines represent non-edges (pairs in
R_{A}\setminus R^{\prime}_{A},R_{B}\setminus R^{\prime}_{B}
). Note that the pairs
(u_{1},u_{2})
and
(v_{1},v_{2})
are given to both Alice and Bob in all inputs as non-edges, and so they are omitted in the figure for simplicity.
The green line in the cell
(A_{2},B_{3})
indicates that the information regarding pair
(u_{2},v_{2})
is not given to either Alice or Bob (and thus, this input pair is invalid). The green cells represent valid inputs where the graph
G=G_{\sf Y}
, the blue cell represents a valid input where the graph
G=G_{\sf N}
, and the red cells represent invalid inputs.

See
Figure

3
for an illustration of the input pairs. We define the distribution
\nu
on the input pairs to be the uniform distribution on the set
\left\{(A_{1},B_{2}),(A_{1},B_{3}),(A_{2},B_{1}),(A_{2},B_{2}),(A_{3},B_{1})\right\}
. We prove the following claim.

Claim 3.1
.

For any randomized protocol
\pi^{\prime}
for the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
,
\textnormal{{IC}}_{\nu}(\pi^{\prime})=\Omega(1)
.

Proof.

For each
i\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
, we let
X^{A}=i
iff Alice’s input is
A_{i}
. We define the variable
X^{B}
similarly.
For any pair
i,j\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
, we denote by
\Pi^{\prime}_{ij}
the random variable representing the transcript of
\pi^{\prime}
when Alice’s input is
A_{i}
and Bob’s input is
B_{j}
(namely
X^{A}=i
and
X^{B}=j
).
We will show that
\textnormal{{IC}}_{\nu}(\pi^{\prime})\geq 10^{-3}
. Assume for contradiction that this does not hold. Then

\displaystyle 10^{-3}

\displaystyle>\textnormal{{IC}}_{\nu}(\pi^{\prime})

\displaystyle=I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X^{A}|X^{B})+I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X^{B}|X^{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{5}\cdot\bigg(I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X^{A}|X^{B}=1)+I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X^{A}|X^{B}=2)+I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X^{B}|X^{A}=1)+I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X^{B}|X^{A}=2)\bigg),

where the last equality follows from the definition of distribution
\nu
(note that
\Pr[X^{B}=3]=1/5
and
\Pr[X^{B}=1]=\Pr[X^{B}=2]=2/5
, and if
X^{B}=3
, then
X^{A}=1
with probability
1
).

Note that, from
1.5
,

I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X^{A}|X^{B}=1)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}\bigg(\Pi_{21}\text{ }\bigg|\bigg|\text{ }\frac{\Pi_{21}+\Pi_{31}}{2}\bigg)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}\bigg(\Pi_{31}\text{ }\bigg|\bigg|\text{ }\frac{\Pi_{21}+\Pi_{31}}{2}\bigg)\geq h^{2}(\Pi_{21},\Pi_{31}).

Therefore,
h^{2}(\Pi_{21},\Pi_{31})<(5/2)\cdot 10^{-3}
, and so
h(\Pi_{21},\Pi_{31})<1/20
. Similarly, we get that
h(\Pi_{21},\Pi_{22})<1/20
. Then from
1.2
,
h(\Pi_{31},\Pi_{22})\leq h(\Pi_{31},\Pi_{21})+h(\Pi_{21},\Pi_{22})<0.1
, and then from
Proposition

1.2
, we get that
h(\Pi_{21},\Pi_{32})<0.1
. From
1.2
,
h(\Pi_{22},\Pi_{32})\leq h(\Pi_{22},\Pi_{21})+h(\Pi_{21},\Pi_{32})<0.15
. Similarly,
h(\Pi_{22},\Pi_{23})<0.15
, so
h(\Pi_{23},\Pi_{32})<0.3
. Again by
Proposition

1.2
,
h(\Pi_{22},\Pi_{33})<0.3
. Lastly, from
1.4
, we get that
\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(\Pi_{22},\Pi_{33})<\frac{3\sqrt{2}}{10}<0.425
.

However, this means that, with probability at least
2\cdot(0.5-0.425)=0.15
, the transcript of
\pi^{\prime}
on the input pair
(A_{2},B_{2})
and the transcript of
\pi^{\prime}
on the input pair
(A_{3},B_{3})
are identical, and thus the output in these two cases are identical. However, when the input pair is
(A_{2},B_{2})
, the graph
G
is
G_{\sf Y}
, while if the input pair is
(A_{3},B_{3})
, the graph
G
is
G_{\sf N}
.
This contradicts the fact that
\pi^{\prime}
is a randomized protocol for the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
(as it needs to succeed with probability at least
99/100
).
∎

We now show a communication complexity lower bound for the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2m}
via a reduction from problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
using a standard direct sum type argument. We first construct an input distribution for
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2m}
as follows.

We denote by
U\cup V
the vertex set, where
|U|=|V|=2m
. The set
U
is further partitioned into
m
subsets:
U=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq m}U^{i}
, where for each
1\leq i\leq m
,
U^{i}=\left\{u^{i}_{1},u^{i}_{2}\right\}
. Similarly, the set
V
is further partitioned into
m
subsets:
V=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq m}V^{i}
, where for each
1\leq i\leq m
,
V^{i}=\left\{v^{i}_{1},v^{i}_{2}\right\}
. From the setting of the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2m}
, the graph
G_{\sf Y}
is a perfect matching between
U
and
V
, and the graph
G_{\sf N}
is a size-
(2m-1)
matching between
U
and
V
.

The non-edge information for every pair in
(U\times U)\cup(V\times V)
is always given to both Alice and Bob.
For each pair
i,i^{\prime}\in[m]
with
i\neq i^{\prime}
and for every pair
(u,v)
of vertices with
u\in U^{i}
and
v\in V^{i^{\prime}}
, the non-edge information is also given to both Alice and Bob.
The distribution of the input edge/non-edge information between each
(U^{i},V^{i})
-pair is identical to
\nu
(with
u^{i}_{1}
viewed as
u_{1}
,
u^{i}_{2}
viewed as
u_{2}
, etc), and the inputs between different pairs
(U^{i},V^{i})
,
(U^{i^{\prime}},V^{i^{\prime}})
are independent. This completes the definition of the input distribution, and we denote this distribution by
\mu
.

We prove the following claim.

Claim 3.2
.

For any randomized protocol
\pi
for the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,m}
,
\textnormal{{CC}}_{\mu}(\pi)=\Omega(m)
.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be any randomized protocol for the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,m}
.
We use
\pi
to construct a randomized protocol
\pi^{\prime}
for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
as follows.

Protocol

\pi^{\prime}
: A protocol for
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
using a protocol
\pi
for
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,m}
.

Input:
An instance
\left\{(R_{A},R^{\prime}_{A}),(R_{B},R^{\prime}_{B})\right\}
of problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
.

Output:
A label
\sf Y
/
\sf N
indicating whether
G
is the graph
G_{\sf Y}
in problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
(a perfect matching on
4
vertices) or
G
is the graph
G_{\sf N}
in problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
(
G
only contains a single edge).

1.

Alice and Bob create an instance
\left\{(\hat{R}_{A},\hat{R}^{\prime}_{A}),(\hat{R}_{B},\hat{R}^{\prime}_{B})\right\}
of problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,m}
as follows:

(a)

Alice and Bob both add all pairs
(u,u^{\prime})
with
u,u^{\prime}\in U
and all pairs
(v,v^{\prime})
with
v,v^{\prime}\in V
to
\hat{R}_{A}
and
\hat{R}_{B}
but not to
\hat{R}^{\prime}_{A}
or
\hat{R}^{\prime}_{B}
. Similarly, for every pair
u,v
with
u\in U^{i}
and
v\in V^{i^{\prime}}
such that
i\neq i^{\prime}
, add the pair
(u,v)
to
\hat{R}_{A}
and
\hat{R}_{B}
but not to
\hat{R}^{\prime}_{A}
or
\hat{R}^{\prime}_{B}
.

(b)

Alice and Bob use
public coins
to sample an index
i^{*}\in[m]
.

(c)

For each
1\leq i<i^{*}
, Alice and Bob generate an instance
\left\{(R_{A}(i),R^{\prime}_{A}(i)),(R_{B}(i),R^{\prime}_{B}(i))\right\}
of
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
as follows: they first use
public coins
to generate Alice’s input
(R_{A}(i),R^{\prime}_{A}(i))
according to
\nu
, and then Bob uses
private coins
to generate his own input
(R_{B}(i),R^{\prime}_{B}(i))
according to
\nu
conditioned on Alice’s input.

(d)

For each
i^{*}<i\leq m
, Alice and Bob generate an instance
\left\{(R_{A}(i),R^{\prime}_{A}(i)),(R_{B}(i),R^{\prime}_{B}(i))\right\}
of
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
as follows: they first use
public coins
to generate Bob’s input
(R_{B}(i),R^{\prime}_{B}(i))
according to
\nu
, and then Alice uses
private coins
to generate her own input
(R_{A}(i),R^{\prime}_{A}(i))
according to
\nu
conditioned on Bob’s input.

(e)

Alice sets
(R_{A}(i^{*}),R^{\prime}_{A}(i^{*}))=(R_{A},R^{\prime}_{A})
, and Bob sets
(R_{B}(i^{*}),R^{\prime}_{B}(i^{*}))=(R_{B},R^{\prime}_{B})
.

(f)

Alice adds all pairs in
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq m}R_{A}(i)
to
\hat{R}_{A}
, adds all pairs in
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq m}R^{\prime}_{A}(i)
to
\hat{R}^{\prime}_{A}
; and Bob adds all pairs in
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq m}R_{B}(i)
to
\hat{R}_{B}
, adds all pairs in
\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq m}R^{\prime}_{B}(i)
to
\hat{R}^{\prime}_{B}
.

2.

Alice and Bob then run protocol
\pi
on the instance
\left\{(\hat{R}_{A},\hat{R}^{\prime}_{A}),(\hat{R}_{B},\hat{R}^{\prime}_{B})\right\}
of problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,m}
, and return the output of
\pi
.

Since for each
i\neq i^{*}
, the information pair
(R_{A}(i),R^{\prime}_{A}(i)),(R_{B}(i),R^{\prime}_{B}(i))
is generated according to
\nu
.
If we denote by
G
the graph for the input instance
(R_{A},R^{\prime}_{A}),(R_{B},R^{\prime}_{B})
of problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
, and denote by
\hat{G}
the graph for the constructed instance
(\hat{R}_{A},\hat{R}^{\prime}_{A}),(\hat{R}_{B},\hat{R}^{\prime}_{B})
of problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,m}
, then
G=G_{\sf Y}
iff graph
\hat{G}
is a perfect matching. Therefore, the output of
\pi^{\prime}
is correct iff the output of
\pi
is correct. On the other hand, it is easy to see that the distribution of the constructed instance
(\hat{R}_{A},\hat{R}^{\prime}_{A}),(\hat{R}_{B},\hat{R}^{\prime}_{B})
is identical to
\mu
.

For each
j\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
, we let
X^{A}=j
iff Alice’s input
(R_{A},R^{\prime}_{A})
for the
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
problem is
A_{j}
, and we define the variable
X^{B}
similarly.
For each
1\leq i\leq m
and for each
j\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
, we let
\hat{X}^{A}_{i}=j
iff the pair
(R_{A}(i),R^{\prime}_{A}(i))
constructed in the protocol is
A_{j}
, and we define variable
\hat{X}^{B}_{i}
similarly.
Lastly, we denote
\hat{X}_{A}=(\hat{X}^{A}_{1},\ldots,\hat{X}^{A}_{m})
and
\hat{X}_{B}=(\hat{X}^{B}_{1},\ldots,\hat{X}^{B}_{m})
.
Then, from
3.1
,

\displaystyle\Omega(1)=I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X^{B}|X^{A})

\displaystyle\leq I_{\mu}(\Pi;X^{B}\mid i^{*},\hat{X}^{A}_{1},\ldots,\hat{X}^{A}_{m},\hat{X}^{B}_{1},\ldots,\hat{X}^{B}_{i^{*}-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}I_{\mu}(\Pi;\hat{X}^{B}_{i}\mid\hat{X}^{A},\hat{X}^{B}_{1},\dots,\hat{X}^{B}_{i-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\cdot I_{\mu}(\Pi;\hat{X}^{B}|\hat{X}^{A}).

where the first equality is from
2
in
1.1
the mutual independence between the random variables in
\left\{\hat{X}^{A}_{t}\mid 1\leq t\leq m\right\}
and
\left\{\hat{X}^{B}_{t}\mid 1\leq t\leq i^{*}\right\}
, the second equality follows from the fact that
i^{*}
is chosen uniformly at random from the set
[m]
, and the last equality is from the chain rule (
1
in
1.1
).

Lastly, from
Proposition

1.3
, we get that
\textnormal{{CC}}_{\mu}(\pi)\geq I_{\mu}(\Pi;\hat{X}^{B}|\hat{X}^{A})=\Omega(m)
. This completes the proof of
3.2
.
∎

3.2
Communication Lower Bound for MST Estimation in Metric Streams

In this subsection, we generalize the ideas and techniques used in
Section

3.1
to prove a lower bound for the communication complexity of the
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
problem, thus establishing
Theorem

11
.
Let
k=\log N
. We consider the communication game
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
in the blackboard model between
2k
players, denoted by
A^{1},\dots,A^{k},B^{1},\dots,B^{k}
.
Similar to
Section

3.1
, we will first show that the information complexity of any randomized protocol for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
is
\tilde{\Omega}(1)
, and then show that the communication complexity of any randomized protocol for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
is
\tilde{\Omega}(m)
via a direct sum type argument.

The crucial step in this subsection is to construct a hard input distribution for the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
, that (i) can be used to show that the information complexity of the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
is
\tilde{\Omega}(1)
; and (ii) can be easily amplified (for example, via a product distribution) into an input distribution for the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
such that the direct sum argument can be applied.
We first provide some intuition.
Let
\nu
be an input distribution for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
.
Intuitively, we would like to mimick the construction in
Section

3.1
for showing that the information complexity of the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
is
\Omega(1)
(see
Figure

3
).
On the one hand, to achieve property (ii),
\nu
should be supported mostly, if not always, on Yes-instances (where the underlying graph is
G_{\sf Y}
in
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
), since otherwise, if with some probability
p=\omega(1/m)
the distribution is supported on No-instances, then the probability that the product input distribution
\nu^{m/2}
for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
is valid is at most
(1-p)^{m/2}+m\cdot p(1-p)^{m/2-1}
, which is too small and therefore insufficient for an
\tilde{\Omega}(1)
information complexity lower bound of problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
.
On the other hand, to make a valid input distribution for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{2,2}
, and also to ensure that there is always some player who gets partial information and is therefore uncertain about the output, mimicking the construction in
Section

3.1
, we decompose a clique of size
N=2^{k}
into the union of
k
bi-cliques, and then pair the players
A_{i},B_{i}
to give them partial inputs similar to the ones in
Figure

3
.

We now provide the construction of such an input distribution
\nu
for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
in more details.
We denote the vertex set by
P\cup Q
, where
|P|=|Q|=N=2^{k}
.
Each vertex
p\in P
are labeled by a string in
\left\{0,1\right\}^{k}
. Abusing the notation, we also denote by
p=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
the string that corresponds to the vertex
p
. For each
1\leq i\leq k
, we define

U^{P}_{i}=\left\{p\in P\mid p_{i}=0\right\}
, namely the set of all vertices in
P
that corresponds to a string whose
i
-th bit is
0
, and we define
V^{P}_{i}=\left\{p\in P\mid p_{i}=1\right\}
.
Clearly, for each
i\in[k]
,
(U^{P}_{i},V^{P}_{i})
is a partition (or a cut) of
P
(that we denote as the
i
-th partition/cut of
P
), the partitions (or cuts) in
\left\{(U^{P}_{i},V^{P}_{i})\mid i\in[k]\right\}
are distinct, and each edge of
E(P)
participates in at least one of these cuts.
Similarly, we label each vertex
q\in Q
by a string in
\left\{0,1\right\}^{k}
, that we denote by
q
as well, abusing the notation, and we define sets
U^{Q}_{i},V^{Q}_{i}
the
i
-th partition/cut

(U^{Q}_{i},V^{Q}_{i})
of
Q
similarly, for each
1\leq i\leq k
.
The edge/non-edge information given to the players will respect these partitions (and not more fine-grained).

We now describe the possible inputs to the players. Let
i
be an index in
[k]
. The
(k+2)
possible inputs for player
A^{i}
are described below:

•

for each
j\in[k],j\neq i
, a general input
A^{i}_{j}
:

R_{A^{i}}\setminus R^{\prime}_{A^{i}}=(U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})\times(U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})
, and

R^{\prime}_{A^{i}}=\bigg((U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})\times(U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})\bigg)\cup\bigg((U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})\times(U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})\bigg)
;

(note that this input is with respect to the
j
-th partitions but not the
i
-th ones, also note that this input contains complete edge/non-edge information, i.e., in this input
A^{i}
is given the whole underlying graph, which is
G_{\sf Y}
, consisting of a clique on
(U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})
and a clique on
(U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})
);

•

special input
A^{i}_{\textsf{Row}(1)}
(corresponding to the first row in
Figure

3
):

R_{A^{i}}\setminus R^{\prime}_{A^{i}}=(U^{P}_{i}\times V^{P}_{i})\cup(U^{Q}_{i}\times V^{Q}_{i})
, and
R^{\prime}_{A^{i}}=(U^{P}_{i}\times V^{Q}_{i})\cup(U^{Q}_{i}\times V^{P}_{i})
;

(if we view set
U^{P}_{i}
as
u_{1}
, set
U^{Q}_{i}
as
u_{2}
, set
V^{P}_{i}
as
v_{1}
, and set
V^{Q}_{i}
as
v_{2}
, then this is exactly the first row in
Figure

3
, and similarly for the next two special inputs);

•

special input
A^{i}_{\textsf{Row}(2)}
(corresponding to the second row in
Figure

3
):

R_{A^{i}}\setminus R^{\prime}_{A^{i}}=(U^{P}_{i}\times V^{Q}_{i})\cup(U^{Q}_{i}\times V^{P}_{i})
, and
R^{\prime}_{A^{i}}=(U^{P}_{i}\times V^{P}_{i})\cup(U^{Q}_{i}\times V^{Q}_{i})
;

•

special input
A^{i}_{\textsf{Row}(3)}
(corresponding to the third row in
Figure

3
):

R_{A^{i}}\setminus R^{\prime}_{A^{i}}=(U^{P}_{i}\times V^{Q}_{i})\cup(U^{Q}_{i}\times V^{P}_{i})
, and
R^{\prime}_{A^{i}}=U^{P}_{i}\times V^{P}_{i}
.

The
(k+2)
possible inputs for player
B^{i}
are:

•

for each
j\in[k],j\neq i
, a general input
B^{i}_{j}
:

R_{B^{i}}\setminus R^{\prime}_{B^{i}}=(U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})\times(U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})
, and

R^{\prime}_{B^{i}}=\bigg((U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})\times(U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})\bigg)\cup\bigg((U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})\times(U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})\bigg)
;

(note that this input is with respect to the
j
-th partitions but not the
i
-th ones, also note that this input contains complete edge/non-edge information, i.e., in this input
B^{i}
is given the whole underlying graph, which is
G_{\sf Y}
, consisting of a clique on
(U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})
and a clique on
(U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})
);

•

special input
B^{i}_{\textsf{Col}(1)}
(corresponding to the first column in
Figure

3
):

R_{B^{i}}\setminus R^{\prime}_{B^{i}}=\emptyset
, and
R^{\prime}_{B^{i}}=(U^{Q}_{i}\times V^{Q}_{i})
;

(if we view set
U^{P}_{i}
as
u_{1}
, set
U^{Q}_{i}
as
u_{2}
, set
V^{P}_{i}
as
v_{1}
, and set
V^{Q}_{i}
as
v_{2}
, then this is exactly the first column in
Figure

3
, and similarly for the next two special inputs)

•

special input
B^{i}_{\textsf{Col}(2)}
(corresponding to the second column in
Figure

3
):

R_{B^{i}}\setminus R^{\prime}_{B^{i}}=\emptyset
, and
R^{\prime}_{B^{i}}=\emptyset
.

•

special input
B^{i}_{\textsf{Col}(3)}
(corresponding to the third column in
Figure

3
):

R_{B^{i}}\setminus R^{\prime}_{B^{i}}=U^{Q}_{i}\times V^{Q}_{i}
, and
R^{\prime}_{B^{i}}=\emptyset
.

We now describe the distribution
\nu
. It is a uniform distribution on the following set of
(2k+3)
input combinations:

•

for each
j\in[k]
, a general input combination
\Phi^{j}_{1,2}
:

(A^{1}_{j},\ldots,A^{j-1}_{j},A^{j}_{\textsf{Row}(1)},A^{j+1}_{j},A^{k}_{j},B^{1}_{j},\ldots,B^{j-1}_{j},B^{j}_{\textsf{Col}(2)},B^{j+1}_{j},B^{k}_{j})
;

(intuitively, this is a combination in which all players except
A^{j}
and
B^{j}
know the whole graph, which is the union of a clique on
(U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})
and a clique on
(U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})
, while players
A^{j}
and
B^{j}
are in the first-row-second-column scenario of
Figure

3
)

•

for each
j\in[k]
, a general input combination
\Phi^{j}_{1,3}
:

(A^{1}_{j},\ldots,A^{j-1}_{j},A^{j}_{\textsf{Row}(1)},A^{j+1}_{j},A^{k}_{j},B^{1}_{j},\ldots,B^{j-1}_{j},B^{j}_{\textsf{Col}(3)},B^{j+1}_{j},B^{k}_{j})
;

(similarly, this is a combination in which all players except
A^{j}
and
B^{j}
know the whole graph, which is the union of a clique on
(U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j})
and a clique on
(U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j})
, while players
A^{j}
and
B^{j}
are in the first-row-third-column scenario of
Figure

3
)

•

special input combination
\Phi_{2,1}
:
(A^{1}_{\textsf{Row}(2)},\ldots,A^{k}_{\textsf{Row}(2)},B^{1}_{\textsf{Col}(1)},\ldots,B^{k}_{\textsf{Col}(1)})
;

(the underlying graph of this input combination is the union of a clique on
P
and a clique on
Q
; where the edge-information are distributed into
k
bi-cliques to the players, and similarly for the next two combinations)

•

special input combination
\Phi_{2,2}
:
(A^{1}_{\textsf{Row}(2)},\ldots,A^{k}_{\textsf{Row}(2)},B^{1}_{\textsf{Col}(2)},\ldots,B^{k}_{\textsf{Col}(2)})
;

•

special input combination
\Phi_{3,1}
:
(A^{1}_{\textsf{Row}(3)},\ldots,A^{k}_{\textsf{Row}(3)},B^{1}_{\textsf{Col}(1)},\ldots,B^{k}_{\textsf{Col}(1)})
.

We denote
\Omega=\left\{\Phi^{j}_{1,2}\mid j\in[k]\right\}\cup\left\{\Phi^{j}_{1,3}\mid j\in[k]\right\}\cup\left\{\Phi_{2,1},\Phi_{2,2},\Phi_{3,1}\right\}
.
It is not hard to verify that, for all input combinations in
\Omega
, each pair of vertices appears simultaneously in at least one set of
\left\{R_{A^{j}}\right\}_{j\in[k]}\cup\left\{R_{B^{j}}\right\}_{j\in[k]}
, and the information received by the players is consistent. Moreover, in all these combinations, the underlying graph is
G_{\sf Y}
(a union of two cliques). In combinations
\Phi_{2,1},\Phi_{2,2}
and
\Phi_{3,1}
, the cliques are on vertex sets
P
and
Q
. For any
1\leq j\leq k
, in combination
\Phi^{j}_{1,2}
and
\Phi^{j}_{1,3}
, the cliques are on vertex sets
U^{P}_{j}\cup V^{Q}_{j}
and
U^{Q}_{j}\cup V^{P}_{j}
.

To give some intuition why the information complexity of any randomized protocol is
\tilde{\Omega}(1)
over the above distribution. Observe that, in each general input combination
\Phi^{j}_{1,2}
or
\Phi^{j}_{1,3}
, there are two players
A^{j},B^{j}
who do not know whether or not the underlying graph is
G_{\sf Y}
or
G_{\sf N}
, so they need to be notified by
\tilde{\Omega}(1)
bit exchange. On the other hand, in each special input combination, no player knows whether or not the underlying graph is
G_{\sf Y}
or
G_{\sf N}
.

We now provide a formal proof of an information complexity lower bound for any protocol for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
.
Fix an index
1\leq i\leq k
. We define a random variable
X_{i}
as follows. For each
j\in[k],j\neq i
, we let
X_{i}=j
iff the input of player
A^{i}
is
A^{i}_{j}
; for each
t\in[3]
,
X_{i}=\textsf{Row}(t)
iff the input of player
A^{i}
is
A^{i}_{\textsf{Row}(t)}
, so
X_{i}
takes value from
([k]\setminus\left\{j\right\})\cup\left\{\textsf{Row}(1),\textsf{Row}(2),\textsf{Row}(3)\right\}
. Similarly, we define a random variable
Y_{i}
according to the input of player
B^{i}
, that takes a value from
([k]\setminus\left\{j\right\})\cup\left\{\textsf{Col}(1),\textsf{Col}(2),\textsf{Col}(3)\right\}
. We denote
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})
, and for each
1\leq i\leq k
, we denote
X_{-i}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{i-1},X_{i+1},\ldots,X_{k})
, and we define
Y,Y_{-i}
similarly.

We prove the following claim.

Claim 3.3
.

For any randomized protocol
\pi^{\prime}
for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
, there exists an index
i\in[k]
, such that either
I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X_{-i}|X_{i})=\Omega(1/k^{3})
or
I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};Y_{-i}|Y_{i})=\Omega(1/k^{3})
holds.

Proof.

Assume for contradiction that for each
1\leq i\leq k
,
I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X_{-i}|X_{i})<1/(10^{5}k^{3})
and
I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};Y_{-i}|Y_{i})<1/(10^{5}k^{3})
, then for each
1\leq i\leq k
, from
1.5
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{10^{5}\cdot k^{3}}

\displaystyle>I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X_{-i}|X_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\Pr_{\nu}[X_{i}=\textsf{Row}(1)]\cdot I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X_{-i}|X_{i}=\textsf{Row}(1))

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{2k+3}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\bigg(D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}\bigg(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,2}^{i})\text{ }\bigg|\bigg|\text{ }\frac{\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,2}^{i})+\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,3}^{i})}{2}\bigg)+D_{\textnormal{{KL}}}\bigg(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,3}^{i})\text{ }\bigg|\bigg|\text{ }\frac{\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,2}^{i})+\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,3}^{i})}{2}\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{2k+3}\cdot h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,2}^{i}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,3}^{i}))^{2}.

So
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,2}^{i}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,3}^{i}))<1/40k
. Similarly, for each index
1\leq i\leq k
,
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,2}^{i}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}))<1/40k
,

h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,1}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}))<1/40k
,
and

h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,1}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{3,1}))<1/40k
.
We define the following new input combinations:

•

\Phi_{2,2}=\big(A^{1}_{\textsf{Row}(2)},\ldots,A^{k}_{\textsf{Row}(2)},B^{1}_{\textsf{Col}(3)},\ldots,B^{k}_{\textsf{Col}(3)}\big)
;

•

\Phi_{3,2}=\big(A^{1}_{\textsf{Row}(3)},\ldots,A^{k}_{\textsf{Row}(3)},B^{1}_{\textsf{Col}(2)},\ldots,B^{k}_{\textsf{Col}(2)}\big)
;

•

\Phi_{3,3}=\big(A^{1}_{\textsf{Row}(3)},\ldots,A^{k}_{\textsf{Row}(3)},B^{1}_{\textsf{Col}(3)},\ldots,B^{k}_{\textsf{Col}(3)}\big)
.

We prove the following claim.

Observation 3.1
.

h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}))<1/10
.

Proof.

For each subset
S\subseteq[k]
, we define a new input combination

\Phi_{2,3}^{S}=\bigg(\big(A^{i}_{\textsf{Row}(2)}\big)_{i\in[k]},\big(B^{i}_{\textsf{Col}(3)}\big)_{i\in S},\big(B^{i}_{\textsf{Col}(2)}\big)_{i\notin S}\bigg).

Note that
\Phi_{2,3}^{[k]}=\Phi_{2,3}
. We prove by inducetion on
|S|
that, for every
S\subseteq[k]
,
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}^{S}))\leq|S|/10k
. Note that
3.1
follows immediately if this is true.

The base case is when
|S|=1
, namely
S=\left\{i\right\}
for some
i\in[k]
. Note that
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,2}^{i}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,3}^{i}))<1/40k
and
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,2}^{i}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}))<1/40k
, from
1.2
, we get that
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,3}^{i}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}))<1/20k
. Then from
Proposition

1.4
,
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{1,2}^{i}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}^{\left\{i\right\}}))<1/20k
, and so
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}^{\left\{i\right\}}))<1/10k
, and therefore the claim is true when
|S|=1
.

Assume now the claim is true for all subsets
S\subseteq[k]
with
|S|\leq t
. Consider now any subset
S
with
|S|=t+1
.
Pick an arbitrary element
i\in S
. From the induction hypothesis,
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}^{S\setminus\left\{i\right\}}))\leq(|S|-1)/10k
. On the other hand, since
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}^{\left\{i\right\}}))<1/10k
, we have
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}^{\left\{i\right\}}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}^{S\setminus\left\{i\right\}}))<|S|/10k
. Then from
Proposition

1.4
,
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}^{S}))<|S|/10k
.
Therefore, the claim is true. This completes the proof of
3.1
.
∎

On the other hand, since
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,1}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}))<1/40k
,
and

h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,1}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{3,1}))<1/40k
, we get that
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{3,1}))<1/20k
. Then from
Proposition

1.4
,
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,1}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{3,2}))<1/20k
, and so
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{3,2}))<1/10k\leq 1/10
. Combined with
3.1
,
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,3}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{3,2}))<1/5
.
Next from
Proposition

1.4
,
h(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{3,3}))<1/5
. From
1.4
,
\Delta_{\textnormal{{TV}}}(\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2,2}),\Pi^{\prime}(\Phi_{3,3}))<0.3
, which means, with probability at least
2\cdot(0.5-0.3)=0.4
, protocol
\pi^{\prime}
will have identical output on input combinations
\Phi_{2,2}
and
\Phi_{3,3}
. However,
\Phi_{3,3}
is a valid input combination where the underlying graph is
G_{\sf N}
, while
\Phi_{2,2}
is a valid input combination where the underlying graph is
G_{\sf Y}
. This contradicts the assumption that
\pi^{\prime}
is an
1/10
-error randomized protocol for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
.
∎

We now proceed to show the communication complexity lower bound for
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
via a reduction from
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
.
We first construct an input distribution
\mu
for
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
as follows.
The vertex set is partitioned into
m/2
groups of size
2N
each. For every pair of vertices that come from different group, the non-edge information of this pair is given to all players.
The distribution on the edge/non-edge information within each group is
\nu
, and the distribution for different groups are independent.

Let
\pi
be a randomized protocol
\pi
for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
. We use
\pi
to construct a procotol
\pi^{\prime}
for problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
as follows.

Protocol

\pi^{\prime}
: A protocol for
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
using the protocol
\pi
for
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
.

Input:
An instance
\Phi^{*}
of
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
.

Output:
A label
\sf Y
or
\sf N
indicating whether
G=G_{\sf Y}
or
G=G_{\sf N}
in problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
.

1.

Player
A_{1}
choose an index
i
from
[m/2]
uniformly at sample.

2.

Player
A_{1}
independently sample
(m/2-1)
instances of
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
problem from distribution
\nu
on
\Omega
, and denote them by
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{i-1},\Phi_{i+1},\dots,\Phi_{m/2}
.

3.

Player
A_{1}
sends the number
i
and instance
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{i-1},\Phi_{i+1},\dots,\Phi_{m/2}
to all other players.

4.

The players run the protocol
\pi
on the instance
\Phi^{\prime}=(\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{i-1},\Phi^{*},\Phi_{i+1},\dots,\Phi_{m/2})
of the
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
problem, where the instance
\Phi_{j}
is on the
j
-th group of vertices and the instance
\Phi^{*}
is on the
i
-th group of vertices, and then return the output of
\pi
.

We now proceed to prove a lower bound on the information complexity of
\pi^{\prime}
. Let
\Phi
be an instance of either the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
or the problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
. For each
1\leq i\leq k
, we denote by
X_{i}(\Phi)
the input of player
A^{i}
in the instance
\Phi
. We denote
X(\Phi)=(X_{j}(\Phi))_{j\in[k]}
and

X_{-i}(\Phi)=(X_{j}(\Phi))_{j\in[k],j\neq i}
.
Similarly, we define
Y_{i}(\Phi)
,
Y(\Phi)
and
Y_{-i}(\Phi)
for inputs of players
B^{1},\ldots,B^{k}
.

Observe that, for each
j\neq i
, instance
\Phi_{j}
is sampled from
\nu
, so instance
\Phi^{\prime}
of problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
is a
YES
instance iff instance
\Phi^{*}
of problem
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,2}
is a
YES
instance. Moreover, since instance
\Phi^{*}
is also sampled from
\nu
, the distribution of instance
\Phi^{\prime}
is exactly
\mu
.

Claim 3.4
.

For each
1\leq j\leq k
,

I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X_{-j}(\Phi^{*})|X_{j}(\Phi^{*}))\leq\frac{2}{m}\cdot I_{\mu}(\Pi;X_{-j}(\Phi^{\prime})|X_{j}(\Phi^{\prime}))
, and

I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};Y_{-j}(\Phi^{*})|Y_{j}(\Phi^{*}))\leq\frac{2}{m}\cdot I_{\mu}(\Pi;Y_{-j}(\Phi^{\prime})|Y_{j}(\Phi^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

Denote
\Phi_{i}=\Phi^{*}
. From the construction of protocol
\pi^{\prime}
,
\Pi^{\prime}=(i,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{i-1},\Phi_{i+1},\dots,\Phi_{m/2},\Pi)
.
From
2
in
1.1
,

\displaystyle I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};X_{-j}(\Phi^{*})|X_{j}(\Phi^{*}))

\displaystyle=I_{\mu}(\Pi;X_{-j}(\Phi^{*})\mid X_{j}(\Phi^{*}))

\displaystyle\leq I_{\mu}(\Pi;X_{-j}(\Phi^{*})\mid i,\Phi_{1}\dots,\Phi_{i-1},X_{j}(\Phi_{i+1}),\dots,X_{j}(\Phi_{m/2}),X_{j}(\Phi^{*}))

\displaystyle=I_{\mu}(\Pi;X_{-j}(\Phi_{i})\mid i,X_{j}(\Phi_{1})\dots,X_{j}(\Phi_{m/2}),X_{-j}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,X_{-j}(\Phi_{i-1}))

\displaystyle=I_{\mu}(\Pi;X_{-j}(\Phi_{i})\mid i,X_{j}(\Phi^{\prime}),X_{-j}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,X_{-j}(\Phi_{i-1}))

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{m}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m/2}I_{\mu}(\Pi;X_{-j}(\Phi_{i})\mid X_{j}(\Phi^{\prime}),X_{-j}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,X_{-j}(\Phi_{i-1}))

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{m}\cdot I_{\mu}(\Pi,X_{-j}(\Phi^{\prime})|X_{j}(\Phi^{\prime}))

where the last equation is from the chain rule (
1
in
1.1
). Similarly, we can prove that

I_{\nu}(\Pi^{\prime};Y_{-j}(\Phi^{*})|Y_{j}(\Phi^{*}))\leq\frac{2}{m}\cdot I_{\mu}(\Pi,Y_{-j}(\Phi^{\prime})|Y_{j}(\Phi^{\prime})).

∎

From
3.4
and
Proposition

1.3
, for any randomized protocol
\pi
for the
{\mathsf{COI}}_{N,m}
problem,
\textnormal{{CC}}_{\mu}(\pi)\geq\Omega(m/k^{3})=\Omega(m/\log^{3}N)
. This completes the proof of
Theorem

11
.

4
Space Lower Bounds for MST and TSP Estimation in Graph Streams

In this section we provide our lower bound results on graph streams.
We will prove a space complexity lower bound for multi-pass streaming algorithms for MST cost estimation in
Section

4.1
, and a space complexity lower bound for one-pass streaming algorithms for TSP-cost estimation in
Section

4.2
.

4.1
Space Lower Bound for Multi-Pass Streaming Algorithms for MST Estimation

In this section we show that any randomized
p
-pass streaming algorithm that outputs any approximation for MST estimation has to use at least
\Omega(n/p)
, thus establishing
Theorem

2
.
Similar to
Section

2.2
, we prove the space lower bound of streaming algorithms by analyzing a 2-player communication game defined as follows.

Two-player graph MST estimation problem (
\emph{\sf MST}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
):

This is a communication problem, in which Alice is given a weighted graph
G_{A}
, and Bob is given a weighted graph
G_{B}
, with the promise that
V(G_{A})=V(G_{B})
,
E(G_{A})\cap E(G_{B})=\emptyset
, and
G_{A}\cup G_{B}
is a connected graph.
Alice and Bob are allowed to communicate in the blackboard model. The goal is to compute an estimate
Y
of
{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{A}\cup G_{B})
.
For any constant
\alpha>0
, we say that
Y
is an
\alpha
-approximation of
{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{A}\cup G_{B})
iff
Y\leq{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{A}\cup G_{B})\leq\alpha\cdot Y
.

The main result of this section is the following theorem, which immediately implies
Theorem

2
.

Theorem 12
.

For any parameter
\alpha>1
, for any randomized blackboard protocol
\pi
that computes an
\alpha
-approximation for the
\emph{\sf MST}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
problem,
\textnormal{{CC}}(\pi)\geq\Omega(n)
.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

12
.
We will prove
Theorem

12
via a reduction from the well-known 2-player communication problem called
Disjointness
(
Disj
). In the
Disj
problem, Alice is given a string
X^{A}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
and Bob is given a string
X^{B}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
. They are allowed to communicate in the blackboard model. The goal is to check if there is an index
i\in[n]
such that
X^{A}_{i}=X^{B}_{i}=1
.
We use the following result on the communication complexity of
Disj
problem.

Lemma 4.1

(​​​
[
BJKS04
]
​)
.

For any randomized protocol
\pi
for the
Disj
problem,
\textnormal{{CC}}(\pi)=\Omega(n)
.

We now show a reduction from
Disj
problem to the
\emph{\sf MST}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
problem. Let
(X^{A},X^{B})
be an input pair of the
Disj
problem. We construct a pair
G_{A},G_{B}
of weighted graphs as follows. We use the parameter
M=2\alpha n
.
The common vertex set is
V(G_{A})=V(G_{B})=\left\{u,u^{\prime}\right\}\cup\left\{v_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\right\}
.
The edge set
E(G_{A})
contains: (i) for each
1\leq i\leq n
, an edge
(u,v_{i})
, that has weight
1
if
X^{A}_{i}=0
and has weight
M
if
X^{A}_{i}=1
; and (ii) an edge
(u,u^{\prime})
with weight
1
.
The edge set
E(G_{B})
contains, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, an edge
(u^{\prime},v_{i})
, that has weight
1
if
X^{B}_{i}=0
and has weight
M
if
X^{B}_{i}=1
.
Clearly,
E(G_{A})\cap E(G_{B})=\emptyset
and
G_{A}\cup G_{B}
is a connected graph.

Observe that, if there is an index
i\in[n]
such that
x_{i}=y_{i}=1
, then the two incident edges of
v_{i}
,
(u,v_{i})
and
(u^{\prime},v_{i})
, both have weight
M
, and since any spanning tree must contain one of these two edges, we get that
{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{A}\cup G_{B})\geq M
. If for all indices
i\in[n]
, either
x_{i}=0
or
y_{i}=0
holds, then for each
i\in[n]
, there is at least one incident edge of
v_{i}
with weight
1
. Taking the union of these edges with the edge
(u,u^{\prime})
, we obtain a spanning tree of
G_{A}\cup G_{B}
with weight
n+1
, so in this case
{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{A}\cup G_{B})=n+1
.
Since
M=2\alpha n
, any
\alpha
-approximation protocol for
{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{A}\cup G_{B})
can indeed determine the value of
{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{A}\cup G_{B})
. Therefore, any randomized protocol for
\alpha
-approximating the
\emph{\sf MST}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
problem can be turned into a randomized protocol for the
Disj
problem, and therefore, from
Lemma

4.1
, it has to have communication complexity
\Omega(n)
.

4.2
Space Lower Bound for One-Pass Streaming Algorithms for TSP Estimation

In this subsection we show that, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
, any randomized one-pass streaming algorithm that takes as input a graph stream and estimates the graphic TSP-cost of a the input graph to within a factor of
2-\varepsilon
, must use
\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}n^{2})
space, thus establishing
Theorem

5
.
At a high level, our proof considers the 2-player communication game, in which Alice is given a graph
G_{A}
and Bob is given a graph
G_{B}
, and they are asked to estimate the TSP-cost of metric induced by the shortest-path distance metric of graph
G_{A}\cup G_{B}
to within a factor of
(2-\varepsilon)
. It is well-known that the space complexity of any one-pass
(2-\varepsilon)
-approximation streaming algorithm is lower bounded by the one-way communication complexity of the game. We then show that the one-way communication complexity of the game is
\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}n^{2})
by constructing a hard distribution and bounding its information complexity from below by reducing the
Index
problem to it.

Two-player graphic TSP cost approximation problem (
\emph{\sf TSP}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
):

This is a 2-player communication problem, in which Alice is given a weighted graph
G_{A}
and Bob is given a weighted graph
G_{B}
, with the promise that
V(G_{A})=V(G_{B})
,
E(G_{A})\cap E(G_{B})=\emptyset
and all weights of the weighted graph
G^{*}=G_{A}\cup G_{B}
form a partial metric.
Alice is allowed to send a message to Bob, and then Bob, upon receiving this message, needs to compute an estimate
Y
of
\mathsf{TSP}(G^{*})
.

Let
0<\delta<1
be a constant and let
{\mathcal{D}}
be an input distribution.
We say that a protocol
\pi

(2-\varepsilon,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf TSP}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
, if and only if, with probability at least
1-\delta
, the estimate
Y
returned by protocol
\pi
satisfies that
Y\leq\mathsf{TSP}(G^{*})\leq(2-\varepsilon)\cdot Y
.

The main result of this subsection is the following theorem, which immediately implies
Theorem

5
.

Theorem 13
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be a constant. For any real number
0<\delta<1/10
, there exists a distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
on input of the problem
\emph{\sf TSP}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
, such that, for any one-way protocol
\pi
that
(2-\varepsilon,\delta)
-approximates
\emph{\sf TSP}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
over
{\mathcal{D}}
,
\emph{\textnormal{{CC}}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}(\pi)=\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}n^{2})
.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

13
.

We now proceed to construct the hard distribution.
Let
r
be some parameter to be fixed later, and let
p=\left\lfloor n/2r\right\rfloor
.
We first define an unweighted graph
G
as follows. Its vertex set is
V(G)=\left\{u_{0},u^{\prime}_{0}\right\}\cup U\cup U^{\prime}
, where
U=\left\{u_{i,t}\mid i\in[p],t\in[r]\right\}
and
U^{\prime}=\left\{u^{\prime}_{j,t}\mid j\in[p],t\in[r]\right\}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq p
, we denote
U_{i}=\left\{u_{i,t}\mid t\in[r]\right\}
and similarly for each
1\leq j\leq p
, we denote

U^{\prime}_{j}=\left\{u^{\prime}_{j,t}\mid t\in[r]\right\}
. Therefore,

V=\left\{u_{0},u^{\prime}_{0}\right\}\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in[p]}U_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in[p]}U^{\prime}_{j}\bigg)
.
The edge set of
G
contains (i) the edge
(u_{0},v_{0})
; (ii) for each
u\in U
, the edge
(u_{0},u)
; and (iii) for each
u^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
, the edge
(u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime})
.
For each
1\leq i\leq p
, we denote
E_{i}=\left\{(u_{0},u_{i,t})\mid t\in[r]\right\}
and similarly for each
1\leq j\leq p
, we denote
E^{\prime}_{j}=\left\{(u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{j,t})\mid t\in[r]\right\}
. Therefore,

E(G)=\left\{(u_{0},u^{\prime}_{0})\right\}\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in[p]}E_{i}\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in[p]}E^{\prime}_{j}\bigg)
.
See
Figure

4(a)
for an illustration.

Let
X
be an
p\times p
matrix, where each entry
X_{i,j}
is either
0
or
1
. Let
i^{*},j^{*}
be two indices of
[p]
(where possibly
i^{*}=j^{*}
). We now define the weighted graph
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
based on
G
as follows. Its vertex set is
V(G)
. Its edge set contains (i) all edges of
E(G)
; and (ii) for each pair
i,j\in[p]
such that
X_{i,j}=1
, the edges of
E_{i,j}=\left\{(u_{i,t},u^{\prime}_{j,t}),(u_{i,t},u^{\prime}_{j,t+1})\mid t\in[r]\right\}
.
The weights on edges of
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
are defined as follows.
Let
L
be a parameter to be fixed later.
The edges of
E_{i^{*}}\cup E^{\prime}_{j^{*}}
have weight
(L+2)
.
The edges of
E(G)\setminus(E_{i^{*}}\cup E_{j^{*}})
have weight
1
.
For each pair
i,j\in[p]
such that
X_{i,j}=1
, the edges of
M_{i,j}
have weight
(L+2)
.
See
Figure

4(b)
for an illustration.

(a)

Graph
G
, where
r=2
,
p=3
and
n=14
.

(b)

Graph
G_{X,1,1}
, where
1
-entries of
X
are
X_{1,1},X_{2,3},X_{3,2}
.

Figure 4:
An illustration of graphs
G
and
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
.

We prove the following observation.

Observation 4.1
.

{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}})=(n+2r-2)+(2r+1)L
.
If
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=0
, then
\mathsf{TSP}(G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}})=2\cdot((n+2r-2)+(2r+1)L)
. If
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=1
, then
\mathsf{TSP}(G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}})\leq 2n-6+(2r+2)L
.

Proof.

It is easy to see, from the definition of graph
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
, that the edges of
E(G)
form a minimum spanning tree of
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
, regardless of
X
and the values of
i^{*},j^{*}
. Therefore,

\begin{split}{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}})=&\text{ }w(u_{0},u^{\prime}_{0})+\sum_{i\in[p]}\sum_{t\in[r]}w(u_{0},u_{i,t})+\sum_{j\in[p]}\sum_{t\in[r]}w(u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{j,t})\\
=&\text{ }L+\bigg(\frac{n}{2}-r-1\bigg)+r\cdot(L+2)+\bigg(\frac{n}{2}-r-1\bigg)+r\cdot(L+2)=(n+2r-2)+(2r+1)L.\end{split}

Assume that
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=0
. It is easy to see that the optimal TSP-tour that visits all vertices of
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
is the Euler-tour of tree
G
, whose cost is
2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}})=(2n+4r-4)+(4r+2)L
.
Assume now that
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=1
. Now consider the tour
\pi
that, starting from vertex
u_{0}
, first sequentially visit vertices
u_{i^{*},1},u^{\prime}_{j^{*},1},u_{i^{*},2},u^{\prime}_{j^{*},2},\ldots,u_{i^{*},t},u^{\prime}_{j^{*},t},u^{\prime}_{0}
, and then visited all other vertices and finally come back to
u_{0}
by travelling along edges of
E(G)\setminus(E_{i^{*}}\cup E^{\prime}_{j^{*}})
at most twice and edge
(u_{0},v_{0})
once. See
Figure

5
for an illustration.
It is clear that the cost of such a tour is

w(u_{0},u^{\prime}_{0})+w(u_{0},u^{\prime}_{i^{*},1})+w(u^{\prime}_{0},u^{\prime}_{j^{*},t})+\sum_{t\in[r]}w(u_{i^{*},t},u^{\prime}_{j^{*},t})+\sum_{t\in[r-1]}w(u_{i^{*},t},u^{\prime}_{j^{*},t+1})+\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus(E_{i^{*}}\cup E^{\prime}_{j^{*}})}2\cdot w(e),

which is bounded by
3L+(2r-1)(L+2)+2(n-2r-2)=2n-6+(2r+2)L
.

Figure 5:
An illustration of part of tour
\pi
when
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=1
.

∎

Distribution

{\mathcal{D}}^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP}(L,r)
: An distribution on pairs
(G_{A},G_{B})
of edge-disjoint graphs on
V
:

1.

Choose a random matrix
X
, where each entry
X_{i,j}
is chosen uniformly at random from
\left\{0,1\right\}
.

2.

Choose two indices
i^{*},j^{*}
uniformly at random from
[p]
(with replacement).

3.

Define graph
G_{A}
to be the subgraph of
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
induced by edges of
\bigcup_{i,j:X_{i,j}=1}E_{i,j}
.

4.

Define graph
G_{B}
to be the subgraph of
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
induced by edges of
E(G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}})\setminus E(G_{A})
.

We now show that, if we set
r=1/\varepsilon
and
L=n^{2}
, then for any protocol
\pi
that
(2-\varepsilon,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf TSP}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
over
{\mathcal{D}}^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP}(L,r)
,
\emph{\textnormal{{CC}}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP}(L,r)}(\pi)=\Omega(\epsilon^{2}n^{2})
.

We will prove this lower bound by a reduction from the well-known problem
Index
to the problem
\emph{\sf TSP}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
. For completeness, we first provide a definition of the
Index
problem and state the previous result on its communication complexity.

Index Problem (
\textsf{Index}_{k}
):

This is a 2-player communication problem, in which Alice is given a vector
X\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{k}
, and Bob is given an index
i^{*}\in[k]
. Alice is allowed to send a message to Bob, and then Bob, upon receiving this message, needs to outputs the value of
X_{i^{*}}
, the
i^{*}
-th coordinate of the input vector
X
of Alice.

Distribution

\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{Index}}^{k}
: An distribution on input pairs
(X,i^{*})
of problem
\textsf{Index}_{k}
:

1.

Vector
X
is chosen uniformly at random from
\left\{0,1\right\}^{k}
.

2.

Index
i^{*}
is chosen uniformly at random from
[k]
.

The following lower bound on the communication complexity of
\textsf{Index}_{k}
over
{\mathcal{D}}^{k}_{\sf Index}
is proved in
[
KNR99
]
.

Theorem 14

(​​​
[
KNR99
]
​)
.

Let
0<\delta<1/2
be any constant. Then for any
\delta
-error one-way protocol
\pi
for problem
Index
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}^{k}_{\textnormal{\sf Index}}
,
\textnormal{{CC}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{k}_{\textnormal{\sf Index}}}(\pi)=\Omega(k)
.

Let
\pi_{\textsf{TSP}}^{\sf g}
be a protocol that
(2-\varepsilon,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf TSP}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP}(L,r)
.
We first construct the following protocol
\pi_{\textsf{Index}}
for solving the problem
Index
using the protocol
\pi_{\textsf{TSP}}^{\sf g}
.
Note that, if
X
is a matrix chosen uniformly at random from
\left\{0,1\right\}^{p\times p}
, and
(i^{*},j^{*})
is a pair chosen uniformly at random from
[p]\times[p]
, then this distribution is equivalent to
\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{Index}}^{p^{2}}
.

Protocol

\pi_{\textsf{Index}}
: A protocol for
\textsf{Index}_{p^{2}}
using a protocol
\pi_{\textsf{TSP}}^{\sf g}
for
\textsf{TSP}_{\textsf{apx}}^{\sf g}
.

Input:
An instance
(X,(i^{*},j^{*}))\sim\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{Index}}^{p^{2}}
.

Output:
A value from
\left\{0,1\right\}
as the answer to
\textsf{Index}_{p^{2}}
.

1.

Defining the instance.
Alice and Bob create an instance
(G_{A},G_{B})
of
\textsf{TSP}_{\textsf{apx}}^{\sf g}
as follows.

(a)

Construct the graph
G^{*}=G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
.

(b)

Define graph
G_{A}
to be the subgraph of
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
induced by edges of
\bigcup_{i,j:x_{i,j=1}}E_{i,j}
.

(c)

Define graph
G_{B}
to be the subgraph of
G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}}
induced by edges of
E(G^{*})\setminus E(G_{A})
.

2.

Computing the answer.
Alice and Bob run the protocol
\pi_{\textsf{TSP}}^{\sf g}
on
(G_{A},G_{B})
to compute an estimate
Y
. If
Y<2\cdot((n+2r-2)+(2r+1)L)
, then they return
1
; otherwise they return
0
.

Claim 4.1
.

If the one-way protocol
\pi^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP}

(2-\varepsilon,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf TSP}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP}(n^{2},1/\varepsilon)
, then the protocol
\pi_{\sf Index}
is a
\delta
-error one-way protocol for
Index
problem over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}_{\sf Index}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that the distribution of instances
(G_{A},G_{B})
for
\textsf{TSP}_{\textsf{apx}}^{\sf g}
created in the reduction by the choice of pairs
(X,i^{*},j^{*})\sim\mathcal{D}_{\textsf{Index}}
, is exactly the same as the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}_{\textnormal{\sf TSP}}^{\sf g}(L,r)
, where
L=n^{2}
and
r=1/\varepsilon
. Moreover, from
4.1
, if
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=1
, then
\mathsf{TSP}(G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}})\leq 2n-6+(2r+2)L
; and if
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=0
, then
\mathsf{TSP}(G_{X,i^{*},j^{*}})=2\cdot((n+2r-2)+(2r+1)L)
.
Therefore, if we set
r=1/\varepsilon
and
L=n^{2}
, then the ratio between two values of
\mathsf{TSP}(G^{*})
when
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=0
and when
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=1
is

\frac{2\cdot((n+2r-2)+(2r+1)L)}{2n-6+(2r+2)L}=\frac{4rL+(2n+4r+2L-4)}{2rL+(2n+2L-6)}>2-\varepsilon,

and it follows that any
(2-\varepsilon)
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}(G^{*})
can in fact determine the value of
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}
.
∎

Combine
4.1
and the communication complexity lower bound for problem
Index
, we get that, if
\pi^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP}
is an one-way protocol that
(2-\varepsilon,\delta)
-approximates the problem
\emph{\sf TSP}^{\sf g}_{\sf apx}
over the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP}(n^{2},1/\varepsilon)
, then

\emph{\textnormal{{CC}}}^{\textnormal{1-way}}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP}(L,r)}(\pi^{\sf g}_{\sf TSP})=\Omega(p^{2})=\Omega((n/r)^{2})=\Omega(\varepsilon^{2}n^{2}).

This completes the proof of
Theorem

13
.

5
A Two-Pass Algorithm for TSP Estimation in Graph Streams

In this section, we present a deterministic
2
-pass
1.96
-approximation algorithm for TSP estimation in graph streams, which uses
\tilde{O}(n)
space, thus proving
Theorem

6
. Our algorithm will utilize the notion of cover advantage, introduced in
Section

2
. This result is in a sharp contrast to
Theorem

5
which showed that any single-pass algorithm requires
\Omega(n^{2})
space to obtain a better than
2
-approximation.

Algorithm.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
be two constants whose values will be set later.
In the first pass, we simply compute a minimum spanning tree
T
and its cost
{\mathsf{MST}}=\sum_{e\in E(T)}w(e)
.
Throughout the second pass, we maintain a subset
E_{\textsf{temp}}
of edges, that is initialized to be
\emptyset
, and will only grow over the course of the algorithm.
Upon the arrival of each edge
e
, we compare
w(e)
with
w({\mathsf{cov}}(e)\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{\textsf{temp}}))=\sum_{f\in{\mathsf{cov}}(e)\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{\textsf{temp}})}w(f)
.
We add the edge
e
to set
E_{\textsf{temp}}
iff
w(e)\leq\alpha\cdot w({\mathsf{cov}}(e)\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{\textsf{temp}}))
.
Let
E^{*}
be the set
E_{\textsf{temp}}
at the end of the algorithm. We then compute
w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))=\sum_{e\in{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*})}w(e)
.
If
w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))\geq\beta\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then we output
(2-\frac{(1-\alpha)\cdot\beta}{2})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as an estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
; otherwise we output
2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.
We use the parameters
\alpha=0.715
and
\beta=0.285
, so
(2-\frac{(1-\alpha)\cdot\beta}{2})\approx\frac{2}{2\alpha(1-\beta)}\approx 1.96
.

Proof of Correctness.

The correctness of the algorithm is guaranteed by the following two claims.

Claim 5.1
.

If
w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))\geq\beta\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then
\mathsf{TSP}\leq(2-\frac{(1-\alpha)\cdot\beta}{2})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

Proof.

Let
E^{\prime}
be the random subset of
E^{*}
that includes each edge of
E^{*}
independently with probability
1/2
. We will show that the expected total weight of all edges in graph
E(H_{T,E^{\prime}})
is at most
(2-\frac{(1-\alpha)\cdot\beta}{2})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, namely
\mathbb{E}[w(E(H_{T,E^{\prime}}))]\leq(2-\frac{(1-\alpha)\cdot\beta}{2})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. Note that this implies that there exists a subset
E^{**}
of
E^{*}
, such that
w(E(H_{T,E^{**}}))\leq(2-\frac{(1-\alpha)\cdot\beta}{2})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. Combined with
2.1
, this implies that there is an Eulerian tour of the same cost (using only edges of graph
H_{T,E^{**}}
). Therefore, there is a TSP-tour of at most the same cost, completing the proof of
5.1
.

We now show that
\mathbb{E}[w(E(H_{T,E^{\prime}}))]\leq(2-\frac{(1-\alpha)\cdot\beta}{2})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. From the definition of graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
,
E(H_{T,E^{\prime}})=E^{\prime}\cup E_{[T,E^{\prime}]}
. On one hand, from the definition of the random subset
E^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{E}[w(E^{\prime})]=w(E^{*})/2
.
On the other hand, for each edge
f\in{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*})
, with probability
1/2
graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
contains
1
copy of it, and with probability
1/2
graph
H_{T,E^{\prime}}
contains
2
copies of it. Therefore,
\mathbb{E}[w(E_{[T,E^{\prime}]})]=2\cdot w(E(T))-w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))/2=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))/2
.
Altogether,
\mathbb{E}[w(E(H_{T,E^{\prime}}))]=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-(w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))-w(E^{*}))/2
.
We use the following observation.

Observation 5.1
.

w(E^{*})\leq\alpha\cdot w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))
.

Proof.

From the algorithm of constructing the set
E^{*}
, an edge
e
is added to the set
E_{\textsf{temp}}
we are maintaining, iff
w({\mathsf{cov}}(E_{\textsf{temp}}\cup\left\{e\right\}))-w({\mathsf{cov}}(E_{\textsf{temp}}))\geq w(e)/\alpha
.
Therefore,
w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))\geq w(E^{*})/\alpha
.
∎

From
5.1
,

\mathbb{E}[w(E(H_{T,E^{\prime}}))]\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\frac{(1-\alpha)\cdot w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))}{2}\leq\bigg(2-\frac{(1-\alpha)\cdot\beta}{2}\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}.

This concludes the proof of
5.1
.
∎

We next show that, if we do not find a sufficiently large cover, i.e., the value of
w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))
is not sufficiently large compared with
{\mathsf{MST}}
, then
\mathsf{TSP}
must be bounded away from
{\mathsf{MST}}
.

Claim 5.2
.

If
w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))<\beta\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then
\mathsf{TSP}\geq 2\alpha(1-\beta)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

Proof.

Recall that set
V_{1}(T)
contains all vertices with odd degree in
T
. Let
M
be a minimum-cost perfect matching on
V_{1}(T)
.
From
Lemma

2.5
,
\mathsf{TSP}\geq 2\cdot w(M)
.

We use the following observations.

Observation 5.2
.

{\mathsf{cov}}(M)=E(T)
.

Proof.

Let
T^{\prime}
be the tree obtained from
T
by suppressing all degree-
2
vertices, so
V(T^{\prime})
is exactly the set of special vertices of
T
,
M
can be viewed as a set of edges (not belonging to
T^{\prime}
) connecting pairs of vertices in
V(T^{\prime})
