, and it suffices to show that each edge of
E(T^{\prime})
is covered by some edge of
M
.

Consider an edge
f^{\prime}\in E(T^{\prime})
where
f^{\prime}=(u,v)
. Let
(S_{u},S_{v})
be cut in
T
that contains a single edge
f^{\prime}
.
Clearly, both
T[S_{v}]
and
T[S_{u}]
have an even number of odd-degree vertices. Therefore, the number of vertices of
S_{u}
with an odd degree in
T[S_{u}]\cup f^{\prime}
is odd, and the same holds for
S_{v}
, and it follows that some edge of
M
connects a vertex of
S_{v}
to a vertex of
S_{u}
, implying that edge
f^{\prime}
is covered by
M
.
∎

Observation 5.3
.

For each
e\in M
,
w({\mathsf{cov}}(e)\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))<w(e)/\alpha
.

Proof.

We denote by
Q_{e}
the shortest-path in
G
connecting the endpoints of
e
(where
G
is the graph underlying the stream). Since
Q_{e}
is a subgraph of
G
, all edges of
Q_{e}
will appear in the graph stream of
G
.
Note that
w(Q_{e})=w(e)
and
{\mathsf{cov}}(e)\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E(Q_{e}))
.

We will show that, for every edge
e^{\prime}\in E(Q_{e})
,
w({\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))<w(e^{\prime})/\alpha
. Note that the observation follows from this assertion, as

w({\mathsf{cov}}(e)\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))\leq w({\mathsf{cov}}(E(Q_{e}))\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))\leq\sum_{e^{\prime}\in E(Q_{e})}w({\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))<\frac{w(Q_{e})}{\alpha}=\frac{w(e)}{\alpha}.

Consider now any edge
e^{\prime}\in E(Q_{e})
, and assume for contradiction that
w({\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))\geq w(e^{\prime})/\alpha
.
Note that set
E_{\textsf{temp}}
only grows over the course of the algorithm that computes set
E^{*}
, and so does the set
{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{\textsf{temp}})
. Therefore, when
e^{\prime}
arrives in the stream,

w({\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E_{\textsf{temp}}))\geq w(e^{\prime})/\alpha
must hold. Then according to the algorithm, the edge
e^{\prime}
should be added to
E_{\textsf{temp}}
right away, which means that edge
e^{\prime}
will eventually belong to
E^{*}
, leading to
{\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*})
and
w({\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))=0
, a contradiction to the assumption that
w({\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime})\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))\geq w(e^{\prime})/\alpha
.
∎

From
5.2
and
5.3
, we get that

(1-\beta)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\leq{\mathsf{MST}}-w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))=w({\mathsf{cov}}(M)\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))\leq\sum_{e\in M}w({\mathsf{cov}}(e)\setminus{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*}))<w(M)/\alpha.

Therefore,
w(M)\geq\alpha(1-\beta)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. Since
\mathsf{TSP}\geq 2\cdot w(M)
, we conclude that

\mathsf{TSP}\geq 2\alpha(1-\beta)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.
∎

6
Space Lower Bound for One-Pass Exact TSP Estimation in Metric Streams

In this section we show that any one-pass streaming algorithm that given a metric stream for some metric
w
on a set of
n
vertices and computes the value of
\mathsf{TSP}(w)
exactly has to use
\Omega(n^{2})
space. At a high level, our proof considers the corresponding
2
-player communication game, and show that the one-way communication complexity of the game is
\Omega(n^{2})
by a reduction from the problem
Index
.

Two-Player TSP Computing Problem (
\mathsf{TSP}_{2}
):

This is a 2-player one-way communication problem, in which Alice is given partial metric
\bar{w}_{A}
and Bob is given a partial metric
\bar{w}_{B}
, both on a set
V
of vertices that is known to both players, with the promise that
\bar{w}_{A}
and
\bar{w}_{B}
are complimentary.
The goal of the players is to compute the value of
\mathsf{TSP}(w)
where
w=\bar{w}_{A}\cup\bar{w}_{B}
.

The main result of this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 15
.

Any one-way protocol
\pi
for the
\mathsf{TSP}_{2}
problem has
\textnormal{{CC}}(\pi)=\Omega(n^{2})
.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

15
.

We now show a reduction from the
Index
problem to the
\mathsf{TSP}_{2}
problem. Assume that
n=2k+1
is an odd integer.
We denote the vertex set by
V=U\cup U^{\prime}
, where
|U|=k+1
and
|U^{\prime}|=k
, and in particular, we denote

U=\left\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k+1}\right\}
and
U^{\prime}=\left\{u^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,u^{\prime}_{k}\right\}
.

Let
X
be a
(k+1)\times(k+1)

0/1
-matrix, such that
X_{i,i}=0
for all
i\in[k+1]
, and
X_{i,j}=X_{j,i}
for all pairs
i,j\in[k+1]
.
For such a matrix
X
, we define a partial metric
\bar{w}_{A}
as follows. For each pair
u_{i},u_{j}
of vertices in
U
,
\bar{w}(u_{i},u_{j})=X_{i,j}+1
; for each pair
u^{\prime}_{i},u^{\prime}_{j}
of vertices in
U^{\prime}
,
\bar{w}(u_{i},u_{j})=*
and for each pair
u\in U,u^{\prime}\in U^{\prime}
,
\bar{w}(u,u^{\prime})=*
. Let
i^{*},j^{*}
be a pair of distinct indices in
[k+1]
. We define a partial metric
\bar{w}_{B}
as follows. Let
P_{i^{*},j^{*}}
be any path that has endpoints
u_{i^{*}},u_{j^{*}}
and alternates between vertices in sets
U
and vertices in set
U^{\prime}
, i.e.,
P_{i^{*},j^{*}}=(u_{q_{1}}=u_{i^{*}},u^{\prime}_{p_{1}},u_{q_{2}},u^{\prime}_{p_{2}},\ldots,u_{q_{k}},u^{\prime}_{p_{k}},u_{q_{k+1}}=u_{j^{*}})
, where
(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k})
is a permutation of
[k]
and
(q_{1},p_{2},\ldots,q_{k+1})
is a permutation of
[k+1]
. Now for each pair
u^{\prime}_{i},u^{\prime}_{j}\in U
, we set
\bar{w}_{B}(u^{\prime}_{i},u^{\prime}_{j})=2
; for each pair
u_{i},u_{j}\in U
, we set
\bar{w}_{B}(u_{i},u_{j})=*
, and for each pair
u_{i}\in U,u^{\prime}_{j}\in U^{\prime}
, we set
\bar{w}_{B}(u_{i},u_{j})=1
iff edge
(u_{i},u^{\prime}_{j})
belongs to path
P_{i^{*},j^{*}}
, otherwise we set we set
\bar{w}_{B}(u_{i},u_{j})=2
.
It is easy to verify that for any matrix
X
and for any index pair
(i^{*},j^{*})
,
\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B}
are valid partial metrics and are complementary.

We now describe the reduction.
Let
\pi
be a one-way protocol for the
\mathsf{TSP}_{2}
problem. We construct a one-way protocol
\pi_{\textsf{Index}}
for the
Index
problem as follows.
Assume Alice and Bob are given an instance
(X^{A},X^{B})
for the
Index
problem, in which
X^{A}
is a
0/1
vector of length
\binom{k+1}{2}
and
X^{B}
is an index in
[\binom{k+1}{2}]
. Note that it is equivalent Alice is given a symmetric matrix
X
with all diagonal entries
0
and Bob is given a pair
(i^{*},j^{*})
of distinct indices of
[k+1]
. We then let Alice construct the partial metric
\bar{w}_{A}
using matrix
X
and Bob construct the partial metric
\bar{w}_{B}
using the index pair
(i^{*},j^{*})
as described above, and then let them run the protocol
\pi
on the pair
\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B}
of partial metrics. If the output of
\pi
is
2k+1
, then we return
0
as the output of
\pi_{\textsf{Index}}
; if the output of
\pi
is
2k+2
, then we return
1
as the output of
\pi_{\textsf{Index}}
.

We now show that, if
\pi
is a one-way protocol for the
\mathsf{TSP}_{2}
problem, then
\pi_{\textsf{Index}}
is a one-way protocol for the
Index
problem as follows. Note that, from the construction of partial metrics
\bar{w}_{A},\bar{w}_{B}
, in the metric
w=\bar{w}_{A}\cup\bar{w}_{B}
, there is a path
P_{i^{*},j^{*}}
of weight-
1
edges, so if
w(u_{i^{*}},u_{j^{*}})=1
, then
w
contains a Hamiltonian cycle of weight-
1
edges. and therefore
\mathsf{TSP}(w)=2k+1
. On the other hand, since
w(u_{i^{*}},u_{j^{*}})\leq 2
,
\mathsf{TSP}(w)\leq 2k+2
, and it is easy to verify that
\mathsf{TSP}(w)=2k+1
implies
w(u_{i^{*}},u_{j^{*}})=1
.
Therefore,
\mathsf{TSP}(w)=2k+1
iff
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=0
, and
\mathsf{TSP}(w)=2k+1
iff
X_{i^{*},j^{*}}=1
, so
\pi_{\textsf{Index}}
is a one-way protocol for the
Index
problem. Now
Theorem

15
follows from the classic communication complexity lower bound on one-way protocols for the
Index
problem.

Part II
Query Algorithm I

In this part we present our algorithm in the query model, that, given a distance oracle to a metric
w
with the promise that the complete weighted graph with edge weights given by
w
(denoted by
G_{w}
) contains a minimum spanning tree consisting of only weight-
1
edges, estimates the value of
\mathsf{TSP}(w)
to within a factor of
(2-\varepsilon_{0})
, for some universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, by performing
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries to the oracle, thus establishing
Theorem

7
.
We start with some additional preliminaries used in this part, then introduce some crucial subroutines, and finally present the query algorithm and its analysis.

Throughout this part, we denote by
G_{1}
the subgraph of
G_{w}
induced by all weight-
1
edges.

1
Preliminaries

Let
G
be an unweighted graph.
For a pair
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices in
G
, we denote by
\textnormal{{dist}}_{G}(v,v^{\prime})
the length (the number of edges) of the shortest path in
G
connecting
v
to
v^{\prime}
.

Let
T
be a tree.
We say that a subgraph
P
of
T
is an
induced subpath
of
T
, iff
P
is a path, and all internal vertices of
P
have degree
2
in
T
. We say that
P
is a
maximal induced subpath
if it is not a proper subpath of any other induced subpath of
T
. Therefore, for any pair
P,P^{\prime}
of maximal induced subpaths of
T
,
P
and
P^{\prime}
are internally vertex-disjoint. The following observation is immediate.

Observation 1.1
.

The number of maximal induced subpaths of a tree is at most twice the number of leaves in the tree.

E-blocks and e-block tree.

We say that an edge
e
of a connected graph
G
is a
bridge
, iff
G\setminus e
is not connected.
A graph is
2
-edge-connected
if it does not contain any bridges. Let
G
be a graph and let
H
be an induced subgraph of
G
, we say that
H
is a
2
-edge-connected block
(or an
e-block
for brevity), iff
H
is a maximal
2
-edge-connected induced subgraph of
G
(namely, there is no other
2
-edge-connected induced subgraph
H^{\prime}\subseteq G
, such that
H\subsetneq H^{\prime}
).
We denote by
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
the set of e-blocks of
G
. Clearly, e-blocks in
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
are vertex-disjoint.

The
e-block tree

{\mathcal{T}}_{G}
of a connected graph
G
is defined to be the graph obtained from
G
by contracting each e-block
B
of
{\mathcal{B}}(G)
into a vertex
v_{B}
.
It is easy to see that, if
G
is connected, then
{\mathcal{T}}_{G}
is a tree.
We call such a vertex
v_{B}
that corresponds to an e-block in
G
an
e-block vertex
.

We use the following results in previous work and easy propositions.

Lemma 1.1

(Theorem 8 in
[
CKK20
]
)
.

There is a randomized algorithm, that, given any simple graph
G
on
n
vertices and any parameter
\epsilon>0
, with probability
1-e^{-\Omega(n)}
, estimates the size of some maximal matching of
G
to within an additive error of
\epsilon n
, by performing
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5}/\epsilon^{2})
pair queries.

Lemma 1.2

(Corollary of Lemma 2.11 in
[
CKK20
]
)
.

Let
H
be a connected graph on
n
vertices. If
H
contains a matching
M
of size
\alpha n
such that at most
\beta n
edges of
M
are bridges of
H
, then the graphic-TSP cost of
H
is at most
(2-\frac{2}{3}(\alpha-\beta))n
.

Proposition 1.1
.

If
G_{1}
is connected, then
n\leq\emph{\textnormal{{TSP}}}(w)\leq 2n-2
.

Proof.

On one hand, let
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},v_{1})
be the optimal TSP-tour that traverses all vertices of
V
. Since
w(v_{i},v_{i+1})\geq 1
for all
1\leq i\leq n
, the cost of the tour is at least
n
. On the other hand, let
T
be a spanning tree of
G_{1}
and consider the Euler tour that traverses each edge of
T
exactly twice. It is easy to see that such a tour can be easily shortcut into a TSP-tour that traverses all vertices of
V
, with at most the same cost
2n-2
.
∎

Proposition 1.2
.

Let
m
be the size of a maximum matching in
G_{1}
, then
\emph{\textnormal{{TSP}}}(w)\geq 2n-2m
.

Proof.

Let
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},v_{1})
be an optimal TSP-tour that traverses all vertices of
V
. Observe that the edges in set
M_{o}=\left\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\mid 1\leq i\leq n,i\text{ odd}\right\}
form a matching in
G_{1}
, and so do the edges in set
M_{e}=\left\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\mid 1\leq i\leq n,i\text{ even}\right\}
. Since the size of a maximum matching in
G_{1}
is at most
m
, the set
M_{o}
contains at most
m
edges of weight
1
, and so does the set
M_{e}
. Therefore,
at most
2m
values of
\left\{w(v_{i},v_{i+1})\right\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
are
1
, and it follows that
\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}w(v_{i},v_{i+1})\geq 2(n-2m)+2m=2n-2m
.
∎

Proposition 1.3
.

If
G_{1}
contains
L
degree-
1
vertices, then
\emph{\textnormal{{TSP}}}(w)\geq n+L/2
.

Proof.

Let
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n},v_{1})
be an optimal TSP-tour. If
v_{i}
is a degree-
1
vertex in
G_{1}
, then at most one of
v_{i-1},v_{i+1}
is its neighbor in
G_{1}
, so at least one of the pairs
(v_{i-1},v_{i}),(v_{i},v_{i+1})
has distance at least
2
. We say that this pair is
in charge of
vertex
v_{i}
. Clearly, each pair is in charge of at most two degree-
1
vertices in
G
. Therefore, at least
L/2
pairs in
\left\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\right\}
has distance at least
2
, and it follows that
\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}w(v_{i},v_{i+1})\geq n+L/2
.
∎

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
T
be a tree on
n
vertices with
L
leaves. Then
T
contains a matching of size at least
(n-L)/2
.

Proof.

We root
T
at an arbitrary vertex, and then iteratively construct a matching of
T
as follows.
Throughout, we maintain a subset
M
of edges of
T
, that is initialized to be
\emptyset
, and each vertex of
T
is either marked matched or unmatched.
The algorithm proceeds in iterations. While there is an unmatched vertex, such that all its ancestors are matched and some of its descendants remain unmatched, we match this vertex with any of its unmatched descendants (by adding the edge connecting them into
M
), and mark both of them matched.
It is easy to see that, at the end of this algorithm, all vertices that are left unmatched are leaves of
T
. Therefore, the matching that we constructed has size at least
(n-L)/2
.
∎

Lemma 1.3
.

There is an efficient algorithm, that, given any constant
\epsilon>0
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
, either (correctly) claims that the number of degree-1 vertices of
G_{1}
is greater than
\epsilon n/2
, or (correctly) claims that the number of degree-1 vertices of
G_{1}
is at most
2\epsilon n
. Moreover, the algorithm performs
O(n/\epsilon)
distance queries.

Proof.

We sample
L=200\log n/\epsilon
vertices from
V
uniformly at random, and for each sampled vertex
v
, we check whether or not
v
is a degree-
1
vertex in
G_{1}
by performing pair queries
(v,v^{\prime})
for all
v^{\prime}\in V
. Let
L^{\prime}
be the number of degree-
1
vertices vertices in our sample set. If
L^{\prime}\geq\epsilon L
, we claim that the number of degree-
1
vertices is at least
\epsilon n/2
(a positive report), otherwise we claim that the number of degree-
1
vertices is at most
2\epsilon n
(a negative report).
It now remains to show the correctness of the algorithm. First assume that the number of degree-
1
vertices in
G_{1}
is at least
\epsilon n/2
. In this case, by Chernoff bound,

\Pr[L^{\prime}\geq 2\epsilon L]\leq\exp\left(-10\log n\right)=O(n^{-10});\mbox{ and }\Pr[L^{\prime}\leq\epsilon L/2]\leq\exp\left(-10\log n\right)=O(n^{-10}).

We then assume that the number of degree-
1
vertices in
G_{1}
is at most
\epsilon n/2
. Similarly, by Chernoff bound, the probability that the algorithm makes an incorrect claim is
\Pr[L^{\prime}\geq 2\epsilon L]\leq\exp\left(-10\log n\right)=O(n^{-10})
.
Therefore, with high probability, the algorithm’s claim is correct.
∎

2
Subroutines

In this section we provide four crucial subroutines in our query algorithm of this part.
The first and the second focus on exploring the neighborhood of a vertex up to a certain size, and then intuitively compute the difference between TSP cost and MST cost within this neighborhood.
The third and the fourth focus on efficiently reconstruct long induced paths of an MST and properly connect them into a TSP tour.

2.1
Subroutine
\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\cdot)

Definition 2.1

(Light Vertices)
.

We say that a vertex
v
is
\ell
-light
for some integer
\ell>0
, iff there is an edge
e\in\delta_{G_{1}}(v)
, such that
(i)
e
is a bridge in
G_{1}
; and (ii) the connected component of graph
G_{1}\setminus e
that contains
v
has at most
\ell
vertices. We call such an edge a
witness
of
v
.

It is easy to verify that, if
\ell<n/2
, then each
\ell
-light vertex has exactly one witness.
We will assume from now on that
\ell<n/2
.
We denote by
e_{v}
the unique witness edge of an
\ell
-light vertex
v
, and denote by
S_{v}
the connected component of
G_{1}\setminus e_{v}
that contains
v
.

Definition 2.2

(Maximal Light Vertices)
.

We say that
v
is
maximal
\ell
-light
, iff
v
is
\ell
-light, and there is no other
\ell
-light vertex
u
such that the subgraph
S_{u}
contains
v
. In this case we also say that
S_{v}
is a
maximal
\ell
-light subgraph
of
G_{1}
.

We denote by
L_{\ell}
the
set of all maximal
\ell
-light vertices
.

We now describe a subroutine that, given a vertex
v\in V
and an integer
s
, explores the neighborhood of
v
in graph
G_{1}
up to size
s
.
This subroutine can help us find the maximal
s
-light subgraph of
G_{1}
that
v
lies in, if it indeed lies in any such subgraph, and will be a crucial building block for our TSP-cost estimation algorithm.

The subroutine is called
\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\cdot)
, and is defined as follows.
The input to the subroutine consists of: (i) a vertex
v\in V
; and (ii) an integer
1\leq s\leq n
. The output of the subroutine
\textsf{Local}(v,s)
is a signal
Success
/
Fail
indicating the termination status of the subroutine, and if the status is
Success
, the subroutine also returns a connected subgraph of
G_{1}
that contains the vertex
v
.

The subroutine
\textsf{Local}(v,s)
proceeds in two phases.
We now describe the first phase, called the
exploring phase
.
Intuitively, in this step we perform a breadth-first search in
G_{1}
starting at vertex
v
and maintain a tree
T
rooted at
v
throughout. Initially,
T
contains a single vertex
v
as the root.
The exploring phase consists of several
exploring steps
. An exploring step at a vertex
\hat{v}
includes: (i) performing distance queries
w(\hat{v},v^{\prime})
for all vertices
v^{\prime}\notin V(T)
; (ii) for all vertices
v^{\prime}
with
w(\hat{v},v^{\prime})=1
, adding
v^{\prime}
and the edge
(v^{\prime},\hat{v})
to
T
(so
v^{\prime}
is a child vertex of
\hat{v}
in
T
). We say that a vertex is
explored
iff we have performed an exploring step at it.
In the exploring phase, we start by performing an exploring step at
v
. Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
be the new vertices that are added to
T
in this phase, we then perform sequentially, for each
1\leq i\leq k
, an exploring step at
v_{i}
.
We then iteratively, for each
1\leq i\leq k
, take a descendant of
v_{i}
that is not yet explored, and perform an exploring step at it.
It is clear that, after each exploring step, the number of explored vertices in
T
increases by
1
. The exploring phase is terminated once the number of explored vertices reaches
2s
.

In the second phase, which is called the
checking phase
, we find the least common ancestor in
T
of all vertices in
T
that are not yet explored.
Clearly, there is at least one such vertex, since otherwise
G_{1}
is not connected.
Let
\hat{v}
be the vertex we get. If
\hat{v}=v
, then we return
Fail
.
Assume now that
\hat{v}\neq v
, and assume that
\hat{v}\in V(T_{v_{i}})
for some first-level vertex
v_{i}
in
T
(recall that
T_{v_{i}}
is the subtree of
T
rooted at
v_{i}
).
If
|V(T\setminus T_{v_{i}})|>s
, then we return
Fail
.
If
|V(T\setminus T_{v_{i}})|\leq s
, then return
Success
and consider the tree path in
T
connecting
v_{i}
to
\hat{v}
.
Let
v^{*}
be the vertex on this path with lowest level, such that
|V(T\setminus T_{v^{*}})|\leq s
. We denote
S=V(T\setminus T_{v^{*}})
.
We further perform queries between every pair of vertices in
S
, and output
G_{1}[S]
, the subgraph of
G_{1}
induced by vertices in
S
.
This completes the description of the subroutine.

The following observations are immediate.

Observation 2.1
.

For every vertex
v\in V
and every integer
1\leq s\leq n
, subroutine
\emph{\textsf{Local}}(v,s)
performs at most
O(ns)
distance queries.

Observation 2.2
.

If the vertex
v
is
\ell
-light, then the subroutine
\emph{\textsf{Local}}(v,\ell)
returns
Success
and outputs the maximal
\ell
-light subgraph of
G_{1}
that contains
v
;
otherwise the subroutine
\emph{\textsf{Local}}(v,\ell)
returns
Fail
.

2.2
Subgraph Reconfiguration

Fix a parameter
0<\ell<n/2
and a vertex
v\in L_{\ell}
(the set of all maximal
\ell
-light vertices in
G_{1}
), and consider the maximal
\ell
-light subgraph
S_{v}
of
G_{1}
. For each vertex
u\in V(S_{v})
, we define its
out-reach distance
, denoted as
\textsf{ord}(u)
, to be the minimum distance between
u
and a vertex of
V\setminus V(S_{v})
, namely
\textsf{ord}(u)=\min\left\{w(u,u^{\prime})\mid u^{\prime}\notin V(S_{v})\right\}
.

Definition 2.3

(Subgraph Reconfiguration)
.

A
reconfiguration
of a subgraph
S_{v}
is defined to be a multi-set of unordered pairs of vertices in
V(S_{v})
that induce a connected graph on
V(S_{v})
.
Equivalently, a reconfiguration of
S_{v}
can be viewed as a connected multi-graph on vertices of
S_{v}
, whose edges may or may not belong to
S_{v}
.

Let
R
be a reconfiguration of
S_{v}
.
We will sometimes refer to
R
as the (multi)-graph induced by edges of
R
. The vertices of this multigraph can be partitioned into two sets: the set
V_{0}(R)
contains all vertices with even degree, and the set
V_{1}(R)
contains all vertices with odd degree.
The
cost
of
R
is defined as

\textnormal{{cost}}(R)=\sum_{(u,u^{\prime})\in R}w(u,u^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in V_{1}(R)}\textsf{ord}(u)-(|V(S_{v})|-1).

The
optimal reconfiguration cost
of
S_{v}
, that we denote by
\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})
, is defined to be the minimum cost of a reconfiguration of subgraph
S_{v}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

There is an algorithm, that, given any maximal
\ell
-light subgraph
S
, computes a reconfiguration
R
that achieves optimal (minimum) reconfiguration cost, by performing
O(n\ell)
queries.

Proof.

We first query the distances between all pairs
(u,u^{\prime})
of vertices with
u\in V(S)
and
u^{\prime}\in V
. We then enumerate all possible reconfigurations and compute the cost of each of them, from the distance information we acquired. Finally, we return the configuration with minimum cost.
∎

2.3
Subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)

We now describe a subroutine that, given a vertex
v
and several parameters, explores the neighborhood of
v
in graph
G_{1}
up to a certain distance, by performing a certain number of distance queries.
As we will see, the subroutine
BFS

is different from the subroutine
Local
, in the sense that the subroutine
BFS

focuses on checking whether or not the neighborhood of a vertex
v
in
G
has a path-like structure, instead of checking whether or not the vertex belongs to a maximal light subgraph.
We will show that this checking can be done in a distinctly more efficient manner than the subroutine
Local
.

The subroutine is called
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
, and is defined as follows.
The input to the subroutine consists of: (i) a vertex
v\in V
; (ii) an integer
1\leq h\leq n
representing the depth of the BFS-tree that the subroutine is aiming for; (iii) an integer
1\leq q\leq n^{2}
representing the number of queries that the subroutine is allowed to perform; and (iv) a real number
\alpha>1
for determining the termination status of ths subroutine. The output of the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
consists of: (i) a signal
Success
/
Fail
indicating the termination status of the subroutine; and (ii) if the status is
Success
, a path
P_{v}
of length
2h
and a subgraph
H_{v}
of
G_{1}
, both containing the vertex
v
.

The subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
proceeds in
h
stages. For each
1\leq i\leq h
, in the
i
th stage, we explore all vertices that are at distance exactly
i
from
v
in
G_{1}
by performing distance queries.
Throughout the subroutine, each vertex is either marked as a
level-
i
vertex for some
0\leq i\leq h
, or is marked as
unexplored
. Initially, the input vertex
v
is marked as a level-
0
vertex and all other vertices are marked as unexplored.
We will also maintain a tree
\hat{T}^{h}_{v}
, that initially contains a single vertex
v
and no edges.
Along with exploring vertices of
V
, we also keep track of the queries that we have already performed so far, and utilize them for efficiently determining which vertices to explore in the next stage.
Specifically, throughout the subroutine, we maintain, for each vertex
\hat{v}\in V
, a set

{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{v})=\left\{v^{\prime}\mid\text{the distance between vertices }\hat{v}\text{ and }v^{\prime}\text{ has been queried}\right\},

that records all distance information we have obtained about vertex
\hat{v}
.
Initially, all sets
\left\{{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{v})\right\}_{\hat{v}\in V}
are
\emptyset
, and the sets evolve as the subroutine proceeds.

In the first stage, we query distances
w(v,v^{\prime})
for all vertices
v^{\prime}\in V
. We then mark all vertices
v^{\prime}
with
w(v,v^{\prime})=1
as
level-1
vertices, and add the edge
(v,v^{\prime})
to
\hat{T}^{h}_{v}
for each level-
1
vertex
v^{\prime}
.

We now fix some
1<i\leq h
and describe the
i
th stage of the subroutine.
Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{p}
be all level-
(i\!-\!1)
vertices, (indexed arbitrarily). We sequentially process vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{p}
as follows. Consider a vertex
v_{j}
with
1\leq j\leq p
.
We would like to discover all unexplored vertices that are at distance
1
from
v_{j}
by performing as few queries as possible.
Specifically, for each unexplored vertex
\hat{v}
, we query all distances in
\left\{w(v^{\prime},v_{j})\mid v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{v})\right\}
and then compute

X(v_{j},\hat{v})=\max\left\{w(v^{\prime},\hat{v})-w(v^{\prime},v_{j})\mid v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{v})\right\}
.
We then query distance
w(v_{j},\hat{v})
for all unexplored vertices
\hat{v}
with
X(v_{j},\hat{v})\leq 1
.
We then mark, among the above vertices, those vertices
\hat{v}
with
w(v_{j},\hat{v})=1
as level-
i
vertices, and then add the edge
(v_{j},\hat{v})
to tree
\hat{T}^{h}_{v}
.
This finishes the description of processing a level-
(i\!-\!1)
vertex
v_{j}
, and also finishes the description of the
i
th stage.

The subroutine also maintains a counter that records the number of distance queries performed so far. If the counter reaches
q
before all
h
stages are finished, then we abort the subroutine and return
Fail
. Otherwise, let
T^{h}_{v}
be the resulting tree that we obtain. The output of the subroutine distinguishes between the following cases.

Case 1.
If
|V(T^{h}_{v})|\leq\alpha h
, and the tree
T^{h}_{v}
contains exactly two level-
h
vertices
v^{*},v^{**}
, and the
v
-
v^{*}
path and the
v
-
v^{**}
path in
T^{h}_{v}
are internally vertex-disjoint, then we return
Success
.
We denote by
P_{v}
the
v^{*}
-
v^{**}
path in tree
T^{h}_{v}
, and call it the
support path
of
v
.
We proceed to query all distances between vertices of
V(T^{h}_{v})
. Note that this takes at most
\alpha^{2}h^{2}
additional queries.
From these queries, we are able to recover the subgraph of
G_{1}
induced by vertices of
V(T^{h}_{v})
, which we denote by
H_{v}
.
We then return the path
P_{v}
and the subgraph
H_{v}
.
We say that vertices
v^{*},v^{**}
are
interface vertices
of
P_{v}
and
H_{v}
.

Case 2.
If Case 1 does not happen, then we say that Case 2 happens. Equivalently, Case 2 happens iff either
|V(T^{h}_{v})|>\alpha h
holds, or tree
T^{h}_{v}
does not contain exactly two level-
h
vertices, or tree
T^{h}_{v}
does contain exactly two level-
h
vertices
v^{*},v^{**}
but the
v
-
v^{*}
path and the the
v
-
v^{**}
path in
T^{h}_{v}
share a vertex other than
v
. In this case we return
Fail
.

This completes the description of the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
.
We use the following observations.

Observation 2.3
.

The subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
performs at most
q+\alpha^{2}h^{2}
distance queries.

Proof.

If the number of performed queries reaches
q
before all
h
stages are finished, then the subroutine is aborted and has performed in total at most
q
queries. If the subroutine ends in Case 1, then at most
\alpha^{2}h^{2}
additional queries are performed. If the subroutine ends in Case 2, then no more queries are performed.
2.3
now follows.
∎

We will use following observation in analyzing our TSP-cost estimation algorithm in
Section

3
.

Observation 2.4
.

For every vertex
u\notin V(T^{h}_{v})
and for every pair
x,x^{\prime}
of vertices such that the distances
w(u,x)
and
w(u,x^{\prime})
are queried by the subroutine,
w(x,x^{\prime})\geq\min_{u^{\prime}\in V(T^{h}_{v})}\left\{w(u,u^{\prime})\right\}-1
.

Proof.

We define
d_{u}=\min_{u^{\prime}\in V(T^{h}_{v})}\left\{w(u,u^{\prime})\right\}
for every vertex
u\notin V(T^{h}_{v})
.
Assume the contrast that there exists some
u\notin V(T^{h}_{v})
and some pair
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(u)
such that
w(x,x^{\prime})\leq d_{u}-2
.
Note that, since
u\notin V(T^{h}_{v})
, the set
{\mathcal{Q}}(u)
only contains vertices of
T^{h}_{v}
(so
x,x^{\prime}\in V(T^{h}_{v})
), and the distance
w(x,u)
(
w(x^{\prime},u)
, resp.) may only be queried in the iteration of processing
x
(
x^{\prime}
, resp.) to find its children in constructing the tree
T^{h}_{v}
.
Assume without loss of generality that vertex
x
was processed before
x^{\prime}
, so the distance
w(x,u)
was queried before the distance
w(x^{\prime},u)
. Consider now the iteration of processing
x^{\prime}
.
Recall that we only query distances
w(x^{\prime},\hat{v})
for all unexplored vertices
\hat{v}
with
X(x^{\prime},\hat{v})\leq 1
, where
X(x^{\prime},\hat{v})=\max\left\{w(z,\hat{v})-w(z,x^{\prime})\mid z\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\hat{v})\right\}
. Therefore, if the distance
w(x^{\prime},u)
is queried in this iteration, then
\max\left\{w(z,u)-w(z,x)\mid z\in{\mathcal{Q}}(u)\right\}\leq 1
. However, since at this moment
x\in{\mathcal{Q}}(u)
, so
\max\left\{w(z,u)-w(z,x^{\prime})\mid z\in{\mathcal{Q}}(u)\right\}\geq w(x,u)-w(x,x^{\prime})\geq d_{u}-(d_{u}-2)=2
, a contradiction.
∎

2.4
Computing Optimal Proper Tours

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}
be a set of vertex-disjoint paths in
G_{1}
, and we denote
V^{\prime}=\bigcup_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}}V(Q)
.
We say that a tour is a
V^{\prime}
-tour
iff it traverses all vertices of
V^{\prime}
exactly once.
We say that a
V^{\prime}
-tour
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r},v_{1})
is a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour
, iff for each
1\leq i\leq r
, either
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
is an edge on some path of
{\mathcal{Q}}
, or
v_{i}
is an endpoint of some path
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
, and
v_{i+1}
is an endpoint of some other path
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Claim 2.1
.

The minimum cost of a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour can be computed by performing
O(n+|{\mathcal{Q}}|^{2})
queries.

Proof.

We first query, for each path
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
, the distance between all pairs of consecutive vertices on
Q
.
This takes in total at most
n
queries.
Let set
\hat{V}
contain, for each path
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
, both endpoints of path
Q
. We then query the distances between all pairs of vertices in
\hat{V}
.
This takes in total
O(|{\mathcal{Q}}|^{2})
queries.
We then enumerate all
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tours that traverses all vertices of
V^{\prime}
and compute the cost of each of them, from the distance information we acquired. Finally, we return the minimum cost of a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour.
∎

Claim 2.2
.

If
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq(1+{\epsilon})n
,
|E({\mathcal{Q}})|\geq(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})n
, and for all pairs
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices belonging to distinct paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}
,
w(v,v^{\prime})=1
implies that
v
and
v^{\prime}
are endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
that they belong to, respectively, then the minimum cost of a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour is at most
(1+3\varepsilon+4{\epsilon}^{\prime})n+2|{\mathcal{Q}}|
.

Proof.

Let
\pi^{*}
be the optimal TSP-tour that visits all vertices of
V
. We will construct a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour that visits all vertices of
V^{\prime}
with cost at most
(1+3\varepsilon+4{\epsilon}^{\prime})n+2|{\mathcal{Q}}|
as follows.

Let
\pi_{1}
be the
V^{\prime}
-tour obtained from
\pi^{*}
by deleting all vertices of
V\setminus V^{\prime}
from
\pi^{*}
, and keeping all vertices of
V^{\prime}
in the same order as in
\pi^{*}
.
Clearly,
\pi_{1}
is a
V^{\prime}
-tour, and
\textnormal{{cost}}(\pi_{1})\leq\textnormal{{cost}}(\pi^{*})\leq(1+\varepsilon)n
due to the triangle inequality.
We denote by
G^{\prime}
the graph on
V^{\prime}
that contains every consecutive pair of vertices in
\pi_{1}
as an edge. Put in other words, if
\pi_{1}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r},v_{1})
, then
E(G^{\prime})=\left\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\mid 1\leq i\leq r\right\}
.
Since
\textnormal{{cost}}(\pi_{1})\leq(1+\varepsilon)n
, and
|V^{\prime}|\geq|E({\mathcal{Q}})|\geq(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})n
, at most
(\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime})
edges of
G^{\prime}
have weight more than
1
.
Since for all pairs
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices lying on distinct paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}
,
w(v,v^{\prime})=1
implies that
v
and
v^{\prime}
are endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
that they belong to, at most
|{\mathcal{Q}}|
edges in
G^{\prime}
have weight
1
but do not belong to
E({\mathcal{Q}})
. Altogether,
at most
|{\mathcal{Q}}|+(\epsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime})n
edges of graph
G^{\prime}
do not belong to
E({\mathcal{Q}})
.

We now iteratively modify the graph
G^{\prime}
to obtain a graph that contains all edges of
E({\mathcal{Q}})
, as follows.
Throughout, we will maintain a graph
\hat{G}
on
V^{\prime}
, that is initialized to be
G^{\prime}
. We will ensure that, at any time,
\hat{G}
is the union of a set of vertex-disjoint cycles. Since
\pi_{1}
is a
V^{\prime}
-tour, from the definition of
G^{\prime}
, this condition holds at the beginning of the algorithm.
The algorithm continues to be executed as long as
E({\mathcal{Q}})\not\subseteq E(\hat{G})
.
We now describe an iteration.
Let
e=(u,u^{\prime})
be an edge in
E({\mathcal{Q}})\setminus E(\hat{G})
. Assume that edge
e
belongs to a path
Q
of
{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Let
u_{1},u_{2}
be the neighbors of
u
in
\hat{G}
.
Since
u_{1},u_{2}\neq u
, at least one of the edges
(u_{1},u),(u_{2},u)
does not belong to
Q
. Assume without loss of generality that
(u_{1},u)
does not belong to
Q
. We define
u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2}
similarly for
u^{\prime}
and assume without loss of generality that
(u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime})
does not belong to
Q
.
We then remove the edges
(u_{1},u),(u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime})
from
\hat{G}
, and add the new edges
(u_{1},u^{\prime}_{1}),(u,u^{\prime})
to
\hat{G}
. This completes the description of an iteration. Clearly, after this iteration, the degree for every vertex in
\hat{G}
is still
2
, so the invariant that
\hat{G}
is the union of disjoint cycles still holds.
Note that after every iteration, the size of
E({\mathcal{Q}})\setminus E(\hat{G})
decreases by at least one. Therefore, the algorithm will eventually terminate in at most
|{\mathcal{Q}}|+(\epsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime})n
iterations.

Let
G^{\prime\prime}
be the graph that we obtain at the end of the algorithm.
Let
G^{\prime\prime}
be the union of disjoint cycles
C_{1},\ldots,C_{t}
.
Clearly,
G^{\prime\prime}
contains all edges of
E({\mathcal{Q}})
, each path
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
is entirely contained in some cycle
C_{j}
.
Note that, after each iteration, the cost of
\hat{G}
, which is defined as
\textnormal{{cost}}(\hat{G})=\sum_{(u,u^{\prime})\in E(\hat{G})}w(u,u^{\prime})
, increases by at most
2
. This is because, from the triangle inequality,

w(u_{1},u^{\prime}_{1})+w(u,u^{\prime})-w(u_{1},u)-w(u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime})\leq 2D(u,u^{\prime})=2
.
Therefore,
\textnormal{{cost}}(G^{\prime\prime})\leq\textnormal{{cost}}(G^{\prime})+2(|{\mathcal{Q}}|+(\epsilon+\varepsilon^{\prime})n)\leq(1+3\varepsilon+2{\epsilon}^{\prime})n+2|{\mathcal{Q}}|
.

We now use cycles
C_{1},\ldots,C_{t}
and edges of
G_{1}
to construct a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour, as follows.
Let
\hat{G}_{1}
be the graph obtained from
G_{1}
by contracting, for each
1\leq j\leq t
, all vertices of
C_{j}
into a single node, that we denote by
v_{C_{j}}
.
Note that, since
G_{1}
is connected and
|E({\mathcal{Q}})|\geq(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})n
,
\hat{G}_{1}
is also connected and contains at most
{\epsilon}^{\prime}n
vertices. Let
\hat{E}
be the set of edges of a Steiner tree in
\hat{G}_{1}
that spans all nodes
v_{C_{1}},\ldots,v_{C_{t}}
with minimum cost, so
|\hat{E}|\leq{\epsilon}^{\prime}n
. Note that each edge of
\hat{E}
is also an edge in
G_{1}
. Moreover, since for all pairs
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices lying on distinct paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}
,
w(v,v)=1
implies that
v
and
v^{\prime}
are endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
that they belong to, it is easy to see that each edge of
\hat{E}
has to connect an endpoint of some path in
{\mathcal{Q}}
to another endpoint of some other path in
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Consider the graph
G^{\prime\prime}\cup\hat{E}
. It is easy to see that, we can construct a tour
\pi
that visits all vertices of
V^{\prime}
and endpoints of all edges in
\hat{E}
, by using each edge of
G^{\prime\prime}
at most once and each edge of
\hat{E}
at most twice, such that for each path
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
, all vertices of
Q
appear consecutively in
\pi_{2}
in the same order as they appear on
Q
.
Moreover, such a tour can be shortcut into a proper
V^{\prime}
-tour
\pi
with at most the same cost. See Figure
6
for an illustration.
Therefore,
\textnormal{{cost}}(\pi)\leq\textnormal{{cost}}(\pi_{2})\leq\textnormal{{cost}}(G^{\prime\prime})+2|\hat{E}|\leq(1+3\varepsilon+4{\epsilon}^{\prime})n+2|{\mathcal{Q}}|
.

(a)

Before: Paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}
are shown in green, cycles are shown in red, and edges of
\hat{E}
are shown in blue.

(b)

After: The tour
\pi
that visits all vertices of
V^{\prime}
and endpoints of all edges in
\hat{E}
is shown in pink.
\pi
can be further shortcut into a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour in a standard way.

Figure 6:
An illustration of constructing a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour.

∎

3
The Algorithm and its Analysis

In this section we present our algorithm and its analysis, completing the proof of
Theorem

7
.
At a high level, our algorithm considers the “bottom part” (light subtrees) and the “top part” (the subtree obtained by peeling off all light subtrees) of an MST separately. If the bottom part is heavy, then we use the first two subroutines to explore random neighborhoods of the bottom parts, and then derive locally either
\mathsf{TSP}
is bounded away from
2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
or bounded away from
{\mathsf{MST}}
. If the “bottom part” is light, then almost all cost of the MST belongs to its top part, and then we use the third and the fourth subroutines to reconstruct the MST and then estimate
\mathsf{TSP}
accordingly.

Parameters:
\epsilon=2^{-100},\hat{\varepsilon}=2^{-40}
,
q=n/\hat{\varepsilon}^{2}
,
\ell=100\sqrt{n}
,
h=\hat{\varepsilon}\ell/200
and
\alpha=10/\hat{\varepsilon}
.

1.

We first use the algorithm from
Lemma

1.3
to compare the number of degree-
1
vertices of
G_{1}
with
\epsilon n/40
.
If the algorithm reports that the number of degree-
1
vertices of
G_{1}
is greater than
\epsilon n/80
, then we return
2n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.
If the algorithm reports that the number of degree-
1
vertices of
G_{1}
is at most
\epsilon n/20
, go to
2
.

2.

We sample
1/\epsilon^{2}
vertices (with replacement) from
V
independently and uniformly at random, and perform the subroutine
\textsf{Local}(v,\ell)
for each sampled vertex
v
.
If at least
\epsilon
-fraction of the subroutines
\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\ell)
return
Success
.
Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{1/\epsilon^{2}}
be the sampled vertices.
For each
1\leq i\leq 1/\epsilon^{2}
, if the subroutine
\textsf{Local}(v_{i},\ell)
returns
Fail
, then we let
\gamma_{i}=0
. Otherwise, the subroutine
\textsf{Local}(v_{i},\ell)
returns
Success
and a maximal
\ell
-light subgraph
S_{i}
that contains
v_{i}
. We apply the algorithm in
Lemma

2.1
to
S_{i}
to compute the optimal reconfiguration cost
\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{i})
of
S_{i}
, and then let
\gamma_{i}=\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{i})/|V(S_{i})|
.
We now compare
\sum_{1\leq i\leq 1/\epsilon^{2}}\gamma_{i}
with
1/80\epsilon
.
If
\sum_{1\leq i\leq 1/\epsilon^{2}}\gamma_{i}\leq 1/80\epsilon
, then we return
(2-{\epsilon}/20)n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
; otherwise we return
2n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.
If less than
\epsilon
-fraction of the subroutines
\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\ell)
return
Success
, go to
3
.

3.

We sample
(100\cdot n\log n)/h
vertices (with replacement) from
V
independently and uniformly at random, and perform the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
for each sampled vertex
v
.
Let
\tilde{V}
be the set of sampled vertices that the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Success
upon.
Recall that the subroutine
BFS

also returns a path
P_{v}
and a graph
H_{v}
.
We define graphs
Z=(V,\bigcup_{v\in\tilde{V}}E(P_{v}))
and
H=(V,\bigcup_{v\in\tilde{V}}E(H_{v}))
.
We then define vertex sets
V_{\textsf{chunk}}=\bigcup_{v\in\tilde{V}}V(H_{v}\setminus P_{v})
and
V_{\textsf{isolated}}=V\setminus(V_{\textsf{chunk}}\cup V(Z))
.
If
|V_{\textsf{isolated}}|\geq 10\hat{\epsilon}n
, go to
4
.
If
|V_{\textsf{chunk}}|\geq 10\hat{\epsilon}n
, go to
5
.
Otherwise,
go to
6
.

4.

We use the algorithm from
Lemma

1.1
with parameter
\hat{\epsilon}/100
to obtain an estimate of the size of some maximal matching of
G_{1}[V_{\textsf{isolated}}]
.
If the estimate is at most
\hat{\epsilon}n
, then we return
2n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.
Otherwise, we return
(2-\hat{\epsilon}/200)n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.

5.

We compute the maximum size of a matching consisting of only edges that belong to some e-block of
H
.
If the size is at most
2\hat{\epsilon}n
, then we return
2n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.
Otherwise, we return
(2-\hat{\epsilon})n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.

6.

We use the following claim.

Claim 3.1
.

We can efficiently find a set
{\mathcal{Q}}
of vertex-disjoint induced subpaths of
Z
, such that each path of
{\mathcal{Q}}
has length at least
\Omega(\hat{\epsilon}^{2}h/\log^{2}n)
, and
\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}}|E(Q)|\geq(1-40\hat{\epsilon})n
.

Proof.

Recall that graph
Z
is obtained from taking the union of paths in
{\mathcal{P}}=\left\{P_{v}\mid v\in\tilde{V}\right\}
, where
P_{v}
is the support path returned by the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
. Also recall that
|V(Z)|=|V|-|V_{\textsf{isolated}}|-|V_{\textsf{chunk}}|\geq(1-20\hat{\varepsilon})n
.
We say that a path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
is
bad
iff the number of vertices
u
in
P
with
\deg_{R}(u)\neq 2
is more than
20\log n/\hat{\varepsilon}
; otherwise we say that
P
is
good
.
Clearly, set
{\mathcal{P}}
contains at most
\hat{\varepsilon}/(20\log n)\cdot|{\mathcal{P}}|
bad paths.
Therefore, the total length of bad paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
is at most
\hat{\varepsilon}/(20\log n)\cdot|{\mathcal{P}}|\cdot 2h\leq\hat{\varepsilon}/(20\log n)\cdot(100n\log n/h)\cdot 2h\leq 10\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
Consider now a good path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, and let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{t}
be the subpaths of
P
obtained by deleting all vertices
u
of
P
with
\deg_{R}(u)\neq 2
.
We denote
{\mathcal{S}}(P)=\left\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{t}\right\}
, and say that a path in
{\mathcal{S}}(P)
is
short
iff its length is at most
\hat{\varepsilon}^{2}h/(20\log n)^{2}
; otherwise we say it is
long
.
Clearly, paths
P_{1},\ldots,P_{t}
are induced subpaths of
Z
. And it is easy to show that the total length of all long paths in
{\mathcal{S}}(P)
is at least
(1-\hat{\varepsilon}/(20\log n))|E(P)|
, namely

\sum_{1\leq i\leq t}\mathbf{1}[|E(P_{i})|\geq\hat{\varepsilon}^{2}h/(20\log n)^{2}]\cdot|E(P_{i})|\geq(1-\hat{\varepsilon}/(20\log n))\cdot|E(P)|.

Therefore, the total length of short subpaths in set
\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}:P\text{ is good}}{\mathcal{S}}(P)
is at most
10\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
Let
{\mathcal{Q}}
be the set that contains, for each good path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, all long subpaths in
{\mathcal{S}}(P)
. Then clearly the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
are vertex-disjoint, and each path of
{\mathcal{Q}}
has length at least
\hat{\varepsilon}^{2}h/(20\log n)^{2}
. Moreover, the total length of all paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
is at least
|V(Z)|-10\hat{\varepsilon}n-10\hat{\varepsilon}n\geq(1-40\hat{\varepsilon})n
.
∎

We use the algorithm from
3.1
to find a set
{\mathcal{Q}}
of vertex-disjoint induced subpaths in
Z
, each of length at least
\Omega(\hat{\epsilon}^{2}h/\log^{2}n)
(so
|{\mathcal{Q}}|\leq O(n\log^{2}n/\ell\hat{\varepsilon}^{2})
).
We denote
V^{\prime}=\bigcup_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}}V(Q)
, so
|V^{\prime}|\geq(1-40\hat{\epsilon})n
.
We then use the algorithm from
2.1
to compute the minimum cost of a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour. If the cost is at most
(2-100\hat{\epsilon})n
, then we return
(2-\hat{\epsilon})n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
; otherwise we return
2n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.

Proof of Correctness.

We first count the total number of queries performed by the algorithm.
The algorithm in
Lemma

1.3
performs
O(n/\epsilon)=O(n)
queries.
From
2.1
, the
1/\epsilon^{2}
executions of the subroutine
\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\ell)
perform in total
O(n\ell/\epsilon^{2})=O(n^{1.5})
queries.
From
2.3
, the
(100\cdot n\log n)/h
executions of the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,h,q,\alpha)
perform in total
O((q+\alpha^{2}h^{2})\cdot\sqrt{n}\log n)=\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries.
The algorithm from
Lemma

1.1
performs at most
O(n^{1.5}/\hat{\varepsilon}^{2})=O(n^{1.5})
queries.
The algorithm in
2.1
performs
\tilde{O}(n)
queries.
Therefore, the whole algorithm performs
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries in total.

We now show that the output of the algorithm is an
(2-\Omega({\epsilon}))
-approximation of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
. We start by defining several bad events and analyzing their probabilities.

Bad Event
\xi_{1}
.

We say that the bad event
\xi_{1}
happens, iff

•

either the number of
\ell
-light vertices in
G_{1}
is at least
2\epsilon n
, namely
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}|V(S_{v})|\geq 2\epsilon n
, while less than
\epsilon
-fraction of executions of
\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\ell)
return
Success
;

•

or the number of
\ell
-light vertices in
G_{1}
is at most
\epsilon n/2
, namely
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}|V(S_{v})|\leq\epsilon n/2
, while at least
\epsilon
-fraction executions of
\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\ell)
return
Success
.

From Chernoff’s bound,

\Pr[\xi_{1}]\leq e^{-\Omega(n)}
.

Bad Event
\xi_{2}
.

We say that the bad event
\xi_{2}
happens iff either the algorithm in
Lemma

1.3
reports incorrectly, or the algorithm from
Lemma

1.1
reports incorrectly.
From
Lemma

1.3
and
Lemma

1.1
,
\Pr[\xi_{2}]\leq O(n^{-10})
.

Bad Event
\xi_{3}
.

We say that the bad event
\xi_{3}
happens, iff

•

either
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})\geq{\epsilon}n/40
, while
\sum_{1\leq i\leq 1/{\epsilon}^{2}}(\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{i})/|V(S_{i})|)\leq 1/80\epsilon
;

•

or
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})\leq{\epsilon}n/160
, while
\sum_{1\leq i\leq 1/{\epsilon}^{2}}(\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{i})/|V(S_{i})|)\geq 1/80\epsilon
.

We define the function
\gamma:V\to\mathbb{R}
as follows. If
v
is
\ell
-light, then
\gamma(v)=\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})/|V(S_{v})|
, otherwise
\gamma(v)=0
. It is clear that
0\leq\gamma(v)\leq 1
for all vertices
v\in V
. Therefore, if vertex
v
is chosen uniformly at random from
V
,
\gamma(v)
is a random variable in
[0,1]
, and moreover,

\mathbb{E}_{v\sim V}[\gamma(v)]=\sum_{v^{\prime}\in L_{\ell}}\frac{|S_{v^{\prime}}|}{n}\cdot\frac{\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v^{\prime}})}{|S_{v^{\prime}}|}=\frac{\sum_{v^{\prime}\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v^{\prime}})}{n}.

Note that the values
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{1/{\epsilon}^{2}}
that we obtained in the algorithm are
1/{\epsilon}^{2}
independent samples of the random variable
\gamma(v)
.
Therefore, from Chernoff’s bound,
\Pr[\xi_{3}]\leq e^{-\Omega(n)}
.

We denote by
X
the output of the algorithm.
We will show that, if none of the bad events
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}
happens, then
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq X\leq(2-\Omega({\epsilon}))\cdot\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.
Recall that, from
Proposition

1.1
,
n\leq\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq 2n-2
.

First of all, if the algorithm from
Lemma

1.3
reports that
G_{1}
contains at least
{\epsilon}n/80
degree-1 vertices.
Since event
\xi_{2}
does not happen, and from
Proposition

1.3
,
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\geq(1+{\epsilon}/160)n
. Therefore, our estimate
X=2n
in this case is a
(2-\Omega({\epsilon}))
-approximation of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
. Assume from now on that algorithm in
Lemma

1.3
reports that
G_{1}
contains at most
{\epsilon}n/20
degree-1 vertices.
We now distinguish between the following two cases, depending on the number of executions of the subroutine

\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\ell)
that return
Success
in Step
2
.

3.1
Case 1. At least
\varepsilon
-fraction of the executions of
\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\ell)
return
Success

Since event
\xi_{1}
does not happen,
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}|V(S_{v})|\geq{\epsilon}n/2
. We distinguish between the following subcases.

Case 1.1.
\sum_{1\leq i\leq 1/{\epsilon}^{2}}\gamma_{i}\leq 1/80{\epsilon}
.

Since event
\xi_{3}
does not happen,
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})\leq{\epsilon}n/40
. We show in the following claim that
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq(2-{\epsilon}/20)n
, so our estimate
X=(2-{\epsilon}/20)n
is a
(2-\Omega({\epsilon}))
-approximation of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.

Claim 3.2
.

If
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}|V(S_{v})|\geq{\epsilon}n/2
,
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})\leq{\epsilon}n/40
and
G_{1}
contains at most
\epsilon n/20
degree-1 vertices, then
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq(2-\epsilon/20)n
.

Proof.

For each vertex
v\in L_{\ell}
, let
R_{v}
be a reconfiguration of the subgraph
S_{v}
with minimum cost.
Recall that set
V_{1}(R_{v})
contains all vertices with odd degree in multi-graph
R_{v}
.
For each vertex
u\in V_{1}(R_{v})
, let
u^{*}
be a vertex of
V\setminus V(S_{v})
with minimum distance from
u
, namely
u^{*}=\arg\min_{u^{\prime}\notin V(S_{v})}\left\{w(u,u^{*})\right\}
and
w(u,u^{*})=\textsf{ord}(u)
.
Denote
\Gamma_{v}=\left\{(u,u^{*})\mid u\in V_{1}(R_{v})\right\}
. Denote
R=\bigcup_{v\in L_{\ell}}R_{v}
and
\Gamma=\bigcup_{v\in L_{\ell}}\Gamma_{v}
.
Denote
V^{\prime}=V\setminus(\bigcup_{v\in L_{\ell}}V(S_{v}))
.
We define a graph
Y
such that
V(Y)=V
and
E(Y)=E_{G_{1}}(V^{\prime})\cup R\cup\Gamma
, where
E_{G_{1}}(V^{\prime})
is the set of edges in
G_{1}
with both endpoints in
V^{\prime}
.
To show that
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
is bounded away from
2n
, we will first show that
\textsf{GTSP}(Y)
, the graphic-TSP cost of
Y
, is bounded away from
2n
, and then we show that the difference between
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
and
\textsf{GTSP}(Y)
is small.

We first show that
\textsf{GTSP}(Y)\leq(2-\epsilon/10)n
. Note that here we view all edges of
Y
as weight-
1
edges. We use the following observation.

Observation 3.1
.

For each vertex
v\in L_{\ell}
,
R_{v}
contains at most
\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})
edges that are bridges in
Y
.

Proof.

Let
H_{v}
be the graph obtained by contracting all vertices of
V\setminus V(S_{v})
into a single node
v^{\prime}
, so
E(H_{v})=R_{v}\cup\Gamma_{v}
. From the definition of a reconfiguration and set
\Gamma_{v}
, it is easy to see that graph
H_{v}
is Eulerian graph.
Therefore, the only edges of
R_{v}
that are possibly bridges in
Y
are the edges that have more than one copies contained in
R_{v}
. Note that, if we delete one copy for each of these edges from
R_{v}
, then the remaining edges in
R_{v}
still induce a connected graph on
V(S_{v})
. Therefore, the number of such edges is at most
|R_{v}|-(|V(S_{v})|-1)\leq\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})
.
∎

Now fix a vertex
v\in L_{\ell}
. Let
T_{v}
be a spanning tree of the graph
(V(S_{v}),R_{v})
. Note that such a spanning tree exists since
R_{v}
is connected.
Also note that, from the definition of sets
R_{v}
and
\Gamma_{v}
, each vertex in
S_{v}
is incident to at least two edges of
R_{v}\cup\Gamma_{v}
. Therefore, each leaf of
T_{v}
is incident to an edge of
(R_{v}\cup\Gamma_{v})\setminus E(T_{v})
. Let
\hat{L}_{v}
be the number of leaves in
T_{v}
, then
\hat{L}_{v}\leq 2(|R_{v}|+|\Gamma_{v}|-|E(T_{v})|)\leq 4\cdot\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})
.
From
Proposition

1.4
,
T_{v}
contains a matching of size at least
(|V(S_{v})|-\hat{L}_{v})/2
.
From
3.1
, we get that
R_{v}
contains a matching consisting of only non-bridge edges with size at least

\frac{|V(S_{v})|-\hat{L}_{v}}{2}-\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})\geq\frac{|V(S_{v})|-4\cdot\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})}{2}-\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})=\frac{|V(S_{v})|}{2}-3\cdot\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v}).

Since sets
\left\{R_{v}\right\}_{v\in L_{\ell}}
are mutually disjoint,
Y
contains a matching of size

\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}:|V(S_{v})|>1}\left(\frac{|V(S_{v})|}{2}-2\cdot\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}|V(S_{v})|-\frac{n}{2\ell}-3\cdot\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})\geq\left(\frac{{\epsilon}}{4}-\frac{3{\epsilon}}{40}-\frac{1}{2\ell}\right)n,

consisting of only non-bridge edges.
From
Lemma

1.2
,
\textsf{GTSP}(Y)\leq(2-\epsilon/10)n
.

We now show that
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)-\textsf{GTSP}(Y)\leq\epsilon n/20
. Note that, together with
\textsf{GTSP}(Y)\leq(2-\epsilon/10)n
, this implies that
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq(2-\epsilon/20)n
, thus completing the proof of
3.2
.
Let
\pi=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n^{\prime}},v_{1})
be the optimal graphic-TSP-tour in
Y
, where possibly
n^{\prime}>n
since for each
1\leq i\leq n^{\prime}
,
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
is required to be an edge of
Y
, so
\pi
may visit some vertices more than once.
Moreover,

\begin{split}\textnormal{{cost}}(\pi)-\textnormal{{cost}}_{g}(\pi)&=\sum_{1\leq i\leq n^{\prime}}(w(v_{i},v_{i+1})-1)\\
&=\sum_{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in E_{G_{1}}(V^{\prime})}(w(v_{i},v_{i+1})-1)+\sum_{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in R\cup\Gamma}(w(v_{i},v_{i+1})-1)\\
&=\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\sum_{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\in R_{v}\cup\Gamma_{v}}(w(v_{i},v_{i+1})-1)\\
&=2\cdot\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v})\leq\varepsilon n/20,\end{split}

where
\textnormal{{cost}}_{g}(\pi)
is the graphic-TSP-cost of the tour
\pi
, namely the cost of
\pi
if the weight of each edge is
1
. Clearly, this implies that
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)-\textsf{GTSP}(Y)\leq\epsilon n/20
.
∎

Case 1.2.
\sum_{1\leq i\leq 1/{\epsilon}^{2}}\gamma_{i}\geq 1/80{\epsilon}
.

Since event
\xi_{3}
does not happen,
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(T_{v})\geq{\epsilon}n/160
. We show in the next claim that
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\geq(1+{\epsilon}/1120)n
, so our estimate
X=2n
in this case is a
(2-\Omega({\epsilon}))
-approximation of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.

Claim 3.3
.

\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\geq n+(\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v}))/7
.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be the optimal TSP-tour that traverses all vertices of
V
, and we denote
\alpha=\textnormal{{cost}}(\pi)-n
.
We will construct, for each maximal
\ell
-light vertex
v\in L_{\ell}
, a reconfiguration
R_{v}
for its maximal
\ell
-light subgraph
S_{v}
, such that
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{cost}}(R_{v})\leq 7\alpha
. Note that this implies

\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\geq n+(\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{rc}}(S_{v}))/7
.

Let
E(\pi)
be the set that contains, for every pair of vertices that appear consecutively in the tour
\pi
, an edge connecting them. In other words, if
\pi=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n},v_{1})
, then
E(\pi)=\left\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\right\}
.
We use the following simple observation.

Observation 3.2
.

|L_{\ell}|\leq 2\alpha
.

Proof.

From the definition of maximal
\ell
-light subgraphs, it is easy to see that, for each
v\in L_{\ell}
, there is at least one edge in
E(\pi)
connecting a vertex of
S_{v}
to a vertex of
S_{v}
. Note that such an edge does not belong to
G_{1}
, so it has cost at least
2
. Since
G_{1}
contains
|L_{\ell}|
maximal
\ell
-light subgraphs,
E(\pi)
contains at least
|L_{\ell}|/2
such edges, so
n+\alpha=\textnormal{{cost}}(\pi)\geq n+|L_{\ell}|/2
. It follows that
|L_{\ell}|\leq 2\alpha
.
∎

Let
v\in L_{\ell}
be a maximal
\ell
-light vertex, and let
S_{v}
be its maximal
\ell
-light subgraph
S_{v}
.
We construct the reconfiguration
R_{v}
as follows.
Starting with
R_{v}=\emptyset
, we first add all edges of
E(\pi)
with both endpoints in
S_{v}
to
R_{v}
. Assume that the graph on
V(S_{v})
induced by these edges contain
k
connected components. We then select a set
E^{\prime}_{v}
of
(k-1)
edges in
E(S_{v})
, such that, together with edges of
E(\pi)
whose both endpoints belong to
V(S_{v})
, they induce a connected graph on
V(S_{v})
.
Since
S_{v}
is connected, such a set
E^{\prime}_{v}
clearly exists.
We then add two copies for each edge of
E^{\prime}_{v}
to
R_{v}
. This completes the construction of
R_{v}
.
Clearly,
R_{v}
is a valid reconfiguration.
Note that, for a vertex
u\in S_{v}
,
\deg_{R_{v}}(u)
is odd iff there is an edge
(u,u^{\prime})
in
E(\pi)
connecting
u
with some other vertex
u^{\prime}\notin S_{v}
.

We denote
V^{\prime}=V\setminus(\bigcup_{v\in L_{\ell}}V(S_{v}))
, and define

\textnormal{{cost}}_{\pi}(V^{\prime})=\sum_{(u,u^{\prime})\in E(\pi):\text{ }u,u^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}}w(u,u^{\prime})\text{ }+\sum_{(u,u^{\prime})\in E(\pi):\text{ }u\notin V^{\prime},u^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}}\frac{w(u,u^{\prime})}{2}.

Clearly,
\textnormal{{cost}}_{\pi}(V^{\prime})\geq|V^{\prime}|
.
We define
\textnormal{{cost}}_{\pi}(S_{v})
for all vertices
v\in L_{\ell}
similarly.
Denote
E^{\prime}=\bigcup_{v\in L_{\ell}}E^{\prime}_{v}
.
From similar arguments in the proof of
3.2
, we can show that
|E^{\prime}|\leq 2\alpha
.
Therefore,
\textnormal{{cost}}_{\pi}(V^{\prime})+\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{cost}}_{\pi}(S_{v})=\textnormal{{cost}}(\pi)+2\cdot\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}|E^{\prime}_{v}|=n+\alpha+2|E^{\prime}|\leq n+5\alpha
.
Altogether,
we have

\begin{split}\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{cost}}(R_{v})&=\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\left(\sum_{(u,u^{\prime})\in R_{v}}w(u,u^{\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{u\in V_{1}(R_{v})}\textsf{ord}(u)-(|V(S_{v})|-1)\right)\\
&\leq\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\left(\textnormal{{cost}}_{\pi}(S_{v})-(|V(S_{v})|-1)\right)\\
&\leq\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}\textnormal{{cost}}_{\pi}(S_{v})-\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}|V(S_{v})|+|L_{\ell}|\\
&\leq(n+5\alpha)-\textnormal{{cost}}_{\pi}(V^{\prime})-\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}|V(S_{v})|+2\alpha\\
&\leq(n+5\alpha)-n+2\alpha\leq 7\alpha.\end{split}

This completes the proof of
3.3
.
∎

3.2
Case 2. Less than
\varepsilon
-fraction of the executions of
\textsf{Local}(\cdot,\ell)
return
Success

Since event
\xi_{1}
does not happen,
\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}|V(S_{v})|\leq 2{\epsilon}n
.
In this case the algorithm continues to execute Step
3
.
Recall that in Step
3
we execute the subroutine
BFS
on
(100\cdot n\log n)/h
random vertices of
V
.
Recall that
Z=(V,\bigcup_{v\in\tilde{V}}E(P_{v}))
and
H=(V,\bigcup_{v\in\tilde{V}}E(H_{v}))
, where
\tilde{V}
is the set of sampled vertices that the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Success
upon.
Also recall that
V_{\textsf{chunk}}=\bigcup_{v\in\tilde{V}}V(H_{v}\setminus P_{v})
and
V_{\textsf{isolated}}=V\setminus(V_{\textsf{chunk}}\cup V(Z))
.
We further distinguish between the following sub-cases, depending on outcomes of subroutines
BFS
.

Case 2.1.
|V_{\textsf{chunk}}|\geq 10\hat{\epsilon}n
.

We first prove the following claim.

Claim 3.4
.

The number of vertices in
H
that do not belong to any e-block of
H
is at most
\hat{\varepsilon}n
.

Proof.

Let
H^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
H
by deleting from it all
\ell
-light vertices in
V
(recall that the number of
\ell
-light vertices of
V
is at most
2\varepsilon n
). Note that every degree-
1
vertex in
H
corresponds to an degree-
1
vertex of
G^{\prime}_{1}
. Since the number of degree-
1
vertices of
G^{\prime}_{1}
is at most
n/\ell
, and since the diameter of each graph in
\left\{H_{v}\right\}_{v\in\tilde{V}}
is at most
2h
, the number of vertices that do not belong to an e-block of
H^{\prime}
is at most
(n/\ell)\cdot(2h)=\hat{\varepsilon}n/100
. It follows that the number of vertices in
H
that do not belong to any e-block of
H
is at most
\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
∎

Assume first that the size of a maximum matching in
H
consisting of only edges in e-blocks of
H
is at least
2\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
Since
H
is a subgraph of
G_{1}
, the size of a maximum matching in
G_{1}
consisting of only edges in e-blocks of
G_{1}
is also at least
2\hat{\varepsilon}n
. From
Lemma

1.2
, we get that
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq(2-\hat{\varepsilon})n
, so our estimate
X=(2-\hat{\varepsilon})n
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\hat{\varepsilon}))
-approximation of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.

Assume now that the size of a maximum matching in
H
consisting of only edges in e-blocks of
H
is at most
2\hat{\varepsilon}n
. We will show that the size of a maximum matching in
G_{1}
is at most
(1/2-\hat{\varepsilon})n
. Then from
Proposition

1.2
,
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\geq(1+2\hat{\varepsilon})n
, so our estimate
X=2n
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\hat{\varepsilon}))
-approximation of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.
Let
M
be a maximum matching in
H
consisting of only edges in e-blocks of
H
. The edges of
M
can be partitioned into three subsets: set
M_{1}
contains edges with both endpoints lying in
V(H)
; set
M_{2}
contains edges with both endpoints lying in
V\setminus V(H)
; and set
M_{3}
contains all other edges.
Clearly,
|M_{2}|\leq(n-|V(H)|)/2
. From
3.4
,
|M_{1}|\leq\hat{\varepsilon}n+2\hat{\varepsilon}n=3\hat{\varepsilon n}
, since every edge in
M_{1}
either contains a vertex that does not belong to any e-block of
H
, or is entirely contained in some e-block of
H
.
Moreover, since the only vertices of
V(H)
that are connected by edges to vertices of
V\setminus V(H)
are interface vertices of graphs in
\left\{H_{v}\right\}_{v\in\tilde{V}}
, so
|M_{3}|\leq 2|\tilde{V}|\leq O(\sqrt{n}\log n)
.
Altogether,

|M|=|M_{1}|+|M_{2}|+|M_{3}|\leq(n-|V(H)|)/2+3\hat{\varepsilon n}+O(\sqrt{n}\log n)\leq(1/2-\hat{\varepsilon})n.

Case 2.2.
|V_{\textsf{isolated}}|,|V_{\textsf{chunk}}|<10\hat{\epsilon}n
.

Recall
V(Z)=V\setminus(V_{\textsf{isolated}}\cup V_{\textsf{chunk}})
, so
|V(Z)|\geq(1-20\hat{\varepsilon})n
.
From algorithm in
3.1
, we obtain a set
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
\tilde{O}(n/h)
paths with
|E({\mathcal{Q}})|\geq(1-40\hat{\varepsilon})n
. It is easy to see, from the definition of the subroutine
BFS
, that the condition of
2.2
is satisfied by the set
{\mathcal{Q}}
of paths.
We then use the algorithm from
2.1
to compute the minimum cost of a
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour.
Assume first that the cost is at least
(2-100\hat{\epsilon})n
. Then from
2.2
,
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)>(1+\hat{\epsilon})n
, and our estimate
X=2n
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\hat{\varepsilon}))
-approximate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.
Assume now that the cost is less than
(2-100\hat{\epsilon})n
, then since
|E({\mathcal{Q}})|\geq(1-40\hat{\epsilon})n
,
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq(2-100\hat{\epsilon})n+2\cdot 40\hat{\epsilon}n=(2-20\hat{\epsilon})n
(a tour with such cost can be easily constructed from the
{\mathcal{Q}}
-proper tour and Euler-tour of any spanning tree of
G_{1}
), and so our estimate
X=(2-\hat{\epsilon})n
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\hat{\varepsilon}))
-approximate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.

Case 2.3.
|V_{\textsf{isolated}}|\geq 10\hat{\epsilon}n
.

From Step
4
, we then use the algorithm from
Lemma

1.1
with parameter
\hat{\epsilon}/100
to obtain an estimate of the size of some maximal matching of
G_{1}[V_{\textsf{isolated}}]
.
If the estimate is at most
\hat{\epsilon}n
, then we return
2n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.
Otherwise, we return
(2-\hat{\epsilon}/200)n
as the estimate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.

We first consider the case where the estimate output by the algorithm from
Lemma

1.1
is at most
\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
Since event
\xi_{2}
does not happen, the size of some maximal matching in graph
G_{1}[V_{\textsf{isolated}}]
is at most
(1+1/100)\hat{\varepsilon}n
, and therefore the size of a maximum matching in
G_{1}[V_{\textsf{isolated}}]
is at most
(2+2/100)\hat{\varepsilon}n
. We use the following claim to show that our estimate
X=2n
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\hat{\varepsilon}))
-approximate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
.

Claim 3.5
.

If
|V_{\textnormal{{isolated}}}|\geq 10\hat{\epsilon}n
, and the size of a maximum matching in graph
G_{1}[V_{\textsf{isolated}}]
is at most
(2+2/100)\hat{\varepsilon}n
, then
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\geq(1+\hat{\epsilon}/10)n
.

Proof.

From
Proposition

1.2
, it is sufficient to show that the size of a maximum matching in graph
G_{1}
is at most
(1/2-\hat{\varepsilon}/20)n
. Assume for contradiction that this is not true.
We will show that the size of a maximum matching in
G_{1}[V_{\textsf{isolated}}]
is at least
(2+2/10)\hat{\varepsilon}n
, leading to a contradiction.

Let
M
be a maximum matching in
G_{1}
, so
|M|\geq(1/2-\hat{\epsilon}/20)n
. The edges of
M
can be partitioned into three subsets: set
M_{1}
contains edges with both endpoints lying in
V_{\textsf{isolated}}
; set
M_{2}
contains edges with both endpoints lying in
V\setminus V_{\textsf{isolated}}
; and set
M_{3}
contains all other edges, so
|M|=|M_{1}|+|M_{2}|+|M_{3}|
.
Since
M_{1}
is a matching in
G_{1}[V_{\textsf{isolated}}]
, it suffices to show that
|M_{1}|\geq(2+2/10)\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
Clearly,
|M_{2}|\leq(n-|V_{\textsf{isolated}}|)/2
. Since
|V_{\textsf{isolated}}|\geq 10\hat{\varepsilon}n
, it suffices to show that
|M_{3}|\leq 2\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
From the definition of
M_{3}
, each edge of
M_{3}
has one endpoint in
V_{\textsf{isolated}}
.
It is easy to verify that, for each vertex
\hat{v}\in V_{\textsf{isolated}}
, the vertices in
H
that
\hat{v}
may possibly be adjacent to are interface vertices of graphs of
\left\{H_{v}\mid v\in\tilde{V}\right\}
, and there are at most
|\tilde{V}|=\tilde{O}(n^{0.5})
of them.
Therefore,

|M_{3}|\leq\tilde{O}(n^{0.5})<2\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
∎

We now consider the case where the estimate output by the algorithm from
Lemma

1.1
is greater than
\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
Since event
\xi_{2}
does not happen, the size of some maximum matching in graph
G_{1}[V_{\textsf{isolated}}]
is at most
0.99\hat{\varepsilon}n
. The following crucial claim immediate implies that our estimate
X=(2-\hat{\varepsilon}/200)n
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\hat{\varepsilon}))
-approximate of
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)
with high probability, and thus completes the proof of
Theorem

7
.
The proof of
3.6
is long and technical, and is therefore deferred to
Section

3.3
.

Claim 3.6
.

If
|V_{\textnormal{{isolated}}}|\geq 10\hat{\epsilon}n
, and the size of a maximum matching in
G_{1}[V_{\textnormal{{isolated}}}]
is at least
0.99\hat{\epsilon}n
, then with probability
1-O(n^{-99})
,
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq(2-\hat{\epsilon}/125)n
.

3.3
Proof of
3.6

In this section we provide the proof of
3.6
.
We start with the some new definitions in subsections
3.3.1
,
3.3.2
and then complete the proof in
Section

3.3.3
.

3.3.1
Pruned Subgraph and its Augmented E-Block Tree

Recall that
L_{\ell}
is the set of maximal
\ell
-light vertices in
V
.
We construct another vertex-weighted graph
G_{1}^{\prime}
, that we call the
pruned subgraph
of
G_{1}
, by starting from
G_{1}
and processing each vertex of
L_{\ell}
as follows. For each maximal
\ell
-light vertex
v\in L_{\ell}
, recall that
e_{v}
is the witness edge of
v
, and we denote by
\hat{v}
the other endpoint of
e_{v}
, which we call the
witness vertex
for
v
. By definition,
\hat{v}
is not
\ell
-light.
Let
G_{1}^{\prime}
be the graph obtained from
G_{1}
by deleting, for each vertex
v\in L_{\ell}
, all vertices in the subgraph
S_{v}
and their incident edges.
It is easy to see that
G_{1}^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
G_{1}
. We use the following simple observation.

Observation 3.3
.

Graph
G_{1}^{\prime}
contains at most
n/\ell
degree-1 vertices.

Proof.

For each vertex that is not the witness vertex for any vertex of
L_{\ell}
, we define its weight to be
1
.
For each vertex
u
such that
u
is the witness vertex for maximal
\ell
-light vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\in L_{\ell}
(namely
\hat{v}_{1}=\cdots=\hat{v}_{r}=u
), we define its weight to be
1+\sum_{1\leq i\leq r}|V(S_{v_{i}})|
.
Therefore, the total weight of all vertices in
G^{\prime}_{1}
is
n
.
From the construction of
G_{1}^{\prime}
, each degree-1 vertex
u
of
G_{1}^{\prime}
must be the witness vertex for some vertex of
L_{\ell}
, and its weight is at least
\ell
.

3.3
then follows.
∎

We then construct a tree
T
as follows. We start from the e-block tree
{\mathcal{T}}_{G^{\prime}_{1}}
of the pruned subgraph
G^{\prime}_{1}
, and process all e-blocks of
G^{\prime}_{1}
whose corresponding e-block vertices in
{\mathcal{T}}_{G^{\prime}_{1}}
have degree
2
one-by-one as follows.
Consider such an e-block
B
of
G^{\prime}_{1}
.
Note that there are at most two vertices of
B
that are incident to an edge of
\delta_{G_{1}}(B)
. We denote them by
s,t
(where possibly
s=t
). We replace the e-block vertex
v_{B}
in
{\mathcal{T}}_{G^{\prime}_{1}}
with an arbitrary shortest path in
B
connecting
s
to
t
.
This completes the construction of
T
. We say that
T
is the
augmented e-block tree
for graph
G_{1}^{\prime}
.
We denote by
L
the total length of all shortest paths that are added to e-block tree
{\mathcal{T}}_{G^{\prime}_{1}}
in the construction of tree
T
.

We use the following simple observations.

Observation 3.4
.

The following statements are true for
T
:

•

T
contains at most
n/\ell
leaves;

•

all bridges of graph
G^{\prime}_{1}
are contained in
T
;

•

every degree-
2
vertex of
T
is also a vertex of the original graph
G_{1}
; and

•

for every pair
v,v^{\prime}
of degree-
2
vertices in
T
that belong to the same maximal induced subpath of
T
,
\textnormal{{dist}}_{G_{1}}(v,v^{\prime})=\textnormal{{dist}}_{T}(v,v^{\prime})
.

Observation 3.5
.

G_{1}
contains a matching of size at least
L/2
consisting of only non-bridge edges.

3.3.2
Representatives and Smooth Vertices

Recall that every vertex of
V
is either an
\ell
-light vertex or a vertex of
V(G^{\prime}_{1})
.
We first define, for each vertex
v\in V
, a set
R(v)
of vertices of
T
, that we call the set of
representatives
of
v
in
T
, as follows:

•

if a vertex
v
belongs to
G_{1}^{\prime}
but does not belong to any e-block of
G_{1}^{\prime}
, then we define
R(v)=\left\{v\right\}
;

•

if a vertex
v
belongs to some e-block
B
of
G_{1}^{\prime}
, whose corresponding e-block vertex
v_{B}
is a special vertex in the e-block tree
{\mathcal{T}}_{G^{\prime}_{1}}
of
G_{1}
, then we let
R(v)
contain only the vertex of
T
that represents the e-block
B
of
G_{1}^{\prime}
;

•

if a vertex
v
belongs to some e-block
B
of
G_{1}^{\prime}
, whose corresponding e-block vertex
v_{B}
is a degree-
2
vertex in
{\mathcal{T}}_{G^{\prime}_{1}}
, then we let
R(v)
contain all vertices on the shortest path that are added to
{\mathcal{T}}_{G^{\prime}_{1}}
when processing the e-block vertex
v_{B}
.

•

if
v
is an
\ell
-light vertex, then we let
v^{\prime}
be the unique witness vertex in
G_{1}
of the maximal
\ell
-light subgraph that contains
v
, and we define
R(v)
depending on whether or not
v^{\prime}
belongs to some e-block of
G^{\prime}_{1}
similarly.

We say that a vertex
v
in
T
is
smooth
, iff (i) it is at tree-distance at least
2h
from all special vertices of
T
; and
(ii) the number of vertices
\hat{v}
in
V
such that
\textnormal{{dist}}_{T}(R(\hat{v}),v)\leq 2h
is at most
10h/\hat{\varepsilon}
, namely

|\left\{\hat{v}\mid\textnormal{{dist}}_{T}(R(\hat{v}),v)\leq 2h\right\}|\leq 10h/\hat{\varepsilon}
,
where
\textnormal{{dist}}_{T}(R(\hat{v}),v)=\min_{v^{\prime}\in R(\hat{v})}\left\{\textnormal{{dist}}_{T}(v^{\prime},v)\right\}
.

We use the following claim.

Claim 3.7
.

The number of non-smooth vertices is at most
0.45\hat{\varepsilon}n+L
.

Proof.

We say that a vertex
v
in
T
is a
type-
1
(
type-
2
, resp.) non-smooth vertex, iff vertex
v
does not satisfy property (i) (property (ii), resp.).
From
3.4
,
T
contains at most
n/\ell
leaves, and therefore
T
contains at most
2n/\ell
maximal induced subpaths (from
1.1
). It is easy to see that each maximal induced subpath of
T
contains at most
4h
type-1 non-smooth vertices.
Therefore, the number of type-1 non-smooth vertices is at most
4h\cdot(2n/\ell)=\hat{\varepsilon}n/25
.

Let
P
be a maximal induced subpath in
T
.
Let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{P}
be the set of shortest paths that are added to
{\mathcal{T}}_{G^{\prime}_{1}}
in the construction of path
P
of
T
.
Let
P^{\prime}
be the path obtained from
P
by contracting all paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{P}
, so
|V(P)|=|V(P^{\prime})|+\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{P}}|E(Q)|
.
Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}
be all type-2 non-smooth vertices in
P^{\prime}
, where the vertices appear on
P^{\prime}
in this order.
Define
S_{P}=\left\{v_{1},v_{4h+3},v_{8h+5},\ldots,v_{(4h+2)\cdot\left\lfloor k/(4h+2)\right\rfloor+1}\right\}
.
It is clear that, if we denote
B(v,2h)=\left\{\hat{v}\mid\textnormal{{dist}}_{T}(R(\hat{v}),v)\leq 2h\right\}
, then for every pair
v_{i},v_{j}
of vertices in
S_{P}
,
B(v_{i},2h)\cap B(v_{j},2h)=\emptyset
. Moreover, if we denote by
{\mathcal{P}}
the set of all maximal induced subpaths in
T
, then the sets
\left\{B(v,2h)\right\}_{v\in S(P),P\in{\mathcal{P}}}
are mutually disjoint. Therefore,
|\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}S_{P}|\leq n/(10h/\hat{\varepsilon})
.
On the other hand, the number of type-2 non-smooth vertices in
T
is at most
(4h+2)\cdot|\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}S_{P}|+\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}|E({\mathcal{Q}}_{P})|
. Therefore, the number of type-2 non-smooth vertices is at most
(4h+2)\cdot n/(10h/\hat{\varepsilon})+L\leq 0.41\hat{\varepsilon}n+L
.
Altogether, the number of non-smooth vertices is at most
0.45\hat{\varepsilon}n+L
.
∎

3.3.3
Completing the Proof of
3.6

Recall that we have denoted by
L
the total length of all shortest paths that are added to e-block tree
{\mathcal{T}}_{G^{\prime}_{1}}
in the construction of tree
T
.
From
3.5
, if
L\geq\hat{\varepsilon}n/80
, then
G_{1}
contains a matching of size
\hat{\varepsilon}n/80
with no bridge edges. From
Lemma

1.2
, this implies that
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq(2-\hat{\epsilon}/120)n
, and
3.6
then follows.
Therefore, we assume from now on that
L<\hat{\varepsilon}n/80
.
And then from
3.7
, the number of non-smooth vertices is at most
\hat{\varepsilon}n/2
.

Let
M
be a maximum matching in graph
G_{1}[{V_{\textsf{isolated}}}]
, so
|M|\geq 0.99\hat{\epsilon}n
. If at least
\hat{\varepsilon}n/40
edges of
M
belong to e-blocks of
G_{1}[{V_{\textsf{isolated}}}]
, then the claim is true since all such edges also belong to e-blocks of
G_{1}
.
Therefore, we only need to consider the case where at least
0.99\hat{\epsilon}n-\hat{\varepsilon}n/40\geq 0.96\hat{\varepsilon}n
edges of
M
are bridges in
G_{1}
.
Since the number of
\ell
-light vertices in
V
is at most
2\varepsilon n
, at least
(0.96\hat{\varepsilon}-2\varepsilon)n\geq 0.95\hat{\varepsilon}n
edges of
M
also belong to the pruned subgraph
G^{\prime}_{1}
as bridges.
Recall that all bridges of
G^{\prime}_{1}
are contained in
T
, so at least
0.95\hat{\varepsilon}n
edges of
M
belong to
T
.
We now delete from these edges, all edges with at least one endpoint that is at tree-distance at most
2h
from some special vertex of
T
.
Clearly, we have deleted at most
2h\cdot(2n/\ell)\leq 0.02\hat{\varepsilon}n
vertices.
We denote by
M^{\prime}
the set of remaining edges of
M
in
T
, so
|M^{\prime}|\geq 0.93\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
We denote by
V^{\prime}
the set of endpoints of edges in
M^{\prime}
, so
|V^{\prime}|\geq 1.86\hat{\varepsilon}n
.

Claim 3.8
.

If
|V_{\textnormal{{isolated}}}|\geq 10\hat{\epsilon}n
, and the size of a maximum matching in
G_{1}[V_{\textnormal{{isolated}}}]
is at least
0.99\hat{\epsilon}n
, then with probability
1-O(n^{-99})
,
T
contains at least
0.93\hat{\varepsilon}n
vertices that subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Fail
on.

Proof.

Recall that in Step
3
, we have sampled a set of
(100\cdot n\log n)/h
random vertices in
V
to execute the subroutine
BFS
on, and a vertex of
V
is eventually added to
V_{\textsf{isolated}}
if it does not belong to any graph of
\left\{H_{v}\right\}_{v\in\tilde{V}}
, where
\tilde{V}
is the subset of sampled vertices that the subroutine
BFS
returns
Success
on.
For any vertex
v\in V
, if the set
B(v,h)=\left\{v^{\prime}\mid w(v,v^{\prime})\leq h\right\}
contains at least
h
vertices that the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Success
upon, then with probability
1-(1-h/n)^{100n\log n/h}=1-O(n^{-100})
, at least one of these vertices is sampled in Step
3
and the corresponding graph
H_{v^{\prime}}
returned by the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v^{\prime},h,q,\alpha)
contains
v
, and therefore
v
will not be added to
V_{\textsf{isolated}}
.
From the union bound over all vertices of
V
, with probability at least
1-O(n^{-99})
, for each vertex
v
in
V_{\textsf{isolated}}
, set
B(v,h)
contains at most
h
vertices that the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Success
upon.
We assume from now on that this is the case.

For each vertex
v
in
V^{\prime}
, let
N_{v}
be the set of vertices that are at tree-distance at most
h
from
v
in
T
, and let
\hat{N}_{v}\subseteq N_{v}
be the subset that contains all vertices that the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Fail
on.
For each vertex
v\in V^{\prime}
, it is immediate that
N_{v}\subseteq B(v,h)
, so
|N_{v}\setminus\hat{N}_{v}|\leq h
, and since
|N_{v}|=2h+1
, we have
|\hat{N}_{v}|\geq h+1
.
On the other hand, from the definition of
V^{\prime}
, every vertex in
N_{v}
is at tree-distance at least
h
from all special vertices of
T
. Therefore, each vertex of
T
appears in at most
(2h+1)
sets of
\left\{N_{v}\right\}_{v\in V^{\prime}}
.
Altogether, if we let
V_{f}
be the set of vertices in
T
that the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Fail
upon, then

|V_{f}|\cdot(2h+1)\geq\sum_{v\in V^{\prime}}|N_{v}|\geq\sum_{v\in V^{\prime}}|\hat{N}_{v}|\geq|V^{\prime}|\cdot(h+1)
,
and it follows that
|V_{f}|\geq|V^{\prime}|(h+1)/(2h+1)>0.93\hat{\varepsilon}n
.
∎

Let
\hat{V}
be the set of all smooth vertices in
T
that the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(\cdot,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Fail
on. From
3.7
and
3.8
, so
|\hat{V}|\geq 0.93\hat{\varepsilon}n-\hat{\varepsilon}n/2\geq 0.43\hat{\varepsilon}n
.

We delete from
\hat{V}
all vertices such that
|V(T^{h}_{v})|\geq 10h/\hat{\varepsilon}
. Since the number of
\ell
-light vertices is at most
2\varepsilon n
, at most
(2\varepsilon n)\cdot(2h+1)/(10h/\hat{\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon n
vertices are deleted. Therefore, at least
0.4\hat{\varepsilon}n
vertices remain in
\hat{V}
.
It is easy to see that, if the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Fail
for some remaining vertex
v
in
\hat{V}
, then either tree
T^{h}_{v}
contains at least
3
level-
h
vertices, or the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
has performed
q
queries before all its
h
stages are finished.
We partition the remaining set
\hat{V}
into two subsets accordingly: set
\hat{V}_{1}
contains vertices
v
such that
T^{h}_{v}
contains at least
3
level-
h
vertices; and set
\hat{V}_{2}
contains vertices
v
such that
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
performs
q
queries before all its
h
stages are finished. We distinguish between the following cases.

Case 2.3.1.
|\hat{V}_{1}|\geq 0.2\hat{\varepsilon}n
.

Consider now a vertex
v\in\hat{V}_{1}
. Since
v
is a smooth vertex,
T
contains at most
2
vertices that are at tree-distance
h
from
v
.
Since tree
T^{h}_{v}
contains at least
3
level-
h
vertices, there exists a vertex
u\notin V(T)
such that
w(u,v)=h
.
We call such a vertex a
failure-certificate
for
v
, and designate any such vertex as
u(v)
.
Clearly, either
u(v)
is an
\ell
-light vertex, or
u(v)
belongs to some e-block of
G^{\prime}_{1}
.
It is not hard to see that each
\ell
-light vertex may be a failure-certificate for at most
2
smooth vertices.
Since there are at most
2\varepsilon n

\ell
-light vertices, set
\hat{V}_{1}
contains at most
4\varepsilon n
smooth vertices that have an
\ell
-light failure-certificate.
Therefore,
\hat{V}_{1}
contains at least
0.19\hat{\varepsilon}n
vertices
v
