such that the failure-certificate
u(v)
belongs to some e-block of
G^{\prime}_{1}
. We then show using in the following observation that the size of a maximum matching consisting of only edges in e-blocks of
G_{1}^{\prime}
is at least
0.19\hat{\varepsilon}n/6\geq\hat{\varepsilon}n/40
.
And then together with
Lemma

1.2
, this implies that
\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq(2-\hat{\varepsilon}/60)n
, thus completing the proof of
3.6
in this case.

Observation 3.6
.

Every e-block
B
of
G^{\prime}_{1}
contains a matching of size at least
\frac{1}{6}\bigg|\left\{v\in\hat{V}_{1}\text{ }\big|\text{ }u(v)\in V(B)\right\}\bigg|
.

Proof.

Since vertices of
\hat{V}_{1}
are smooth, they are at tree-distance at least
2h
from all special vertices of
T
. Therefore, if
B
contains at least one failure-certificate
u(v)
for some vertex
v\in\hat{V}_{1}
, then
B
must contain exactly two vertices that are incident to edges of
\delta_{G_{1}}(B)
, which we denote by
s_{B},t_{B}
, and
T
contains a shortest path in
B
connecting
s_{B}
to
t_{B}
, which we denote by
Q_{B}
.

Denote
S=\left\{v\in\hat{V}_{1}\text{ }\big|\text{ }u(v)\in V(B)\right\}
and
x=|S|
.
If
|E(Q_{B})|\geq x/3
, then
Q_{B}
contains a matching of size at least
x/6
.
Assume now that
|E(Q_{B})|<x/3
.
Since
\hat{V}_{1}\subseteq V(T)
, at least
2x/3
vertices of
S
do not belong to
B
.
Therefore, at least one of the two subtrees obtained by deleting all edges of
Q_{B}
from
T
contains at least
x/3
vertices of
R
.
Assume without loss of generality that the subtree containing vertex
s_{B}
, that we denote by
T_{B}
, contains at least
x/3
vertices of
S
.
From the definition of
T
, it is easy to see that the distances in
\left\{w(v,s_{B})\mid v\in V(T_{B})\cap\hat{V}_{1}\right\}
are mutually distinct, which implies that the distances in
\left\{w(u(v),s_{B})\mid v\in V(T_{B})\cap\hat{V}_{1}\right\}
are also mutually distinct. Therefore, the diameter of the e-block
B
is at least
x/3
, and it follows that
B
contains a matching of size at least
x/6
, completing the proof of
3.6
.
∎

Case 2.3.2.
|\hat{V}_{2}|\geq 0.2\hat{\varepsilon}n
.

For each vertex
v\in\hat{V}_{2}
, we designate an arbitrary vertex in the set
R(v)
as the
representative
of
v
, denoted by
r(v)
.
We use the following claim.

Claim 3.9
.

For each vertex
v\in\hat{V}_{2}
, there exists a pair
(v^{\textnormal{{in}}},v^{\textnormal{{out}}})
of vertices in
V
, such that: (i) the tree-distance between vertices
r(v^{\textnormal{{in}}})
and
v
in
T
is at most
h
; (ii) the tree-distance between vertices
r(v^{\textnormal{{in}}})
and
r(v^{\textnormal{{out}}})
in
T
is at least
h
; and (iii)
w(v^{\textnormal{{in}}},v^{\textnormal{{out}}})\leq h/500
.

Proof.

For each
u\notin T^{h}_{v}
, we denote by
d_{u}
the minimum distance between
u
and a vertex in
T^{h}_{v}
, namely
d_{u}=\min_{u^{\prime}\in V(T^{h}_{v})}\left\{w(u,u^{\prime})\right\}
.
Recall that set
{\mathcal{Q}}(u)
contains all vertices
v^{\prime}
, where the distance between
v^{\prime}
and
u
has been queried in the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(u,h,q,\alpha)
.
As a corollary of
2.4
, every pair of vertices in
{\mathcal{Q}}(u)
are at tree-distance at least
(d_{u}-1)
in
T
. Therefore,
|{\mathcal{Q}}(u)|\leq 2|V(T^{h}_{v})|/(d_{u}-1)
, since otherwise there exists a pair of vertices in
{\mathcal{Q}}(u)
that are at distance at most
(d_{u}-2)
in the Euler tour of
T^{h}_{v}
, and so they are at distance at most
(d_{u}-2)
, a contradiction.

Denote
N_{v}=V(T^{h}_{v})
. From the definition of the subroutine
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
, if the distance between a pair
v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{2}
of vertices are ever queried, then at least one of
v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{2}
belongs to set
N_{v}
.
Let
N^{\prime}_{v}
be the set of vertices
u
such that the tree-distance between vertices
r(u)
and
v
in
T
is at most
2h
. Since
v
is a smooth vertex,
|N^{\prime}_{v}|\leq 10h/\hat{\varepsilon}
. Therefore, the number of performed queries on the distances between a pair of vertices in
N^{\prime}_{v}
is at most
(10h/\hat{\varepsilon})^{2}\leq 100n
. Since
\textnormal{{BFS}}(v,h,q,\alpha)
returns
Fail
, there exists a vertex
x\notin N^{\prime}_{v}
with
|{\mathcal{Q}}(x)|\geq(q-100n)/n\geq 2^{20}/\hat{\varepsilon}
. From
2.4
,
d_{w}\leq\frac{2\cdot(10h/\hat{\varepsilon})}{2^{20}/\hat{\varepsilon}}+1\leq h/500
. We designate
x
as
v^{\textnormal{{out}}}
, and define
v^{\textnormal{{in}}}=\arg_{v^{\prime}\in N_{v}}\left\{w(v^{\prime},v^{\textnormal{{out}}})\right\}
.
It remain to verify the properties (i) and (ii).
On one hand, since
v^{\textnormal{{in}}}\in N_{v}
and
v
is a smooth vertex,
r(v^{\textnormal{{in}}})
and
v
are at tree-distance at most
h
in
T
. On the other hand, since
r(v^{\textnormal{{out}}})
and
v
are at tree-distance at least
2h
in
T
, from the triangle inequality,
r(v^{\textnormal{{out}}})
and
r(v^{\textnormal{{in}}})
are at tree-distance at least
h
in
T
.
∎

Consider now a vertex
v\in\hat{V}_{2}
, let
X^{\prime}_{v}
be the tree-path in
T
connecting
v^{\textnormal{{in}}}
to
v^{\textnormal{{out}}}
.
Since
v
is smooth, vertices
v^{\textnormal{{in}}}
and
v
belong to the same maximal induced subpath of
T
. We denote by
X_{v}
the intersection between this maximal induced subpath and
X^{\prime}_{v}
. Clearly,
X_{v}
is a path of length at least
h
.
We use the following claim.

Claim 3.10
.

Let
P
be a maximal induced subpath of
T
and define
\hat{V}(P)=\hat{V}_{2}\cap V(P)
.
Then there exists a subset
\bar{V}(P)\subseteq\hat{V}(P)
, such that: (i) for any pair
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices in
\bar{V}(P)
, the paths
X_{v}
and
X_{v^{\prime}}
are disjoint; and (ii)
\sum_{v\in\bar{V}(P)}|V(X_{v})|\geq|\hat{V}(P)|/5
.

Proof.

We iteratively construct the set
\bar{V}(P)
as follows.
Throughout, we maintain a set
Z
of vertices of
\hat{V}(P)
, initialized
\emptyset
.
The algorithm proceeds in iterations, and will continue to be executed as long as there is a vertex
v\in\hat{V}(P)
, such that the path
X_{v}
is disjoint from all paths in
\left\{X_{u}\mid u\in Z\right\}
.
We now describe an iteration. Let
\hat{v}
be a vertex in
\hat{V}(P)
that, among all vertices
v
in
\hat{V}(P)
such that
X_{v}
is disjoint from all paths in
\left\{X_{u}\mid u\in Z\right\}
, maximizes
|X_{v}|
. We add vertex
\hat{v}
into
Z
and continue to the next iteration.
This finishes the description of the algorithm.
Let
\bar{V}(P)
be the set
Z
at the end of this algorithm.
It is clear from the algorithm that, for any pair
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices of
\bar{V}(P)
, the paths
X_{v}
and
X_{v^{\prime}}
are disjoint. It remains to show that
\sum_{v\in\hat{V}^{\prime}(P)}|V(X_{v})|\geq|\hat{V}(P)|/5
.

We claim that, if
v=\arg_{\hat{v}\in\hat{V}(P)}\max\left\{|X_{\hat{v}}|\right\}
, then the number of vertices
v^{\prime}
such that
X_{v}\cap X_{v^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
is at most
5|X_{v}|
. Note that this immediately implies that
\sum_{v\in\bar{V}(P)}|V(X_{v})|\geq|\hat{V}(P)|/5
.
It remains to prove the claim.
Let
v_{1}
be a vertex of
\hat{V}(P)
such that
X_{\hat{v}}\cap X_{v}\neq\emptyset
. Since
v_{1}
is a smooth vertex in
P
,
v^{\textnormal{{in}}}_{1}
also belongs to
P
. Note that
v^{\textnormal{{in}}}_{1}
is an endpoint of path
X_{v_{1}}
, and we denote by
x
the other endpoint of
X_{v_{1}}
.
From the optimality of
v
,
|V(X_{v_{1}})|\leq|V(X_{v})|
. Since the tree-distance between
v_{1}
and
X_{v_{1}}
in
T
is at most
h
, the tree-distance between
v_{1}
and
X_{v}
in
T
is at most
|V(X_{v_{1}})|+h\leq 2|V(X_{v})|
. Clearly, there are at most
5|X_{v}|
vertices on
P
that are at tree-distance at most
2|X_{v}|
from
X_{v}
in
T
.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the set of maximal induced subpaths of
T
.
Denote
\bar{V}=\bigcup_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\bar{V}(P)
.
From
3.10
,
|E({\mathcal{R}})|\geq|\hat{V}|/5
, the paths of
{\mathcal{R}}
are mutually disjoint, and each path of
{\mathcal{R}}
have length at least
h
each.
Therefore,
|{\mathcal{R}}|\leq|E({\mathcal{R}})|/h
, and
\sum_{v\in\bar{V}}w(v^{\textnormal{{in}}},v^{\textnormal{{out}}})\leq(h/500)\cdot(|E({\mathcal{R}})|/h)=|E({\mathcal{R}})|/500
.
On the other hand, if we add, for each vertex
v\in\bar{V}
, the edge
(v^{\textnormal{{in}}},v^{\textnormal{{out}}})
to
T
, then all edges of
E({\mathcal{R}})
are contained in some e-block in the resulting graph, and are therefore also contained in some e-block of
G_{1}
.
Additionally, it is easy to see that there is a matching of size at least
|E({\mathcal{R}})|/3
that only contains edges of
E({\mathcal{R}})
. Via similar arguments in the proof of
3.2
, we can show that

\textnormal{{TSP}}(w)\leq 2n-\frac{|E({\mathcal{R}})|}{3}\cdot\frac{2}{3}+\frac{|E({\mathcal{R}})|}{500}\leq 2n-\frac{|E({\mathcal{R}})|}{5}\leq(2-\hat{\varepsilon}/125)n.

Part III
Query Algorithm II

In this part we present our second algorithm in the query model, that, given a distance oracle to a metric
w
and an MST of the complete weighted graph with edge weights given by
w
, estimates the value of
\mathsf{TSP}(w)
to within a factor of
(2-\varepsilon_{0})
, for some universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, by performing
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries to the oracle, thus establishing
Theorem

8
.
We start with some additional preliminaries used in this part, then introduce some crucial subroutines, and finally present the query algorithm and its analysis in
Section

4
.

1
Preliminaries

Let
H
be a subgraph of
G
.
For a set
E^{\prime}
of edges, we denote
w(E^{\prime})=\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}w(e)
. For a subgraph
H^{\prime}
of
H
, we denote
w(H^{\prime})=w(E(H^{\prime}))
, and we denote
w(H)=w(E(H))
.

The following lemma is folklore.

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
H
be any connected Eulerian multigraph on
V
. Then
\mathsf{TSP}\leq w(E(H))
.

We use the following theorem from
[
Beh21
]
.

Theorem 16

(Theorem 1.4 in
[
Beh21
]
​)
.

For any
\hat{\varepsilon}>0
, there is an algorithm that takes an unweighted graph as input, with probability
1-1/\textnormal{poly}(n)
, computes a
(2,\hat{\varepsilon}n)
-approximation to the size of maximum matching, by performing
\tilde{O}(n/\hat{\varepsilon}^{3})
queries to the adjacency matrix of the input graph.

2
Subroutine I: Reorganization of an Independent Set of
c
-Trees

In this section we introduce the first crucial subroutine that will be used in the query algorithm.
At a high level, the subroutine in this section handles the problem in which we are given a set of
simple
subtrees of an MST, and we want to estimate the TSP cost of a tour that only visits all vertices of the given subtrees. It turns out that the simplicity (which will be explained later) of the input subtrees can be properly utilized to reduce the number of queries needed in the TSP cost estimation task.

Let tree
T
be rooted at a vertex
r\in V(T)
. We introduce the following definition.

Definition 2.1
.

We say that a subtree
F
of
T
is a
c
-tree
, for some integer
c>0
, iff tree
F
contains at most
c
leaves.

Definition 2.2
.

We say that a set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
c
-trees in
T
is
independent
, iff (i) the subtrees in
{\mathcal{F}}
are edge-disjoint; and (ii) for every pair
v,v^{\prime}
of vertices in
V({\mathcal{F}})
that serve as non-root vertices of different subtrees of
{\mathcal{F}}
,
v
is neither an ancestor nor a descendant of
v^{\prime}
in
T
.

Skeleton of an independent set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
c
-trees.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be an independent set of
c
-trees of
T
.
For each
c
-tree
F\in{\mathcal{F}}
, we denote by
r_{F}
the root of
F
(recall that the root
r_{F}
is the vertex of
V(F)
that is closest to the root
r
of
T
).
Let tree
T^{\prime}
be obtained from
T
by contracting all edges of
E(T)\setminus E({\mathcal{F}})
.
We call tree
T^{\prime}
the
skeleton
of
{\mathcal{F}}
.
Since the set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
c
-trees is independent, it is not hard to see that, equivalently,
T^{\prime}
can be viewed as the graph obtained by taking the union of all trees in
{\mathcal{F}}
and then identifying all vertices of
\left\{r_{F}\right\}_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}
.
We denote
V^{\prime}=V(T^{\prime})
and denote by
r^{\prime}
the vertex of
T^{\prime}
that corresponds to vertices of
\left\{r_{F}\right\}_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}
, then every vertex of
V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
is also a vertex of
V({\mathcal{F}})
.

Based on the given metric
w
on
V
,
we define a function
w^{\prime}:V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
as follows.
For every pair
v_{1},v_{2}\in V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
, we define
w^{\prime}(v_{1},v_{2})=w(v_{1},v_{2})
.
For every
v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
, we define
w^{\prime}(r^{\prime},v)=w(v_{F},v)
, where
F
is the
c
-tree of
{\mathcal{F}}
that contains
v
. Note that
w^{\prime}
may not be a metric on
V^{\prime}
. But since
w
is a metric, the restriction of
w^{\prime}
on
(V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\})\times(V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\})
is a metric. We call
w^{\prime}
the
induced metric
of
w
on
V^{\prime}
.

We prove the following observation.

Observation 2.1
.

T^{\prime}
is a minimum spanning tree of
w^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume for contradiction that
T^{\prime}
is not a spanning tree of
w^{\prime}
. Then there is a pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
with
(v,v^{\prime})\notin E(T^{\prime})
, such that
w^{\prime}(v,v^{\prime})<\max\left\{w(e)\mid e\in P_{T^{\prime}}(v,v^{\prime})\right\}
. Assume first that
v,v^{\prime}\neq r
, so
w^{\prime}(v,v^{\prime})=w(v,v^{\prime})
. Since both
v,v^{\prime}
are vertices of
V
, from the construction of
T^{\prime}
,
E(P_{T^{\prime}}(v,v^{\prime}))\subseteq E(P_{T}(v,v^{\prime}))
, and it follows that
w(v,v^{\prime})<\max\left\{w(e)\mid e\in P_{T}(v,v^{\prime})\right\}
, which is a contradiction to the fact that
T
is a minimum spanning tree of
w
. Assume now that
v=r
. We denote by
P
the path of
{\mathcal{P}}
that contains
v^{\prime}
, and denote by
v^{\prime\prime}
the endpoint of
P
that is closer to
r
in
T
. From the definition of
T^{\prime}
,
w^{\prime}(v,v^{\prime})=w(v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore,
w(v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})<\max\left\{w(e)\mid e\in P_{T^{\prime}}(v,v^{\prime})\right\}=\max\left\{w(e)\mid e\in P_{T}(v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime})\right\}
, a contradiction to the fact that
T
is a minimum spanning tree of
w
.
∎

Special walks on a skeleton.

A
walk
on
V^{\prime}
is a sequence
\sigma=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{k})
of vertices of
V^{\prime}
, where vertices in sequence
\sigma
are allowed to repeat.
We say that a walk
\sigma
on
V^{\prime}
is a
special walk
, iff
\sigma
starts and ends at
r^{\prime}
, and every vertex of
V\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
is visited exactly once (while the vertex
r^{\prime}
can be visited an arbitrary number of times).
Let
w:V\times V\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
be any metric on
V
, and let
w^{\prime}:V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}
be the induced metric of
w
on
V^{\prime}
. The
minimum special walk cost
of
w^{\prime}
, denoted by
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})
, is defined to be the minimum cost of any special walk on
V^{\prime}
, where the cost of a walk
\sigma
is defined to be
w^{\prime}(\sigma)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k-1}w^{\prime}(v_{i},v_{i+1})
.

The main result of this section is the following lemma, whose proof is deferred to
Section

2.1
.

Lemma 2.1
.

There is an efficient algorithm, that takes as input a constant
0<\varepsilon<10^{-9}
, an integer
0<c<10^{3}
, a metric
w
on
V
and a set
{\mathcal{F}}
of independent
c
-trees of
T
, either correctly reports that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
where
w^{\prime}
is the induced metric of
w
on
V^{\prime}=V(T^{\prime})
and
T^{\prime}
is the skeleton of
{\mathcal{F}}
, or correctly reports that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\geq(2-c_{0}\cdot c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
, where
c_{0}
is some universal constant, by performing
\tilde{O}(n)
queries.

We then obtain the following crucial corollary, whose proof is deferred to
Section

2.2
.

Corollary 2.1
.

There is an efficient algorithm, that takes as input a constant
0<\varepsilon<10^{-9}
, an integer
0<c<10^{3}
, a metric
w
on
V
and an independent set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
c
-trees in
T
such that
\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}w(F)\geq(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w)
,
computes an
\big(2-O(\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{\log(1/\varepsilon)})\big)
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}(w)
, by performing
\tilde{O}(n)
queries.

2.1
Proof of
Lemma

2.1

In this section we provide the proof of
Lemma

2.1
.
We denote the
c
-trees of
{\mathcal{F}}
by
F_{1},\ldots,F_{k}
, and for each
1\leq i\leq k
, we denote
w^{\prime}_{i}=w^{\prime}(F_{i})
, so
{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})=\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}w^{\prime}_{i}
.

Consider a pair
F_{i},F_{j}
of distinct
c
-trees in
{\mathcal{F}}
. Let
V^{*}_{i}
be the set of special vertices of
F_{i}
, and we define set
V^{*}_{j}
similarly. We define
\zeta_{i,j}
to be the maximum cover advantage of any subset of edges with both endpoints in
V^{*}_{i}\cup V^{*}_{j}
on
F_{i}\cup F_{j}
. Since
|V^{*}_{i}|,|V^{*}_{j}|\leq 2c
, the value of
\zeta_{i,j}
can be computed with
O(c^{2})
queries.

We construct a weighted graph
L
as follows. We use a parameter
0.9<\alpha<1
whose value will be set later. The vertex set of
L
is
V(L)=\left\{1,\ldots,k\right\}
. For every pair
i,j\in V(L)
, set
E(L)
contains the edge
(i,j)
iff
\zeta_{i,j}\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot(w^{\prime}_{i}+w^{\prime}_{j})
, and, if such an edge exists in
E(L)
, then it has weight
w^{\prime}_{i}+w^{\prime}_{j}
. Let
\mathsf{MM}(L)
be the maximum total weight of a matching in
L
. We prove the following claims.

Claim 2.1
.

There is an algorithm that performs
\tilde{O}(c^{2}n)
queries and outputs an estimate
X
of
\mathsf{MM}(L)
, such that

X\leq\mathsf{MM}(L)\leq\frac{16\cdot\log(\frac{1}{1-\alpha})}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\cdot X+\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)+\frac{{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})}{\log^{8}n}.

Proof.

Denote
q=\log_{2}(n^{2}/\varepsilon)
. For each
1\leq t\leq q
, we define set
V_{t}=\left\{i\mid 2^{t}\leq w^{\prime}_{i}<2^{t+1}\right\}
. Clearly, sets
V_{1},\ldots,V_{q}
partition set
V(L)
.
Define
\ell=\left\lceil\log_{2}\big(\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\big)\right\rceil
.
Let graph
L^{\prime}
be obtained from
L
by removing all edges
(i,i^{\prime})
such that

\zeta_{i,i^{\prime}}\geq 2\cdot\min\left\{w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\right\}+(1-\alpha)^{3}\cdot\max\left\{w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\right\}
.

We first prove the following observations.

Observation 2.2
.

Let
t,t^{\prime}
be any pair such that
t^{\prime}>t+\ell
, and let
i,i^{\prime}
be any two indices such that
i\in V_{t}
and
i^{\prime}\in V_{t^{\prime}}
. Then the edge
(i,i^{\prime})
does not belong to
L^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
t^{\prime}>t+\ell
, from the definition of
\ell
,
w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}>\frac{1}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\cdot w^{\prime}_{i}
. If
\zeta_{i,i^{\prime}}\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot(w^{\prime}_{i}+w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})
, then

\begin{split}\zeta_{i,i^{\prime}}\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot w^{\prime}_{i}=&\text{ }(1-\alpha)\cdot\bigg((1-\alpha)^{2}\cdot w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+(2\alpha-\alpha^{2})\cdot w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\bigg)\\
>&\text{ }(1-\alpha)^{3}\cdot w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+(1-\alpha)\cdot(2\alpha-\alpha^{2})\cdot\frac{w^{\prime}_{i}}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\\
=&\text{ }(1-\alpha)^{3}\cdot w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}+2\cdot w^{\prime}_{i}\\
=&\text{ }(1-\alpha)^{3}\cdot\max\left\{w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\right\}+2\cdot\min\left\{w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}\right\}.\end{split}

From the definition of
L^{\prime}
, the edge
(i,i^{\prime})
does not belong to
L^{\prime}
.
∎

Observation 2.3
.

\mathsf{MM}(L\setminus L^{\prime})\leq\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)
.

Proof.

Let
M^{\prime\prime}
be a matching in graph
L\setminus L^{\prime}
. Consider an edge
(i,j)\in M^{\prime\prime}
and assume that
w^{\prime}_{i}\geq w^{\prime}_{j}
, so
\zeta_{i,j}\geq 2\cdot w^{\prime}_{j}+(1-\alpha)^{3}\cdot w^{\prime}_{i}
.
Let
E^{\prime}_{i,j}
be the set of edges with both endpoints in
V^{*}_{i}\cup V^{*}_{j}
that achieves the maximum cover advantage on
F_{i}\cup F_{j}
.
Consider now the cover advantage of set
E^{\prime}_{i,j}
on
F_{i}
. Note that

w^{\prime}({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime}_{i,j},F_{i}))\geq w^{\prime}({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime}_{i,j},F_{i}\cup F_{j}))-w^{\prime}(F_{j})=\zeta_{i,j}-w^{\prime}_{j}>(1-\alpha)^{3}\cdot w^{\prime}_{i}.

Therefore, since all edges of
E^{\prime}_{i,j}
have one endpoint being a special vertex of
F_{i}
, we get that
\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{i})\geq(1-\alpha)^{3}\cdot w^{\prime}_{i}\geq\frac{(1-\alpha)^{3}}{2}\cdot(w^{\prime}_{i}+w^{\prime}_{j})
.
Altogether,

\sum_{(i,j)\in M^{\prime\prime}}(w^{\prime}_{i}+w^{\prime}_{j})\leq\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{(i,j)\in M^{\prime\prime}}\bigg(\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{i})+\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j})\bigg)\leq\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F).

∎

Observation 2.4
.

A pair query in graph
L^{\prime}
can be simulated by
O(c^{2})
distance queries in
w
.

Proof.

Since for each pair
i,j\in[k]
, the value of
\zeta_{i,j}
can be computed using
O(c^{2})
queries, and whether or not the edge
(i,j)
belongs to graph
L^{\prime}
depends solely on the value of
\zeta_{i,j}
, the pair query
(i,j)
for any pair
i,j\in[k]
can be obtained by
O(c^{2})
distance queries in
w
.
∎

For each pair
1\leq t,t^{\prime}\leq q
such that
|t-t^{\prime}|\leq\ell
, we define
L^{\prime}_{t,t^{\prime}}
as the unweighted graph on
V_{t}\cup V_{t^{\prime}}
, whose edge set is
E_{L^{\prime}}(V_{t}\cup V_{t^{\prime}})
. We then apply the algorithm from
Theorem

16
(with
\hat{\varepsilon}=1/\log^{9}n
) to the unweighted graph
L^{\prime}_{t,t^{\prime}}
. Let
X_{t,t^{\prime}}
be the estimate we obtain, so
X_{t,t^{\prime}}\leq\mathsf{MM}(L^{\prime}_{t,t^{\prime}})\leq 2\cdot X_{t,t^{\prime}}+\hat{\varepsilon}\cdot|V_{t}\cup V_{t^{\prime}}|
.
Since
q=O(\log n)
and
\ell=O(1)
, combined with
2.4
, the number of queries performed by all the applications of the algorithm from
Theorem

16
is
\tilde{O}(c^{2}n)
.
For each
0\leq r\leq\ell
, we compute
Y_{r}=\sum_{1\leq t\leq q}X_{t,t+r}\cdot 2^{t}
. We compute
X=\max_{0\leq r\leq\ell}\left\{Y_{r}/2\right\}
and return
X
as the estimate of
\mathsf{MM}(L)
. It remains to show that
X
satisfies the properties in
2.1
.

For each pair
1\leq t,t^{\prime}\leq q
such that
|t-t^{\prime}|\leq\ell
, we let
M^{\prime}_{t,t^{\prime}}
be a maximum matching in graph
L^{\prime}_{t,t^{\prime}}
, and denote by
M_{t,t^{\prime}}
the corresponding matching in
L
. Clearly, for each
0\leq r\leq\ell
,
M_{r}=\bigcup_{1\leq t\leq q}M_{t,t_{r}}
is the union of two matchings in
L
. Additionally, according to the definition of the partition
(V_{1},\ldots,V_{q})
and the definition of edges of
L
, we get that
w^{\prime}(M_{r})\geq Y_{r}
.
Therefore, each
Y_{r}
is a lower bound for
2\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)
, and so
X\leq\mathsf{MM}(L)
. On the other hand, let
M^{*}
be a maximum weight matching in
L
. For each
0\leq r\leq\ell
and for each
1\leq t\leq q
, we denote
M^{*}_{t,t+r}=M^{*}\cap E(V_{t},V_{t+r})
. From
Theorem

16
, we get that

w(M^{*}_{t,t+r})\leq 2\cdot 2^{t+\ell}\cdot|M^{*}_{t,t+r}|\leq 2\cdot 2^{t+\ell}\cdot(2\cdot X_{t,t+r}+\hat{\varepsilon}\cdot|V_{t}\cup V_{t+r}|).

From the definition of sets
V_{t}
and
V_{t+r}
,

|V_{t}\cup V_{t+r}|\leq{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})/2^{t}
.
Therefore, combined with
2.3
, we get that

\begin{split}w(M^{*})&\leq\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)+\sum_{0\leq r\leq\ell}\sum_{1\leq t\leq q}w(M^{*}_{t,t+r})\\
&\leq\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)+\sum_{0\leq r\leq\ell}\sum_{1\leq t\leq q}2\cdot 2^{t+\ell}\cdot\bigg(2\cdot X_{t,t+r}+\hat{\varepsilon}\cdot|V_{t}\cup V_{t+r}|\bigg)\\
&\leq\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)+4\cdot\sum_{0\leq r\leq\ell}2^{\ell}\cdot\bigg(\bigg(\sum_{1\leq t\leq q}2^{t}\cdot X_{t,t+r}\bigg)+\hat{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})\bigg)\\
&=\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)+2^{\ell+2}\cdot\bigg(\hat{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})+\sum_{0\leq r\leq\ell}Y_{r}\bigg)\\
&\leq\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)+2^{\ell+2}\cdot(\ell+1)\cdot 2X+2^{\ell+2}\cdot\hat{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})\\
&\leq\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)+\frac{16\cdot\log\big(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\big)}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\cdot X+\frac{{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})}{\log^{8}n}.\end{split}

This completes the proof of
2.1
.
∎

Claim 2.2
.

\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-\frac{(1-\alpha)}{2}\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)
.

Proof.

Let
M
be the max-weight matching in
L
. We construct a special walk
\sigma
as follows. Consider first a pair
(i,j)\in M
. From
Lemma

2.3
, we know that there is a walk
\sigma_{i,j}
that starts and ends at
r^{\prime}
, visits all vertices of
F_{i}\cup F_{j}
exactly once, and has cost
w^{\prime}(\sigma_{i,j})\leq w^{\prime}(F_{i})+w^{\prime}(F_{j})-\zeta_{i,j}/2
.
Consider now an index
i
that does not participate in any pair in
M
, we define
\sigma_{i}
to be the walk obtained from the Euler tour of
F_{i}
by deleting the repeated appearance of all vertices. So the walk
\sigma_{i}
starts and ends at
r^{\prime}
, visits all vertices of
V(F_{i})
exactly once, and
w^{\prime}(\sigma_{i})\leq 2\cdot w^{\prime}_{i}
.
Finally we define the walk
\sigma
as the concatenation of all walks
\left\{\sigma_{i,j}\mid(i,j)\in M\right\}\cup\left\{\sigma_{i}\mid i\text{ does not participate in any pair of }M\right\}
.
It is not hard to verify that
\sigma
is a special walk.
From the above discussion, we get that

\begin{split}w^{\prime}(\sigma)&\leq\sum_{(i,j)\in M}(w^{\prime}_{i}+w^{\prime}_{j}-\zeta_{i,j})+\sum_{i:(i,*)\notin M}2\cdot w^{\prime}_{i}\\
&\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-\frac{(1-\alpha)}{2}\cdot\sum_{(i,j)\in M}(w^{\prime}_{i}+w^{\prime}_{j})\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-\frac{(1-\alpha)}{2}\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L).\end{split}

This completes the proof of
2.2
.
∎

Claim 2.3
.

\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\geq\bigg(2-3c\cdot(1-\alpha)\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)-4c\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be the minimum cost special walk on
V^{\prime}
, so
w^{\prime}(\sigma)=\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})
.
Define
V^{*}=\big(\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}V^{*}_{i}\big)\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
to be the set of all non-root special vertices of all subtrees in
{\mathcal{F}}
.
Denote
V^{*}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}=\left\{v^{*}_{1},\ldots,v^{*}_{s}\right\}
.
Since each vertex of
V^{*}
is visited exactly once in the walk
\sigma
, up to naming, we can assume without loss of generality that the walk
\sigma
can be partitioned into subwalks
\sigma=(v^{*}_{1},\sigma_{1},v^{*}_{2},\sigma_{2},\ldots,v^{*}_{s},\sigma_{s},v^{*}_{1})
, where for each
1\leq i\leq s
, the walk
\sigma_{i}
does not contain any vertex of
V^{*}
.

Fix an index
1\leq i\leq s
and consider the subwalk
(v^{*}_{i},\sigma_{i},v^{*}_{i+1})
. Assume first that
r^{\prime}\notin V(\sigma_{i})
. From the definition of
w^{\prime}
, for every pair
v,v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
,
w^{\prime}(v,v^{\prime})=w(v,v^{\prime})
. Since
w
is a metric, the total cost of the subwalk
(v^{*}_{i},\sigma_{i},v^{*}_{i+1})
is at least
w^{\prime}(v^{*}_{i},v^{*}_{i+1})
, which is, assuming
v^{*}_{i}\in V^{*}_{j}
and
v^{*}_{i+1}\in V^{*}_{j^{\prime}}
, at least
{\mathsf{cov}}(e^{*}_{i},F_{j}\cup F_{j^{\prime}})-\zeta_{j,j^{\prime}}
. Assume now that
r^{\prime}\in V(\sigma_{i})
. We further partition the walk
\sigma_{i}
at the first and the last appearances of
r^{\prime}
as
\sigma_{i}=(\sigma^{a}_{i},r^{\prime},\sigma^{b}_{i},r^{\prime},\sigma^{c}_{i})
(if
r^{\prime}
appears only once in
\sigma_{i}
, then
\sigma^{b}_{i}
is an empty subwalk).
Assume
v^{*}_{i}
belongs to
V^{*}_{j}
, the set of special vertices of tree
F_{j}
.
Note that, the subwalk
\sigma^{a}_{i}
may or may not contain vertices outside of
F_{j}
, and it is not hard to see that, in either case, the
w^{\prime}
-cost of the subwalk
(v^{*}_{i},\sigma^{a}_{i},r^{\prime})
is at least
w^{\prime}(v^{*}_{i},r_{F_{j}})-\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j})
, and similarly, if
v^{*}_{i+1}
belongs to
V^{*}_{j^{\prime}}
, then the
w^{\prime}
-cost of the subwalk
(v^{*}_{i},\sigma^{c}_{i},r^{\prime})
is at least
w^{\prime}(v^{*}_{i+1},r_{F_{j^{\prime}}})-\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j^{\prime}})
.
Therefore, if we denote
e^{*}_{i}=(v^{*}_{i},v^{*}_{i+1})
, then

\begin{split}w^{\prime}(v^{*}_{i},\sigma_{i},v^{*}_{i+1})\geq&\text{ }w^{\prime}(v^{*}_{i},r_{F_{j}})+w^{\prime}(v^{*}_{i+1},r_{F_{j^{\prime}}})-\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j})-\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j^{\prime}})\\
\geq&\text{ }w^{\prime}(P^{F_{j}}_{v^{*}_{i},r_{F_{j}}})+w^{\prime}(P^{F_{j^{\prime}}}_{v^{*}_{i+1},r_{F_{j^{\prime}}}})-2\cdot\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j})-2\cdot\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j^{\prime}})\\
=&\text{ }{\mathsf{cov}}(e^{*}_{i},F_{j}\cup F_{j^{\prime}})-2\cdot\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j})-2\cdot\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j^{\prime}})\\
=&\text{ }{\mathsf{cov}}(e^{*}_{i},T^{\prime})-2\cdot\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j})-2\cdot\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j^{\prime}}).\end{split}

Denote
E^{*}=\left\{(e^{*}_{i},e^{*}_{i+1})\mid i\in[s]\right\}
. We say that an edge
(e^{*}_{i},e^{*}_{i+1})
in
E^{*}
is a type-1 edge iff
r^{\prime}\notin V(\sigma_{i})
, otherwise we say that it is a type-2 edge. We denote by
E^{*}_{1}
the set of all type-1 edges, and we define set
E^{*}_{2}
similarly. For each
1\leq j\leq k
, we denote by
E^{*}_{1,j}
the set of all type-1 edges with at least one endpoint in
V^{*}_{j}
, and we define the set
E^{*}_{2,j}
similarly.

On the one hand, clearly for each
1\leq j\leq k
,
|E^{*}_{1,j}|\leq|V^{*}_{j}|\leq 2c
. Therefore, from Vizing’s theorem
[
Viz64
]
, set
E^{*}_{1}
can be partitioned into at most
2c+1
subsets, such that in each of these subsets, no two edges have endpoint in the same set of
\left\{V^{*}_{1},\ldots,V^{*}_{k}\right\}
.
On the other hand, clearly for each
1\leq j\leq k
,
|E^{*}_{1,j}|\leq|V^{*}_{j}|\leq 2c
. Altogether, if we define, for each
1\leq i\leq s
,
j(i)
to be the index
j\in[k]
such that
v^{*}_{i}\in V^{*}_{j}
, then

\begin{split}\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\geq&\text{ }\bigg(2-3c\cdot(1-\alpha)\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)-4c\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\\
\geq&\text{ }2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-\sum_{i:e^{*}_{i}\in E^{*}_{1}}\zeta_{j(i),j(i+1)}-\sum_{i:e^{*}_{i}\in E^{*}_{2}}\bigg(2\cdot\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j(i)})+2\cdot\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{j(i+1)})\bigg)\\
\geq&\text{ }2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-\sum_{i:e^{*}_{i}\in E^{*}_{1}}\zeta_{j(i),j(i+1)}-2\cdot 2c\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\\
\geq&\text{ }2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-(2c+1)\cdot\bigg(\mathsf{MM}(L)+(1-\alpha)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})\bigg)-4c\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\\
=&\text{ }\bigg(2-(2c+1)(1-\alpha)\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-(2c+1)\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)-4c\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\\
=&\text{ }\bigg(2-3c\cdot(1-\alpha)\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)-4c\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F).\end{split}

This completes the proof of
2.3
.
∎

Algorithm.

We now describe the algorithm for
Lemma

2.1
.
We use the parameters

\alpha=1-\varepsilon
and set
c_{0}=100
.

We first apply the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
to tree
T
and the independent set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
c
-trees with parameter
2\varepsilon^{4}
. If the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
reports that
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(P)>2\varepsilon^{4}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
, then we report that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
. Otherwise,
we then construct the graph
L
as described before and apply the algorithm from
2.1
to obtain an estimate
X
of
\mathsf{MM}(L)
.
If
X\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{\log(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
, we report that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
.
Otherwise, we report that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq(2-c_{0}\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
.

Proof of Correctness.

We now prove the correctness of the algorithm.
Since the algorithm calls the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
once and the algorithm from
2.1
once, it performs
\tilde{O}(c^{2}n)
queries.

Assume first that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
reports that
\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)>2\varepsilon^{4}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
.
Then from similar arguments in the proof of
Lemma

2.3
, it is easy to show that

\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\leq(2-\varepsilon^{4})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime}),

so our report that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
is correct in this case. We assume now that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
reports that
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(P)<4\varepsilon^{4}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. In this case, assume first that the estimate
X
given by algorithm from
2.1
satisfies that
X\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{\log(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
. Then from
2.2
and
2.1
,

\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-\frac{(1-\alpha)}{2}\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot X\leq\bigg(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)}\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime}).

so our report that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
is correct.
Assume now
X<\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{\log(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
. Then from
2.3
and
2.1
,

\begin{split}\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\geq&\text{ }\bigg(2-3c\cdot(1-\alpha)\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)-4c\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\\
\geq&\text{ }(2-3c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)-4c\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\\
\geq&\text{ }(2-3c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)-4c\cdot 4\varepsilon^{4}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})\\
\geq&\text{ }(2-4c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\mathsf{MM}(L)\\
\geq&\text{ }(2-4c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\bigg(\frac{16\cdot\log(\frac{1}{1-\alpha})}{(1-\alpha)^{2}}\cdot X+\frac{2}{(1-\alpha)^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)+\frac{{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})}{\log^{8}n}\bigg)\\
\geq&\text{ }(2-5c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\bigg(\frac{16\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot X+\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{3}}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\bigg)\\
\geq&\text{ }(2-5c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\frac{16\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot X-24\varepsilon\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})\\
\geq&\text{ }(2-29c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-3c\cdot\frac{16\cdot\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{\log(1/\varepsilon)}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})\\
\geq&\text{ }\bigg(2-\big(29c+48c\big)\cdot\varepsilon\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})\geq(2-c_{0}\cdot c\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime}).\end{split}

where the last step is due to the definition of
c_{0}
, and so our report that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime},r^{\prime})>(2-c_{0}\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
is correct in this case.
This completes the proof of
Lemma

2.1
.

2.2
Proof of
Corollary

2.1

In this subsection we provide the proof of
Corollary

2.1
.
We first prove the following claims.

Claim 2.4
.

\mathsf{TSP}(w)\geq\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})-2\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)
.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be an optimal TSP-tour on
V
, so
w(\pi)=\mathsf{TSP}
. We will convert tour
\pi
into a special walk on
V^{\prime}
as follows.
Let
{\mathcal{U}}
be the set of connected components of
T\setminus\big(\bigcup_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}F\big)
. It is easy to see that there is a unique connected component
U_{0}\in{\mathcal{U}}
that contains all vertices of
\left\{r_{F}\mid F\in{\mathcal{F}}\right\}
.

Recall that each vertex of
V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
is a vertex of
V
.
We denote
V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}=\left\{v^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{s}\right\}
, where the vertices are indexed according to their appearance in
\pi
.
We first partition the tour
\pi
at vertices of
V^{\prime}
into
\pi=(v^{\prime}_{1},\sigma_{1},v^{\prime}_{2},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{s},v^{\prime}_{1})
, where each
\sigma_{i}
is a simple walk in
V({\mathcal{U}})
.
Assume without loss of generality (up to naming on vertices of
V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
) that
\sigma_{1}
contains a vertex of
U_{0}
.
We then construct a walk
\sigma
as
\sigma=(v^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime}_{3},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{s},v^{\prime}_{1})
. Clearly,
\sigma
is a special walk on
V^{\prime}
.

We now show that
w^{\prime}(\sigma)\leq\mathsf{TSP}+2\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)
. On the one hand, for each
2\leq i\leq s
, from the definition of
w^{\prime}
and the triangle inequality of metric
w
,
w^{\prime}(v^{\prime}_{i},v^{\prime}_{i+1})
is at most the total
w
-cost of the walk
(v^{\prime}_{i},\sigma_{i},v^{\prime}_{i+1})
. On the other hand, let
v^{\prime}
be an arbitrary vertex of
V(U_{0})\cap V(\sigma_{1})
, then the total
w
-cost of the walk
(v^{\prime}_{1},\sigma_{1},v^{\prime}_{2})
is at least
w(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime})+w(v^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{2})
.
Assume that
v^{\prime}_{1}\in F_{1}
and
v^{\prime}_{2}\in F_{2}
.
Then from the definition of
w^{\prime}
,
w^{\prime}(v^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime})+w^{\prime}(r^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{2})=w(v^{\prime}_{1},r_{F_{1}})+w(v^{\prime}_{2},r_{F_{2}})
.

Now if we consider the edge
e^{\prime}_{1}=(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime})
, from
Lemma

2.4
, we get that

\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{1})\geq\mathsf{adv}(e^{\prime}_{1},F_{1})\geq w(P^{T}_{v^{\prime}_{1},r_{F_{1}}})-w(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime})\geq w^{\prime}(r^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{1})-w(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}),

and similarly, if we denote
e^{\prime}_{2}=(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime})
, then

\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{2})\geq\mathsf{adv}(e^{\prime}_{2},F_{2})\geq w(P^{T}_{v^{\prime}_{2},r_{F_{2}}})-w(v^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime})\geq w^{\prime}(r^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{2})-w(v^{\prime}_{2},v^{\prime}).

Since
\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{1})+\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F_{2})\leq 2\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)
, we get that

\bigg(w(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime})+w(v^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{2})\bigg)-\bigg(w^{\prime}(v^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime})+w^{\prime}(v^{\prime}_{2},r^{\prime})\bigg)\leq 2\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F).

Altogether, we get that

\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq w^{\prime}(\sigma)\leq\mathsf{TSP}-(w(v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime})+w(v^{\prime},v^{\prime}_{2}))+w^{\prime}(v^{\prime}_{1},r^{\prime})+w^{\prime}(v^{\prime}_{2},r^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{TSP}+2\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F).

This completes the proof of
2.4
.
∎

Claim 2.5
.

\mathsf{TSP}(w)\leq\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})+2\cdot w(E(T)\setminus E({\mathcal{F}}))
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be the special walk on
V^{\prime}
with minimum cost. We first partition
\sigma
at every appearance of
r^{\prime}
into
\sigma=(r^{\prime},\sigma_{1},r^{\prime},\sigma_{2},r^{\prime}\ldots,r^{\prime},\sigma_{s},r^{\prime})
. From the definition of a special walk, each
\sigma_{i}
is a walk in
V^{\prime}
that does not contain
r^{\prime}
, and the sets
\left\{V(\sigma_{i})\right\}_{1\leq i\leq s}
partitions
V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
.
Recall that every vertex in
V^{\prime}\setminus\left\{r^{\prime}\right\}
is also a vertex of
V^{\prime}
.

We construct a Eulerian multigraph
H
on
V
as follows.
Similar as the proof of
2.4
, we let
{\mathcal{U}}
be the set of connected components obtained from
T
by deleting all subtrees of
{\mathcal{F}}
, and let
U_{0}
be the unique connected component in
{\mathcal{U}}
that contains all vertices of
\left\{r_{F}\mid F\in{\mathcal{F}}\right\}
. So
E(T)=E({\mathcal{F}})\cup(\bigcup_{U\in{\mathcal{U}}}E(U))
.

For each
U\in{\mathcal{U}}\setminus\left\{U_{0}\right\}
, we denote by
H_{U}
the multigraph on
V(U)
that contains two copies of every edge of
E(U)
, so
w(H_{U})=2\cdot w(U)
.
We define multigraph
H_{U_{0}}
as follows.
For each
1\leq i\leq s
, we denote by
v_{i}
(
v^{\prime}_{i}
, resp.) the first (last, resp.) vertex of
\sigma_{i}
, and we denote by
u_{i}
(
u^{\prime}_{i}
, resp.) the closer-to-
r
endpoint of the subtree in
{\mathcal{F}}
that contains
v_{i}
(
v^{\prime}_{i}
, resp.). We denote
M=\left\{(u_{i},u^{\prime}_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq s\right\}
.
We set
H_{U_{0}}=(V(U_{0}),E_{[U_{0},M]})
(see definition in
Section

2
), so
w(H_{U_{0}})\leq 2\cdot w(U_{0})
.
Then for each
1\leq i\leq s
, we define the
L_{i}
to be the graph induced by edges of
E(\sigma_{i})\cup\left\{(v_{i},u_{i}),(v^{\prime}_{i},u^{\prime}_{i})\right\}
. So for each
1\leq i\leq s
,
w(L_{i})
equals the
w^{\prime}
-cost of the subwalk
(r^{\prime},\sigma_{i},r^{\prime})
of
\sigma
, and it follows that
\sum_{1\leq i\leq s}w(L_{i})=\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})
.
Finally, we define graph

H=(\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq s}L_{i})\cup(\bigcup_{U\in{\mathcal{U}}}H_{U})
.
It is not hard to verify that
H
is a connected Eulerian graph on
V
.
From
Lemma

1.1
,
\mathsf{TSP}\leq w(H)
.

On the other hand,

w(H)=\sum_{1\leq i\leq s}w(L_{i})+\sum_{U\in{\mathcal{U}}}w(H_{U})\leq\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})+2\cdot\sum_{U\in{\mathcal{U}}}w(U)=\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})+2\cdot w(E(T)\setminus E({\mathcal{F}})).

Altogether, we get that
\mathsf{TSP}\leq\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})+2\cdot w(E(T)\setminus E({\mathcal{F}}))
.
∎

We now describe the algorithm for
Corollary

2.1
.
We use parameters
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon/4
and
\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{0}/(4\cdot c\cdot c_{0})
, so
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}=\Omega(\varepsilon)
.

We first apply the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
to tree
T
and the independent set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
c
-trees in
T
, with parameter
\varepsilon_{1}
. If it reports that
\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)>\varepsilon_{1}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w)
, then we return
(2-\varepsilon_{1}/2)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as an estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
. Otherwise, we continue to apply the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
to metric
w
, the set independent set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
c
-trees in
T
with parameter
\varepsilon_{2}
. If the algorithm reports that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq(2-\varepsilon^{4}_{2}/2\log(1/\varepsilon_{2}))\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
, then we return
(2-\varepsilon^{4}_{2}/4\log(1/\varepsilon_{2}))\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as an estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
. Otherwise, we return
2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

We now prove the correctness of the above algorithm.
First, since the algorithm calls the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
once and the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
at most once, it performs
\tilde{O}(n)
queries.
Let
X
be the estimate returned by the algorithm. We now show that
\mathsf{TSP}\leq X\leq(2-\Omega(\varepsilon^{4}/\log(1/\varepsilon)))\cdot\mathsf{TSP}
.

Assume first that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
reports that
\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)>\varepsilon_{1}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.
Then from
Lemma

2.3
and the fact that
\mathsf{adv}(F)\geq\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)
for every tree
F\in{\mathcal{F}}
,

\mathsf{TSP}\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}(F)\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\leq\bigg(2-\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2}\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}},

so our estimate
X=(2-\varepsilon_{1}/2)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\varepsilon))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
. Assume now that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.8
reports that
\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)<2\varepsilon_{1}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. In this case, assume first that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
reports that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq(2-\varepsilon^{4}_{2}/2\log(1/\varepsilon_{2}))\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
. Then from
2.5
,

\begin{split}\mathsf{TSP}&\leq\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime},r^{\prime})+2\cdot w(E(T)\setminus E({\mathcal{F}}))\\
&\leq\bigg(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}_{2}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon_{2})}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+2\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\leq\bigg(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}_{2}}{4\log(1/\varepsilon_{2})}\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}},\end{split}

so our estimate
X=(2-\varepsilon^{4}_{2}/4\log(1/\varepsilon_{2}))\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\varepsilon^{4}/\log(1/\varepsilon)))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.
Assume now that the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
reports that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\geq(2-c_{0}\cdot c\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
. Then from
2.4
,

\begin{split}\mathsf{TSP}&\geq\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})-2\cdot\sum_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}}\mathsf{adv}^{*}(F)\\
&\geq(2-c_{0}\cdot c\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})-2\varepsilon_{1}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\\
&\geq(2-c_{0}\cdot c\cdot\varepsilon_{2})\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-2\varepsilon_{1}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\\
&\geq\bigg(1+2\varepsilon-\frac{c_{0}\cdot c\cdot\varepsilon_{2}}{2}-c_{0}\cdot c\cdot\varepsilon_{2}\cdot\varepsilon-2\varepsilon_{1}\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\geq(1+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}.\end{split}

and so our estimate
X=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is a
\big(2-\Omega\big(\varepsilon^{4}/\log(1/\varepsilon)\big)\big)
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.
This completes the proof of
Corollary

2.1
.

3
Subroutine II: Light Subtrees, Segments, and Extensions

In this section, we introduce the second main subroutine that will be used in the query algorithm. Intuitively, the subroutine in this section partitions the tree into top and bottom parts, similar to the techniques used in the first query algorithm in Part II. In addition, we also further partition the top part of the MST into segments, that will be used later as basic objects to compute cover advantage on.

Definition 3.1

(Maximal Light Vertices)
.

We say that a vertex
v
is
maximal
\ell
-light
for some integer
\ell>0
, iff the subtree
T_{v}
contains at most
\ell
vertices, and the subtree
T_{v^{\prime}}
contains more than
\ell
vertices, where
v^{\prime}
is the parent node of
v
in
T
.

We denote by
L_{\ell}
the set of all maximal
\ell
-light vertices.
Let
v
be a maximal
\ell
-light vertex and let
v^{\prime}
be the parent vertex of
v
in
T
. We denote
T^{+}_{v}=T_{v}\cup\left\{(v,v^{\prime})\right\}
, so
T^{+}_{v}
is a subtree of
T
(but note that
T^{+}_{v}
is not equal to
T_{v^{\prime}}
since
v^{\prime}
may have other child nodes in
T
). We call
T^{+}_{v}
the
\ell
-light subtree
of
T
at
v
. We denote
w_{\sf{light}}=\sum_{v\in L_{\ell}}w(T^{+}_{v})
.

Next, we compute a partition of the tree
T
into segments (which we will define later), as follows.
Let
T^{\prime}
be the tree obtained from
T
by deleting from it, for each vertex
v\in L_{\ell}
, all edges and vertices (except for the root) of
T^{+}_{v}
. We use the following observation.

Observation 3.1
.

Tree
T^{\prime}
has at most
n/\ell
leaves.

Proof.

Let
v
be a leaf of
T^{\prime}
. Then clearly
|V(T_{v})|\geq\ell
, since otherwise
v
is an
\ell
-light vertex and should not belong to
T^{\prime}
. On the other hand, let
v,v^{\prime}
be any pair of leaves in
T^{\prime}
, then subtrees
T_{v},T_{v^{\prime}}
are vertex-disjoint. It follows that
T^{\prime}
has at most
n/\ell
leaves.
∎

For every vertex
v\in V(T^{\prime})
, we denote by
X_{\ell}(v)
the set of all maximal
\ell
-light child vertices of
v
in
T
, and denote by
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(v)
the set of all
\ell
-light subtrees rooted at vertices of
X_{\ell}(v)
. For each
v\in V(T^{\prime})
, we denote
n_{\ell}(v)=\sum_{u\in X_{\ell}(v)}|V(T_{u})|
, and for each path
P
in
T^{\prime}
, we denote
n_{\ell}(P)=\sum_{v\in V(P)}n_{\ell}(v)
.
We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

We can efficiently partition
T^{\prime}
into a set
{\mathcal{P}}
of
O(n/\ell)
vertex-disjoint paths, such that, for each path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, either
P
contains a single-vertex of
T
, or satisfies that
n_{\ell}(P)\leq 11\ell
.

Proof.

We say that a vertex
v
of
T^{\prime}
is
giant
iff
n_{\ell}(v)\geq 10\ell
. Clearly, the number of giant vertices in
T^{\prime}
is at most
n/(10\ell)
.
For each vertex
v\in T^{\prime}
that is either a special vertex or a giant vertex
v
, we add a path into
{\mathcal{P}}
that contains the single vertex
v
.
We then delete from
T^{\prime}
all special vertices and all giant vertices. It is easy to see that the remaining subgraph of
T^{\prime}
is the union of at most
O(n/\ell)
vertex-disjoint subpaths of
T^{\prime}
, that we denote by
{\mathcal{Q}}
. We further partition each path of
{\mathcal{Q}}
as follows. Let
Q=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})
be a path of
{\mathcal{Q}}
, we compute a sequence of indices
i_{0}=0<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{k}<r
, such that, for each
1\leq j\leq k
,
\ell\leq\sum_{i_{j-1}+1\leq t\leq i_{j}}n_{\ell}(v_{t})\leq 11\ell
, so
k\leq n_{\ell}(Q)/\ell
. It is easy to see that this can be done since
Q
does not contain giant vertices. We then define, for each
1\leq j\leq k
, path
P_{j}=(v_{i_{j-1}+1},\ldots,v_{i_{j}})
, and we define
P_{k+1}=(v_{i_{k}+1},\ldots,v_{r})
. Clearly, paths
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k+1}
partition
Q
, and we add them into set
{\mathcal{P}}
. Altogether, we have added a total of

O\bigg(\frac{n}{\ell}\bigg)+\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}}\bigg(\frac{n_{\ell}(Q)}{\ell}+1\bigg)\leq O\bigg(\frac{n}{\ell}\bigg)+|{\mathcal{Q}}|+\bigg(\frac{\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}}n_{\ell}(Q)}{\ell}\bigg)=O\bigg(\frac{n}{\ell}\bigg)

paths into
{\mathcal{P}}
, and each path
P
of
{\mathcal{P}}
is either a single-vertex path, or satisfies that
n_{\ell}(P)\leq 11\ell
.
∎

Segments of
T
.

We apply the algorithm from
Lemma

3.1
to tree
T
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the resulting set of vertex-disjoint paths of
T
.
For each path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, we denote by
T_{P}
the subtree of
T
that contains (i) all vertices of
P
; and (ii) for each vertex
v\in P
, the set of all
\ell
-light subtrees rooted at vertices of
X_{\ell}(v)
. In other words,
T_{P}
is the subgraph of
T
induced by all vertices of
V(P)\cup(\bigcup_{v\in P}V({\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(v)))
.
We call each subtree
T_{P}
a
segment
of tree
T
. For each vertex
v\in T^{\prime}
that is either a special vertex or a giant vertex, we also call each
\ell
-light subtree in
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(v)
a
segment
of
T
. Therefore, if we denote by
V^{\prime}
the set of all special and giant vertices in
T^{\prime}
, then the set of segments of
T
is
{\mathcal{S}}=\left\{T_{P}\mid P\in{\mathcal{P}}\right\}\cup\big(\bigcup_{v\in V^{\prime}}{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(v)\big)
. Clearly, the segments are edge-disjoint subgraphs of
T
, and each segment contains
O(\ell)
vertices.

Extension of
T^{\prime}
.

Let
v
be a vertex of
T^{\prime}
. Recall that
X_{\ell}(v)
is the set of all
\ell
-light child vertices of
v
, and
{\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(v)
is the set of
\ell
-light subtrees rooted at vertices of
X_{\ell}(v)
. We say that a path
Q
in
T
is
v
-nice
, iff one endpoint of
Q
is
v
and the other endpoint of
Q
is a vertex of
V({\mathcal{T}}_{\ell}(v))
.
In other words,
Q
is
v
-nice iff it is a nice path of
T
with
v
as its close-to-
r
endpoint.
We say that a subgraph (not necessarily connected)
F
of
T\setminus T^{\prime}
is a
k
-
extension
of
T^{\prime}
, iff
F=\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq k}F_{i}
, where each
F_{i}
is a
v
-nice path for some
v\in V(T^{\prime})
.
We say that
F
is a maximum
k
-extension of
T^{\prime}
, iff
F
is the
k
-extension of
T^{\prime}
with maximum total weight
w(F)
.
We denote by
w^{\sf ext}_{k}(T^{\prime})
the weight of a maximum
k
-extension.
The following observation is immediate.

Observation 3.2
.

Given any integer
k
, we can efficiently compute
w^{\sf ext}_{k}(T^{\prime})
and a
k
-extension
F
of
T^{\prime}
that achieves the weight
w^{\sf ext}_{k}(T^{\prime})
.

4
The Main Algorithm and its Analysis

In this section we provide the main algorithm in this part and then complete the proof of
Theorem

8
.
At a high level, our algorithm computes the cover advantage of the the top part and the bottom part of the MST separately. On the one hand, the cover advantage of the bottom parts (light subtrees) can be estimated efficiently in an exhaustive way. On the other hand, the top part is a subtree that contains few special vertices, so its special cover advantage can be estimated efficiently. Finally, for the cover advantage achieved by edges with an endpoint between different light subtrees, we utilize the first subroutine to estimate it. We then carefully combine the estimates to compute the final output.

Parameters:
\ell=\sqrt{n},\epsilon=2^{-100}/c_{0}
, where
c_{0}
is the constant in
Lemma

2.1
.

1.

We first apply the algorithm from
Lemma

2.2
to the subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
that is defined in
Section

3
, and compute the value of
w(T^{\prime})
. If
\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})\geq(\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then we return
X=(2-\varepsilon/20)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as the estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
.
If
\mathsf{adv}^{*}(T^{\prime})\leq(\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
and
w(T^{\prime})\geq(1/2+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then we return
X=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as the estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
. Otherwise, go to
2
.

2.

We compute a maximum weight independent set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
800
-trees in
T\setminus T^{\prime}
(note that this only uses edge weights of
T
and does not require performing additinal queries). If
w({\mathcal{F}})\geq(1/2+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then we apply the algorithm from
Corollary

2.1
to
{\mathcal{F}}
, and return the output of
Corollary

2.1
as an estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
.
If
\varepsilon\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}<w({\mathcal{F}})<(1/2+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, we then apply the algorithm from
Lemma

2.1
to
{\mathcal{F}}
.
If it reports that
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
(see the definition of
w^{\prime}
in
Lemma

2.1
), then we return
X=\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{5}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as the estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
.
Otherwise, go to
3
.
If
w({\mathcal{F}})\leq\varepsilon\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then go to
3
.

3.

We apply the algorithm from
Lemma

2.7
to the set
{\mathcal{S}}
of segments of
T
defined in
Section

3
, and parameter
\varepsilon/1000
. If the algorithm reports that
\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mathsf{adv}(S)\geq(\varepsilon/1000)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then we return
X=(2-\varepsilon/2000)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as the estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
. Otherwise, go to
4
.

4.

We apply the algorithm from
3.2
to compute a maximum
k
-extension
F
of
T^{\prime}
, and its weight
w^{\sf ext}_{k}(T^{\prime})
. Denote
T^{\prime\prime}=T^{\prime}\cup F
, and then we apply the algorithm from
Lemma

2.2
to tree
T^{\prime\prime}
.
If
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime\prime})\geq(\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then we return
X=(2-\varepsilon/20)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as the estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
.
If
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime\prime})<(\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, and
w(T^{\prime\prime})>(1/2+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then we return
X=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as the estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
. Otherwise, go to
5
.

5.

Return
X=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
as the estimate of
\mathsf{TSP}
.

We first count the total number of queries performed by the algorithm. First, in Step
1
we invoke the algorithm from
Lemma

2.2
, which performs
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries, since the number of special vertices in
T^{\prime}
is at most
O(n/\ell)=O(\sqrt{n})
.
Second, in Step
2
we invoke the algorithm from
Corollary

2.1
, which performs
\tilde{O}(n)
queries.
Third, in Step
3
we invoke the algorithm from
Lemma

2.7
, which performs in total
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries, since each segment contains
O(\ell)=O(\sqrt{n})
vertices.
Fourth, in Step
4
we invoke the algorithm from
Lemma

2.2
, which performs
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries, since the number of special vertices in
T^{\prime\prime}
is at most
O(n/\ell+k)=O(\sqrt{n})
.
Altogether, the algorithm performs in total
\tilde{O}(n^{1.5})
queries.

We now show that the algorithm indeed returns a
(2-\Omega(\varepsilon^{5}/\log(1/\varepsilon)))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.

Case 1.
Assume that, in Step
1
, the algorithm from
Lemma

2.2
returns that
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})\geq(\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. From
Lemma

2.3
, we get that
\mathsf{TSP}\leq 2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-(\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}/2=(2-\varepsilon/20)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, so our estimate
X=(2-\varepsilon/20)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is an
(2-\Omega(\varepsilon))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.

Case 2.
Assume that, in Step
1
, the algorithm from
Lemma

2.2
returns that
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})\leq(\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, and
w(T^{\prime})\geq(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. From
Lemma

2.6
, we get that
\mathsf{TSP}\geq 2\cdot w(T^{\prime})-2\cdot\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})\geq(1+\frac{9\varepsilon}{5})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, so our estimate
X=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is an
(2-\Omega(\varepsilon))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.

Case 3.
Assume that, in Step
2
, the independent set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
800
-trees satisfies that
w({\mathcal{F}})>(1/2+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. Then from
Corollary

2.1
, the output is an
(2-\Omega(\varepsilon^{4}/\log(1/\varepsilon)))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
. Assume that
\varepsilon\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}<w({\mathcal{F}})<(1/2+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
and
\mathsf{MWC}(w^{\prime})\leq\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}(w^{\prime})
, then from similar arguments in
Section

2.2
, it is easy to show that the estimate
X=\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{5}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is a
\big(2-\frac{\varepsilon^{5}}{2\log(1/\varepsilon)}\big)
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.

Case 4.
Assume that, in Step
3
, the algorithm from
Lemma

2.7
reports that
\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mathsf{adv}(S)\geq(\varepsilon/1000)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. From
Lemma

2.3
, we get that
\mathsf{TSP}\leq(2-\varepsilon/2000)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, so our estimate
X=(2-\varepsilon/2000)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\varepsilon))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.

Case 5.
Assume that we return an estimate in Step
4
. Similar to Case 1 and Case 2, it is easy to show that our estimate is a
(2-\Omega(\varepsilon))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.

We assume from now on that Cases
1
to
5
do not happen.
In other words,

P1.

\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})<(\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
;

P2.

the maximum weight of an independent set
{\mathcal{F}}
of
800
-trees in
T\setminus T^{\prime}
is at most
(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
;

P3.

w(T^{\prime})<(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
;

P4.

\mathsf{adv}({\mathcal{S}}):=\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mathsf{adv}(S)\leq(\varepsilon/500)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
; and

P5.

w(T^{\prime})+\text{ max weight of an }(100\sqrt{n})\text{-extension of }T^{\prime}<(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

We will show that the above five properties implies that
\mathsf{TSP}\geq(1+\Omega(1))\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, which implies that our estimate
X=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in Step
5
is a
(2-\Omega(1))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.

We first prove the following lemma.

Claim 4.1
.

Let
\hat{T}
be a subtree of
T
and let
\hat{E}
be a set of edges that do not belong to
T
. For each edge
f\in E(\hat{T})
, we denote by
{\mathsf{cov}}_{\hat{E}}(f)
the number of edges in
\hat{E}
that covers
f
. Then for any integer
k\geq 2
and for any real number
0<\delta<1/k
,

w(\hat{E})\geq\bigg(1-(k-1)\delta\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\sum_{f\in E(\hat{T})}w(f)\cdot\min\left\{k,{\mathsf{cov}}_{\hat{E}}(f)\right\}\bigg)-\frac{\mathsf{adv}(\hat{T})}{2\delta}.

Proof.

Let
E^{\prime}
be a random subset of
\hat{E}
that includes every edge of
\hat{E}
independently with probability
2\delta
.
Consider an edge
f\in E(\hat{T})
. If
{\mathsf{cov}}_{\hat{E}}(f)=c
, then the probability that
f\in{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime})
is
1-(1-2\delta)^{c}\geq 2\delta c-2\delta^{2}c(c-1)
, and if
c\geq k
, then
\Pr[f\in{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime})]\geq 2\delta k-2\delta^{2}k(k-1)
.
Therefore, from linearity of expectation, we get that

\mathbb{E}[w(E^{\prime})]=2\delta\cdot w(\hat{E})
and

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime},\hat{T}))]\geq&\text{ }\sum_{f\in E(\hat{T})}w(f)\cdot\bigg(2\delta\cdot\min\left\{k,{\mathsf{cov}}_{\hat{E}}(f)\right\}\cdot\bigg(1-\delta\cdot\big(\min\left\{k,{\mathsf{cov}}_{\hat{E}}(f)\right\}-1\big)\bigg)\bigg)\\
\geq&\text{ }\sum_{f\in E(\hat{T})}w(f)\cdot\bigg(2\delta\cdot\min\left\{k,{\mathsf{cov}}_{\hat{E}}(f)\right\}\cdot(1-\delta(k-1))\bigg)\\
=&\text{ }2\delta\cdot\big(1-\delta(k-1)\big)\cdot\bigg(\sum_{f\in E(\hat{T})}w(f)\cdot\min\left\{k,{\mathsf{cov}}_{\hat{E}}(f)\right\}\bigg).\end{split}

From the definition of
\mathsf{adv}(\hat{T})
,

\mathsf{adv}(\hat{T})\geq\mathbb{E}[w(E^{\prime})]-\mathbb{E}[w({\mathsf{cov}}(E^{\prime},\hat{T}))]
. Now
4.1
follows from the above inequalities.
∎

Let
\pi
be an optimal TSP-tour that visits all vertices of
V
, so
\mathsf{TSP}=w(\pi)
.
We construct a set
E^{\prime}
of edges as follows. We start with the set
E(\pi)
of the edges traversed in tour
\pi
. Then for each edge
e\in E(\pi)
such that either both endpoints of
e
belong to
V(T^{\prime})
or both endpoints of
e
belong to the same segment of
{\mathcal{S}}
, we replace
e
with edges in
E(P^{T}_{e})
, the tree path in
T
connecting the endpoints of
e
. Denote by
E^{\prime}_{\pi}
the resulting set after this step. Note that
E^{\prime}_{\pi}
may contain many copies of the same edge.
Finally, for each edge
e
that has more than
2
copies contained in
E^{\prime}_{\pi}
, if
E^{\prime}_{\pi}
contains an odd number of copies of
e
, then we delete all but one copies from
E^{\prime}_{\pi}
; if
E^{\prime}_{\pi}
contains an even number of copies of
e
, then we delete all but two copies from
E^{\prime}_{\pi}
. Denote by
E^{\prime}
the resulting set of edges, so each edge has at most
2
copies contained in
E^{\prime}
.
It is easy to verify that the graph induced by edges of
E^{\prime}
(with multiplicity) is connected and Eulerian.
Additionally, we prove the following observations.

Observation 4.1
.

w(E^{\prime})-w(E_{\pi})\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be a segment in
{\mathcal{S}}
. We denote by
E_{\pi}(S)
the subset of
E_{\pi}
that contains all edges with both endpoints in
S
.
We apply
4.1
with
\delta=\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5
and
k=2
(so
\delta<1/k
) to set
E_{\pi}(S)
of edges and segment
S
, and get

w(E_{\pi}(S))\geq\bigg(1-\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{5}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\sum_{f\in E(S)}w(f)\cdot\min\left\{2,{\mathsf{cov}}_{E_{\pi}(S)}(f)\right\}\bigg)-\frac{\mathsf{adv}(S)}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5}.

On the other hand, we denote by
E^{\prime}(S)
the subset of the resulting set
E^{\prime}
that contains all edges with both endpoints in
S
. Then from the construction of
E^{\prime}
, it is easy to see that, for each edge
f\in E(S)
, the number of its copies contained in
E^{\prime}(S)
is at most
\min\left\{2,{\mathsf{cov}}_{E_{\pi}(S)}(f)\right\}
. Therefore,

w(E_{\pi}(S))\geq\bigg(1-\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{5}\bigg)\cdot w(E^{\prime}(S))-\frac{\mathsf{adv}(S)}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5}.

Similarly, for all other segments in
{\mathcal{S}}
and for tree
T^{\prime}
we can get similar inequalities. Altogether, if we denote by
E^{*}_{\pi}
the subset of
E_{\pi}
that contains all edges such that neither both endpoints belong to some segment in
{\mathcal{S}}
nor both endpoints belong to
V(T^{\prime})
, then clearly
E^{\prime}=E^{*}_{\pi}\cup\big(\bigcup_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}E^{\prime}(S)\big)
, and so

\begin{split}w(E_{\pi})=&\text{ }w(E^{*}_{\pi})+w(E_{\pi}(T^{\prime}))+\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}w(E_{\pi}(S))\\
\geq&\text{ }w(E^{*}_{\pi})+\bigg(1-\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{5}\bigg)\cdot w(E^{\prime}(T^{\prime}))-\frac{\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5}+\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}\bigg(\bigg(1-\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{5}\bigg)\cdot w(E^{\prime}(S))-\frac{\mathsf{adv}(S)}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5}\bigg)\\
=&\text{ }w(E^{\prime})-\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{5}\cdot\bigg(w(E^{\prime}(T^{\prime}))+\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}w(E^{\prime}(S))\bigg)-\frac{\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})+\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mathsf{adv}(S)}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5}\\
\geq&\text{ }w(E^{\prime})-\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{5}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\frac{(\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+(\varepsilon/500)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5}>w(E^{\prime})-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}},\end{split}

where the last but one inequality utilizes Properties
P1
and
P4
.
∎

Observation 4.2
.

There is a subset
E^{*}
of
E^{\prime}
, such that (i)
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*},T)
; and (ii)
E^{*}
contains
O(n/\ell)
edges with at least one endpoint in
V(T\setminus T^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Recall that in
Lemma

3.1
we partitioned tree
T^{\prime}
into a set
{\mathcal{P}}
of vertex-disjoint paths, such that, for each
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
, either
{\mathcal{P}}
contains a single vertex (that is either a special vertex or a giant vertex) of
T^{\prime}
, or contains no special vertices of
T^{\prime}
. We construct the set
E^{*}
of edges by processing paths of
{\mathcal{P}}
one-by-one, as follows.

Initially, we set
E^{*}=\emptyset
.
Consider a path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
. Assume first that path
P
consists of a single vertex
v
of
T^{\prime}
. Then for each edge
f
of
T^{\prime}
that is incident to
v
, we arbitrarily pick an edge
e\in E^{\prime}
such that
f\in{\mathsf{cov}}(e,T^{\prime})
, and add
e
to
E^{*}
. Clearly, such an edge exists since the graph induced by edges of
E^{\prime}
is connected and Eulerian on
V
.
Assume now that path
P
contains no special vertices of
T^{\prime}
. We denote by
f_{1}(P),f_{2}(P)
the two edges of
T^{\prime}
that are incident to the endpoints of
P
. We then let
e_{1}(P)
be an edge in
E^{\prime}
that, among all edges
e
in
E^{\prime}
that covers
f_{1}(P)
, maximizes
|{\mathsf{cov}}(e,T^{\prime})\cap E(P)|
, and we define edge
e_{2}(P)
similarly for
f_{2}(P)
.
If
E(P)\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(\left\{e_{1}(P),e_{2}(P)\right\},T^{\prime})
, then we add edges
e_{1}(P),e_{2}(P)
to set
E^{*}
.
If
E(P)\not\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(\left\{e_{1}(P),e_{2}(P)\right\},T^{\prime})
, then let
P^{\prime}
be the subpath of
P
that is not covered by either
e_{1}(P)
or
e_{2}(P)
. From the construction of
E^{\prime}
, it is easy to verify that all edges of
P^{\prime}
belong to
E^{\prime}
(since otherwise they are covered by an non-tree-edge with both endpoints lying in
T_{P}
, the segment corresponding to path
P
, a contradiction). We then add edges
e_{1}(P),e_{2}(P)
and all edges of
E(P^{\prime})
to set
E^{*}
. This completes the construction of set
E^{*}
.

It is easy to verify that
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq E^{*}
from the construction. We now show that the number of edges in
E^{*}
with an endpoint in
V(T\setminus T^{\prime})
is
O(n/\ell)
. In fact, for a path
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
that does not contain a special vertex of
T^{\prime}
, we added to
E^{*}
at most two edges with an endpoint in
V(T\setminus T^{\prime})
; and for all single-special-vertex paths of
{\mathcal{P}}
, since
T^{\prime}
contains
O(n/\ell)
leaves, we added a total of
O(n/\ell)
edges to
E^{*}
. Therefore, the number of edges in
E^{*}
with an endpoint in
V(T\setminus T^{\prime})
is
O(n/\ell)
. This completes the proof of
4.2
.
∎

Let
E^{\prime\prime}
be the subset of
E^{\prime}
that contains all edges
e
such that (i) both endpoints of
e
belong to the same segment of
{\mathcal{S}}
; and (ii) there is no edge
e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime},e^{\prime}\neq e
, such that
e\in{\mathsf{cov}}(e^{\prime},T)
. In other words, edges of
E^{\prime\prime}
are the bridges in the graph induced by edges of
E^{\prime}
(ignoring multiplicities), and it is easy to verify that each edge has two copies contained in
E^{\prime}
. We prove the following simple observation.

Observation 4.3
.

w(E^{\prime})\geq{\mathsf{MST}}+w(E^{\prime\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
T_{1},\ldots,T_{r}
be the connected components of graph
T\setminus E^{\prime\prime}
. From the definition of set
E^{\prime\prime}
, it is easy to see that each edge of
E^{\prime}\setminus E^{\prime\prime}
has both endpoints belong to the same connected component
T_{i}
, and moreover, if we denote by
E^{\prime}_{i}
the subset of
E^{\prime}
that contains all edges of
E^{\prime}\setminus E^{\prime\prime}
with both endpoints in
T_{i}
, then the graph induced by edges of
E^{\prime}_{i}
(with multiplicity) is connected and Eulerian on
V(T_{i})
. Therefore,
w(E^{\prime}_{i})\geq w(T_{i})
since
T_{i}
is the MST on
V(T_{i})
. Altogether,

w(E^{\prime})\geq 2\cdot w(E^{\prime\prime})+\sum_{1\leq i\leq r}w(E^{\prime}_{i})\geq 2\cdot w(E^{\prime\prime})+\sum_{1\leq i\leq r}w(T_{i})\geq w(E^{\prime\prime})+{\mathsf{MST}}.

∎

From
4.3
, if
w(E^{\prime\prime})>2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, then
w(E^{\prime})>(1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
and then from
4.1
,
w(\pi)>(1+2\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\geq(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. Therefore, our estimation
X=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(\sqrt{\varepsilon}))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.
We assume from now on that
w(E^{\prime\prime})\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

We use the following lemma, whose proof is deferred to
Section

4.1
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume
w(E^{\prime\prime})\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.
Let
T^{\prime\prime}
be a subtree of
T
such that
T^{\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime\prime}
and
w(T^{\prime\prime}\setminus T^{\prime})\leq{\mathsf{MST}}/20
.
Let
\hat{E}_{T^{\prime\prime}}
be the subset of
E^{\prime}
that contains all edges with at least one endpoint in
V(T\setminus T^{\prime\prime})
. Then

w(\hat{E}_{T^{\prime\prime}})\geq\bigg(1.9-5\cdot\frac{w(T^{\prime\prime}\setminus T^{\prime})}{{\mathsf{MST}}}\bigg)\cdot w(T\setminus T^{\prime\prime})-10\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}.

Assume that
w(T^{\prime})\leq 0.47\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, so
w(T\setminus T^{\prime})\geq 0.53\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.
Applying
Lemma

4.1
to subtree
T^{\prime\prime}=T^{\prime}
(so
w(T^{\prime\prime}\setminus T^{\prime})=0
and
w(T\setminus T^{\prime\prime})\geq 0.53\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
),
we get that
w(E^{\prime})>0.53\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\cdot 1.9-10\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, and then from
4.1
,
w(\pi)>(1.007-11\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
,
and so our estimation
X=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(1))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.
We assume from now on that
w(T^{\prime})>0.47\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

Let
E^{*}
be the set of edges in
4.2
.
Since
E(T^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*},T)
, from the definition of
\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})
and Property
P1
, we get that
w(E^{*})\geq w(T^{\prime})-\mathsf{adv}(T^{\prime})\geq(0.47-\varepsilon/10)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
. On the other hand, let
F
be the subgraph of
T
induced by edges of
{\mathsf{cov}}(E^{*},T)
, so
T^{\prime}\subseteq F
. Since
E^{*}
only contains
O(n/\ell)
edges with an endpoint in
V(T\setminus T^{\prime})
, it is easy to verify that
F\setminus T^{\prime}
is an
O(n/\ell)
-extension of
T^{\prime}
. From Property
P3
,
w(F)\leq(1/2+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, so
w(F\setminus T^{\prime})\leq(0.03+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

Last, applying
Lemma

4.1
to subtree
F
(note that
w(F\setminus T^{\prime})\leq{\mathsf{MST}}/20
), and noticing that
\hat{E}_{F}\cap E^{*}=\emptyset
(since from the definition of
F
, every edge of
E^{*}
has both endpoints in
F
), we get that

\begin{split}w(E^{\prime})\geq w(E^{*})+w(\hat{E}_{F})\geq&\text{ }\bigg(0.47-\frac{\varepsilon}{10}\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+(1.9-0.2)\cdot(w(T)-w(F))-10\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\\
\geq&\text{ }\bigg(0.47-\frac{\varepsilon}{10}\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+1.7\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\bigg)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-10\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\\
\geq&\text{ }1.32\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-12\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}.\end{split}

Then from
4.1
,
w(\pi)\geq(1.32-13\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}>1.3\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, so our estimation
X=2\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
in this case is a
(2-\Omega(1))
-approximation of
\mathsf{TSP}
.
This completes the proof of
Theorem

8
.

4.1
Proof of Lemma
4.1

Recall that we are given a subtree
T^{\prime\prime}
of
T
such that
T^{\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime\prime}
.
For convenience, we denote
\hat{E}=\hat{E}_{T^{\prime\prime}}
to be the subset of
E^{\prime}
that contains all edges with at least one endpoint in
V(T\setminus T^{\prime\prime})
.
Throughout the subsection, we set constant
c=400
.

Denote
F=T\setminus T^{\prime\prime}
. Clearly,
F
is a subgraph of the graph obtained by taking the union of all segments, namely
F\subseteq\bigcup_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}S
.
We partition set
\hat{E}
into four subsets as follows: set
\hat{E}_{1}
contains all edges of
\hat{E}
with exactly one endpoint in
F
; and set
\hat{E}_{2}
contains all edges of
\hat{E}
with its endpoints lying in different segments; set
\hat{E}_{3}
contains all edges
e
of
\hat{E}
such that both endpoints of
e
belong to the same segment and
e
as a tree edge is covered by some edge of
\hat{E}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}
; and set
\hat{E}_{4}
contains all edges
e
of
\hat{E}
such that both endpoints of
e
belong to the same segment and
e
as a tree edge is not covered by any edge of
\hat{E}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}
. Clearly,
\hat{E}=\hat{E}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}\cup\hat{E}_{3}\cup\hat{E}_{4}
, and from the construction of
\hat{E}
and
E^{\prime}
, sets
\hat{E}_{3}
and
\hat{E}_{4}
are subsets of
E(F)
. Moreover,
\hat{E}_{4}\subseteq E^{\prime\prime}
, so
w(\hat{E}_{4})\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
, and from the previous discussion on set
E^{\prime\prime}
, each edge of
\hat{E}_{4}
has at least two copies contained in
\hat{E}_{4}
. In other words, if we denote by
F_{4}
the set of edges without multiplicity in
\hat{E}_{4}
, then
w(\hat{E}_{4})\geq 2\cdot w(F_{4})
.

Let
E^{*}
be the subset of
E(F)
that contains all edges that are covered by either at least
c
edges of
\hat{E}_{1}
or at least
c
edges of
\hat{E}_{2}
.
We distinguish between the following two cases, depending on whether or not the total weight of edges in
E^{*}
is large enough.

Case 1.
w(E^{*})>w(F\setminus F_{4})\cdot(2/c)

We construct a new set
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}
of edges from set
\hat{E}_{1}
, similarly to the construction of set
E^{\prime}
from set
E(\pi)
, as follows.
We start with the set
\hat{E}_{1}
. For each edge
e\in\hat{E}_{1}
such that both endpoints of
e
belong to the same segment, we replace
e
with edges in
{\mathsf{cov}}(e,F)
, the set of edges in
F
that are covered by
e
.
Finally, for each edge
e
that has more than
c
copies contained in the current edge set, if the current set contains an odd number of copies of
e
, then we delete from it all but one copies of
e
; if the current set contains an even number of copies of
e
, then we delete from it all but two copies of
e
. Denote by
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}
the resulting set of edges, so each edge has at most
c
copies contained in
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}
.
From
4.1
(by setting
\delta=\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5c
and
k=c
), and observing that, for each edge
f\in E(F)
, the number of its copies contained in
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}
is at most
\min\left\{c,{\mathsf{cov}}_{\hat{E}_{1}}(f)\right\}
,
we get that

\begin{split}w(\hat{E}_{1})\geq&\text{ }\bigg(1-(c-1)\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{5c}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\sum_{f\in E(F)}w(f)\cdot\min\left\{c,{\mathsf{cov}}_{\hat{E}_{1}}(f)\right\}\bigg)-\frac{\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mathsf{adv}(S)}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5c}\\
\geq&\text{ }\bigg(1-\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{5}\bigg)\cdot w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1})-\frac{(\varepsilon/500)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}}{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}/5c}\geq w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1})-3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}},\end{split}

where the second inequality utilizes Property
P4
.

We then construct a new set
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{2}
from
\hat{E}_{2}
in the same way as the construction of
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}
of edges from
\hat{E}_{1}
. From similar arguments, and noticing that each edge of
\hat{E}_{2}
covers the edges of at most two connected component of
F
, we get that

2\cdot w(\hat{E}_{2})\geq w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{2})-3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.

Note that, from the construction of new sets
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1},\hat{E}^{\prime}_{2}
, it is easy to verify that every edge in
E^{*}
has at least
(c-1)
of its copies contained in set
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\hat{E}^{\prime}_{2}
. Also, every edge of
F\setminus F_{4}
has at least two of its copies contained in
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\hat{E}^{\prime}_{2}\cup\hat{E}_{3}
. Therefore,

\begin{split}w(\hat{E})=&\text{ }w(\hat{E}_{1})+w(\hat{E}_{2})+w(\hat{E}_{3})+w(\hat{E}_{4})\\
\geq&\text{ }w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1})-3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+\frac{w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{2})-3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}}{2}+w(\hat{E}_{3})+2\cdot w(F_{4})\\
\geq&\text{ }\frac{w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\hat{E}^{\prime}_{2}\cup\hat{E}_{3})}{2}-5\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+2\cdot w(F_{4})\\
\geq&\text{ }\bigg(\frac{c-1}{2}\bigg)\cdot w(E^{*})+w(F\setminus F_{4})-5\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+2\cdot w(F_{4})\\
\geq&\text{ }\bigg(\frac{c-1}{2}\bigg)\cdot w(F\setminus F_{4})\cdot\frac{2}{c}+w(F\setminus F_{4})-5\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+2\cdot w(F_{4})\\
\geq&\text{ }\bigg(\frac{2c-1}{c}\bigg)\cdot w(F)-5\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}},\end{split}

which implies that the required inequality of
Lemma

4.1
holds.

Case 2.
w(E^{*})\leq w(F\setminus F_{4})\cdot(2/c)

Define forest
F^{\prime}=F\setminus(F_{4}\cup E^{*})
and denote
\eta=w(F)/{\mathsf{MST}}
.
Since
\eta<0.05
, and
w(T^{\prime})\leq(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
,
w(F^{\prime})\geq 0.44\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
.
We claim that

w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1})+w(\hat{E}_{2})+w(\hat{E}_{3})\geq(1.91-5\eta)\cdot w(F^{\prime}).

(2)

Note that, if the inequality
2
is true, then since we have assumed that
w(E^{*})\leq w(F\setminus F_{4})\cdot(2/c)
,

\begin{split}w(\hat{E})=&\text{ }w(\hat{E}_{1})+w(\hat{E}_{2})+w(\hat{E}_{3})+w(\hat{E}_{4})\\
\geq&\text{ }w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1})-3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}+w(\hat{E}_{2})+w(\hat{E}_{3})+2\cdot w(F_{4})\\
\geq&\text{ }(1.91-5\eta)\cdot w(F^{\prime})+2\cdot w(F_{4})-3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\\
\geq&\text{ }(1.9-5\eta)\cdot w(F\setminus F_{4})+2\cdot w(F_{4})-3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\\
\geq&\text{ }(1.9-5\eta)\cdot w(F)-3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}},\end{split}

which implies that the required inequality of
Lemma

4.1
holds.

The remainder of this subsection is dedicated to the proof of inequality
2
.
We first prove the following observation.

Observation 4.4
.

F^{\prime}=F\setminus(F_{4}\cup E^{*})
is an independent set of
2c
-trees.

Proof.

On the one hand, note that each edge of
F\setminus E^{*}
is covered at most
2c
times by edges of
\hat{E}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}
. Also note that, from the definition of sets
\hat{E}_{1}
and
\hat{E}_{2}
, if an edge of
F\setminus E^{*}
is covered by some edge of
\hat{E}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}
, then all its ancestors are also covered by the same edge. Therefore,
F\setminus E^{*}
is an independent set of
2c
-trees. On the other hand, for the same reasons, it is easy to verify that edges of
F_{4}
are at the bottom of each connected component of
F
. Therefore,
F^{\prime}=(F\setminus E^{*})\setminus F_{4}
is also an independent set of
2c
-trees.
∎

Let
T_{F^{\prime}}
be the skeleton of
F^{\prime}
. From the construction of sets
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1},\hat{E}_{2}
, it is easy to verify that the graph induced by edges of
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}\cup\hat{E}_{3}
is connected and Eulerian on
V(T_{F^{\prime}})
, so the minimum special walk cost on
T_{F^{\prime}}
is at most
w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}\cup\hat{E}_{3})
.

Since
w(\hat{E}_{4})\leq 2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
,

\begin{split}w(F^{\prime})\geq&\text{ }\bigg(1-\frac{2}{c}\bigg)\cdot w(F\setminus F_{4})\geq\bigg(1-\frac{2}{c}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(w(T\setminus T^{\prime})-\eta\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}-2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\bigg)\\
\geq&\text{ }w(T\setminus T^{\prime})-(\eta+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}+2/c)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}.\end{split}

Let
F^{*}
be the
(2c)
-tree computed in Step
2
of the algorithm. From the maximality of
F^{*}
,
w(F^{*})\geq w(F^{\prime})
. Let
T_{F^{*}}
be the skeleton of
F^{*}
. Consider the special walk on
T_{F^{*}}
constructed as follows. We start with the tour induced by all edges of
\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}\cup\hat{E}_{3}\cup\hat{E}_{4}
, and delete all vertices that do not belong to
F^{*}
.
Assume for contradiction that
w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}\cup\hat{E}_{3})<(1.91-5\eta)\cdot w(F^{\prime})
. Then the cost of such a walk is at most

\begin{split}w(\hat{E}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}\cup\hat{E}_{3}\cup\hat{E}_{4})+2\cdot(\eta+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}+4/c)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}<&\text{ }(1.91-5\eta)\cdot w(F^{\prime})+(2\eta+4\sqrt{\varepsilon}+4/c)\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\\
<&\text{ }1.91\cdot w(F^{\prime})+0.02\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}\\
<&\text{ }1.99\cdot w(F^{\prime})\\
\leq&\text{ }1.99\cdot w(F^{*}),\end{split}

where the last but one step uses the property that
w(F^{\prime})>0.44\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
(and so
5\eta\cdot w(F^{\prime})>2\eta\cdot{\mathsf{MST}}
).
This is a contradiction to the fact that the minimum special walk cost on
F^{*}
is at least
(2-10^{3}\cdot c_{0}\cdot\varepsilon)\cdot w(F^{*})
. Therefore, inequality
2
holds. This completes the proof of the correctness of the algorithm.

5
Future Directions

In this work, we studied the problems of MST and TSP cost estimation in the streaming
and query settings. For TSP cost estimation, we introduced and utilized a novel notion called
cover advantage
that may prove useful for solving this problem in other computational models also.
In the streaming setting, an interesting open problem is to obtain a one-pass
o(n^{2})
-space
(2-\varepsilon)
-approximate estimation of TSP cost in the metric stream.
In the query model, we believe a major open problem is to obtain an
o(n^{2})
-query
(2-\varepsilon)
-approximate estimation of TSP-cost in general metrics.
